Thomason’s completion for K K -theory and cyclic homology of quotient stacks
Amalendu Krishna, Ritankar Nath

Abstract




2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 14F43, Secondary 19D55

Abstract.

We prove several completion theorems for equivariant
K
-theory
and cyclic homology of schemes with group action over a field. One of these
shows that for an algebraic space over a field acted upon by a linear algebraic
group, the derived completion of equivariant
K^{\prime}
-theory at the
augmentation ideal of the representation ring of the group coincides with the
ordinary
K^{\prime}
-theory of the bar construction associated to the group action.
This provides a solution to Thomason’s completion problem. For action with finite
stabilizers, we show that the equivariant
K
-theory and cyclic homology have
non-equivariant descriptions even without passing to their completions. As an
application, we describe all equivariant Hochschild and
other homology groups for such actions.

1.
Introduction

Equivariant
K
-theory of algebraic varieties equipped with actions of
linear algebraic groups was introduced by Thomason
[
87
]
.
Its significance is premised on two key observations: (1) it is an important
tool for describing the ordinary
K
-theory of varieties with group action (cf.

[
59
]
,
[
71
]
,
[
94
]
), and (2) it is often a key
ingredient in the study of representation theory of algebraic groups
(cf.
[
19
]
). A key advantage of equivariant
K
-theory
is that it does not depend on the underlying variety and the group but
only on the quotient stack resulting from the action. This provides a great deal of
flexibility in computing equivariant
K
-theory.

Despite the above advantages, equivariant
K
-theory suffers from the drawback
that there aren’t many cohomological tools to compute them.
In order to apply the tools (e.g., motivic cohomology) for computing ordinary

K
-theory, a fundamental problem in equivariant
K
-theory is to find out
how far it is from the ordinary
K
-theory and if there is a way to approximate it
by ordinary
K
-theory. A solution to this problem in topology
is via the celebrated Atiyah-Segal completion theorem
[
2
]
.
The latter says roughly that if a compact Lie group
G
acts on a compact Hausdorff
space
X
, then the (topological) equivariant
K
-theory
K^{G}(X)
of
X

becomes the ordinary (topological)
K
-theory
K(EG\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}X)
of the
homotopy orbit space
EG\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}X
after its completion with respect to
the augmentation ideal
I_{G}
of the representation ring
R(G)
.
The objective of this paper is the study of algebraic analogues of this theorem.
There are two approaches to this problem and we shall discuss
them separately.

1.1.
Equivariant
K
-theory via ordinary
K
-theory of bar construction

Let
k
be a field and
G
a linear algebraic group over
k
acting on a
smooth
k
-scheme
X
. Let
\pi\colon X\to[X/G]
be the stack quotient map for the

G
-action. Given this datum, Thomason (cf.
[
87
, § 1.3]
) constructed
a simplicial scheme

(1.1)

X_{G}^{\bullet}:=\left(\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{\underset{\rightarrow}{\rightrightarrows}}}G\times G\times X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{\underset{\rightarrow}{\to}}}G\times X\rightrightarrows X\right)

whose face maps are built by iterating the action and projection maps

G\times X\to X
and which is functorial in
G
and
X
.
This is known as the bar construction associated to
G
-action on
X
.
For the action of a compact Lie group
G
on a compact Hausdorff
space
X
, the geometric realization of
X^{\bullet}_{G}
provides a model for
the homotopy orbit space
EG\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}X
.

In his 1986 paper (cf.
[
86
, § 0]
),
Thomason asked if the pull-back by
the projection map
\widetilde{\pi}\colon X^{\bullet}_{G}\to[X/G]
(induced by
\pi
)
would produce a weak equivalence of spectra

(1.2)

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon K_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K(X^{\bullet}_{G}),

where
K(X^{\bullet}_{G})=\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}K(G^{n}\times X)
is the ordinary
K
-theory spectrum
of the simplicial scheme
X^{\bullet}_{G}
,

K_{G}(X)
is the equivariant
K
-theory spectrum (cf. §
3.1
) and

K_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}
is a homotopy theoretic completion of
K_{G}(X)

at the augmentation ideal
I_{G}
of
R(G)
.
Thomason predicted the existence of the spectrum
K_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}
but did not
construct it.

In the same paper, Thomason showed that if
k
is a separably
closed field (and some other fields), then a version of the above
problem has a positive answer if one replaces
K_{G}(X)
by
the Bott-inverted
K
-theory with finite coefficients

K_{G}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n})[\beta^{-1}]
(with
n
prime to
{\rm char}(k)
)
and the conjectured homotopy theoretic
I_{G}
-completion of the spectrum

K_{G}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n})[\beta^{-1}]

is replaced by algebraic
I_{G}
-completion of the
R(G)
-modules

\pi_{*}(K_{G}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n})[\beta^{-1}])
. It was shown later in

[
60
]
that using finite coefficients and Bott-inversion are both
necessary in Thomason’s theorem.
In this paper, we shall refer to the problem of showing (
1.2
) is a
weak equivalence as
Thomason’s completion problem
.

Thomason’s completion problem is a very powerful statement as a positive
answer to it would allow one to compute
the equivariant
K
-theory up to completion in terms of the
ordinary
K
-theory of the C̆ech nerve of the group action.
In particular, one would be able to apply the machinery of ordinary
motivic cohomology to compute equivariant
K
-theory just as one does for
ordinary
K
-theory.

One of the main results of this paper provides a solution to Thomason’s completion
problem.
In order to state it, let
K^{\prime}_{G}(X)
denote the
K
-theory spectrum of
the
G
-equivariant pseudo-coherent complexes on
X
. In
[
70
, Chap. 7.3]
,
Lurie introduced a simple procedure of derived completion of spectra
which implies in particular that
the derived
I_{G}
-completion of
K^{\prime}_{G}(X)
postulated by Thomason
indeed exists (as its homotopy groups are compatible with the classical
algebraic
I_{G}
-completions on the homotopy groups of
K^{\prime}_{G}(X)
). We let

K^{\prime}_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}
denote this spectrum level completion. Let
K^{\prime}(-)
denote the
ordinary
K^{\prime}
-theory functor and let
K^{\prime}(-,{\mathbb{Q}})
(resp.
K^{\prime}_{G}(-,{\mathbb{Q}})
) denote the
rationalized ordinary (resp. equivariant)
K^{\prime}
-theory functor. All algebraic spaces
will be separated and finite type over the given base field.

We prove the following.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
k
be a field and
G
a linear algebraic group over
k
acting on an algebraic
space
X
. Let
m\in k^{\times}
be an integer. We now have the following.

(1)

If
G
is special, then the pull-back map
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}

induces a weak equivalence of spectra

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G}).

(2)

If
G
is arbitrary, then the pull-back map
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}
induces a weak
equivalence of spectra

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G},{\mathbb{Q}}).

(3)

If
G
is arbitrary and
k
contains all roots of unity, then
the pull-back map
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}
induces a weak equivalence of spectra

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon\left(K^{\prime}_{G}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{m})[\beta^{-1}]\right)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)[\beta^{-1}].

Remark 1.2
.

(1)

The failure of Galois descent for algebraic
K
-theory of fields
shows that one should not expect Theorem
1.1
(2) to be true with integral
coefficients if
G
is non-special.

(2)

If
k
is separably closed, then
the homotopy groups of
K^{\prime}_{G}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{m})[\beta^{-1}]
are
finitely generated
R(G)
-modules, and in particular, commute with Lurie’s completion
(cf.
[
70
, Cor.7.3.6.6]
).
Using this observation, item (3) of the
theorem also follows from Thomason’s theorem (the main result of

[
86
]
) if
k
is separably closed.
Item (3) can therefore be interpreted as an extension of
Thomason’s theorem to other fields.

(3)

We also remark that one can replace
K^{\prime}
-theory by
K
-theory (of perfect complexes)
in the theorem if
X
is regular.

Recall that given a
G
-equivariant morphism
f\colon X\to Y
between smooth

k
-schemes with
G
-actions, it is a hard problem to determine
what kind of weak equivalence between
X
and
Y
would imply a weak equivalence
between their equivariant
K
-theory.
This makes the following application of Theorem
1.1
relevant.

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
k
be a field and
G
a linear algebraic group over
k
. Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a
G
-equivariant morphism between smooth
k
-schemes with
G
-actions.
Assume that
f
is an
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-weak equivalence (after ignoring the
G
-action) in the
sense of Morel-Voevodsky. We then have the following.

(1)

The map
f^{*}\colon K_{G}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to K_{G}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}
is a weak equivalence.

(2)

If
G
is special, the map
f^{*}\colon K_{G}(Y)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to K_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}
is a weak equivalence.

(3)

If
k
contains all roots of unity and
m\in k^{\times}
, the map

f^{*}\colon\left(K_{G}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{m})[\beta^{-1}]\right)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to\left(K_{G}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{m})[\beta^{-1}]\right)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}

is a weak equivalence.

1.2.
Equivariant
K
-theory via ordinary
K
-theory of Borel construction

The second approach to algebraic Atiyah-Segal theorem
is via the Borel construction. The Borel equivariant cohomology theory is a
classical concept in topology. Using their
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-homotopy theory of
schemes, Morel-Voevodsky
[
72
]
introduced Borel construction
for linear algebraic groups in algebraic geometry. Totaro
[
93
]

and Edidin-Graham
[
28
]
used this construction to define equivariant
motivic cohomology while it was used in
[
60
]
to
define Borel equivariant
K
-theory.

Let
k
be a field and
G
a linear algebraic group over
k
. A Borel construction
for a
G
-action on an algebraic space
X
is an ind-space

X_{G}:=\{X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}U_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
, where
\{(V_{n},U_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}

(known as an admissible gadget)
is an increasing (in terms of inclusion) sequence of pairs of
k
-schemes
such that
V_{i}
is a finite-dimensional rational representation of
G
and

U_{i}\subset V_{i}
is
G
-invariant open which is a
G
-torsor over a scheme.
These pairs have the
advantage that the quotients
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}U_{n}
always exist as
algebraic spaces.

By its definition, the Borel construction
X_{G}
depends
on the choice of the admissible gadget. But its
K^{\prime}
-theory

K^{\prime}(X_{G}):=\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}K^{\prime}(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}U_{n})
does not. We refer to
§
6
for more details.
The
G
-equivariant projection maps
p_{n}\colon X\times U_{n}\to X
define
a map of ind-stacks
\widetilde{p}\colon X_{G}\to[X/G]
and hence a map

\widetilde{p}^{*}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X)\to K^{\prime}(X_{G})
. The Borel construction approach to
algebraic Atiyah-Segal theorem asks if Lurie’s
I_{G}
-completion of

K^{\prime}_{G}(X)
could be described by the ordinary
K^{\prime}
-theory of
X_{G}
.
We answer this as follows.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
k
be a field and
G
a linear algebraic group over
k
acting on an algebraic
space
X
. Then the pull-back map
\widetilde{p}^{*}
induces a weak equivalence of spectra

\widetilde{p}^{*}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K^{\prime}(X_{G}).

Remark 1.5
.

Some remarks are in order.

(1)

We can replace
K^{\prime}
-theory by
K
-theory in Theorem
1.4
if
X
is
regular.

(2)

One may note that our description of the equivariant
K
-theory via
Borel construction
holds integrally for all groups in contrast to the description via the bar
construction. The reason for this is that Theorem
1.1
is much more
subtle than Theorem
1.4
because the admissible gadgets are easier
to deal with and one has flexibility while choosing them. This isn’t true
with the bar construction. Theorem
1.4
is in fact a key step in
the proof of more challenging Theorem
1.1
.

(3)

Using the
T
-filtrability of smooth projective schemes with torus action
and
[
70
, Cor.7.3.6.6]
,
the completion theorems of
[
60
]
can be recovered
from Theorem
1.4
.

(4)

When
k
is an infinite perfect field, a version of Theorem
1.4
was
proven by Carlsson-Joshua
[
15
]
. There are two main differences
between their result and ours. First, in the completion theorem of

[
15
]
, the authors use Carlsson’s derived completion

[
14
]
whose definition is more involved and
which is
a priori
different from Lurie’s completion.
In particular, it is unclear if it coincides with the
I_{G}
-completion on the
integral homotopy groups of the
K
-theory spectrum
(except when the underlying group is a split torus in which case the two
completions coincide).

Second, the authors of
[
15
]
fix an
ambient group (which is a product of general linear groups) and assume other
groups (including
G
) to be subgroups of this fixed
ambient group in the construction of Borel
K
-theory and in their main
theorems. As a result, their right hand side of
\widetilde{p}^{*}
depends on
the fixed choice of this ambient group. It is as such not functorial in

G
. In contrast, Theorem
1.4
does not rely on any such assumption.

1.3.
Completion of equivariant
KH
-theory

Our third result extends Theorems
1.1
and
1.4

to the equivariant homotopy
K
-theory under some restrictions.
Before we state it, recall that
a linear algebraic group
G
over a field
k
is called
nice

if it is an extension of a finite linearly reductive
group scheme over
k
by a group scheme over
k
of multiplicative type (cf.

[
50
, § 2]
). The category of nice group schemes includes
diagonalizable groups and finite constant groups whose orders are invertible
in
k
. For an algebraic space
X
over
k

with an action of a linear algebraic group
G
,
let
KH_{G}(X)
denote the non-connective equivariant homotopy
K
-theory spectrum
for
X
(cf.
[
63
, § 5]
).
We prove the following.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
k
be a field and
G
a linear algebraic group over
k
acting on a
k
-scheme

X
. Assume that either

({\rm A})

X
is a toric variety over
k
on which
G
acts as the
dense torus, or

({\rm B})

{\rm char}(k)=0
and
G
acts on
X
with
nice stabilizers.

Then the following hold.

(1)

The maps of spectra

(i)\ \widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon KH_{G}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to KH(X^{\bullet}_{G},{\mathbb{Q}})\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ (ii)\ \widetilde{p}^{*}\colon KH_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to KH(X_{G})

are weak equivalences.
Moreover,
(i)
holds integrally if
G
is special.

(2)

If
k
contains all roots of unity and
m\in k^{\times}
, then the maps of spectra

(i)\ \widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon(KH_{G}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)[\beta^{-1}])^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to KH(X^{\bullet}_{G},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)[\beta^{-1}]\ \ \mbox{and}

\ \ (ii)\ \widetilde{p}^{*}\colon(KH_{G}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)[\beta^{-1}])^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to KH(X_{G},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)[\beta^{-1}]

are weak equivalences.

Since the Quillen-Thomason
K
-theory agrees with its
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-invariant version with
finite coefficients prime to the characteristic, we get the following completion
theorem for the equivariant
K
-theory (of perfect complexes).

Corollary 1.7
.

Assume that the hypotheses of Theorem
1.6
are satisfied and
m\in k^{\times}
.
Then the following hold.

(1)

The map
\widetilde{p}^{*}\colon K_{G}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to K(X_{G},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)

is a weak equivalence.

(2)

If
G
is special,

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon K_{G}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to K(X^{\bullet}_{G},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)

is a weak equivalence.

(3)

Item (2) of Theorem
1.6
is true also
K
-theory.

Remark 1.8
.

A version (where the
I_{G}
-completion is replaced by Carlsson’s completion)
of the Borel part (equivalence of
\widetilde{p}^{*}
in (1)(ii)) in Theorem
1.6

is claimed in
[
16
]
for action
of any linear algebraic group
G
over an infinite perfect field
k
on any
normal quasi-projective
k
-scheme
X
. However, the key steps
of
[
16
]
are its Lemma 2.8 and
Proposition 2.9, whose correct proofs are not yet known.

1.4.
Completions at other ideals of
R(G)

The Atiyah-Segal completion theorem describes the completion of equivariant

K
-theory at the augmentation ideal. In order to fully recover equivariant

K
-theory from the ordinary
K
-theory, this completion alone is however not
enough and one instead needs to describe the completions of equivariant

K
-theory at all maximal ideals of
R(G)
in terms of ordinary
K
-theory.
This approach of describing equivariant algebraic
K
-theory was first studied by
Edidin-Graham
[
31
]
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero and

G
a linear algebraic group acting on
an algebraic space
X
. Let
g\in G(k)
be a semisimple element and let

\Psi\subset G(k)
be the conjugacy class of
g
. We let

R_{k}(G)=R(G)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}k
and let
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}\subset R_{k}(G)

denote the maximal ideal defined by
\Psi
. Let
X^{g}
denote the fixed point
subspace of
X
for the action of
g
and let
Z_{g}\subset G
denote the centralizer
of
g
. Let
K^{\prime}_{G}(-,k)
(resp.
K^{\prime}(-,k)
) denote the equivariant (resp.
ordinary)
K
-theory functor with
k
-coefficients.
The following result addresses the completion problem at an arbitrary maximal ideal of

R_{k}(G)
.

Theorem 1.9
.

There are natural weak equivalences of spectra

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X,k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K^{\prime}((X^{g})^{\bullet}_{Z_{g}},k);\ \ \ \widetilde{p}^{*}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X,k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K^{\prime}((X^{g})_{Z_{g}},k).

We also prove the analogous statement for the homotopy
K
-theory when
G
acts
on
X
with nice stabilizers.

1.5.
Thomason’s completion of equivariant homology theories

We now turn our attention to equivariant homology theory
(i.e., Hochschild, negative cyclic, cyclic and periodic cyclic homology)
of schemes over a field
k
with group action.
Recall that these homology theories for commutative
k
-algebras can be computed
using explicit Hochschild complexes. Using their
étale descent property for
k
-schemes,
one can also compute them for arbitrary schemes. However, the
equivariant Hochschild (and other) homology of a scheme with a group action is
defined abstractly as the Hochschild (and other)
homology theory of the dg category of equivariant
perfect complexes. As such, they are very difficult to compute in general,
just like equivariant
K
-theory.
It is therefore natural to ask if Thomason’s completion
theorem holds for these equivariant
homology theories so that they could be computed using ordinary
homology theory. We settle this question as well.

For a
k
-scheme
X
, let
HH(X)
(resp.
HC^{-}(X),HC(X),HP(X)
)
denote the Hochschild (resp. negative cyclic, cyclic, periodic cyclic) homology
of
X
relative to
k
. For the action of a group
G
on a
k
-scheme
X
, we let

HH_{G}(X)
(resp.
HC^{-}_{G}(X),HC_{G}(X),HP_{G}(X)
) denote the equivariant Hochschild
(resp. negative cyclic, cyclic, periodic cyclic) homology of
X
relative to
k
.
These are objects of the derived category of
k
-vector spaces. We can therefore
consider them as spectra via the Eilenberg-MacLane functor.
Our result is the following.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero.
Let
G
be a reductive group over
k
acting on a smooth
k
-scheme
X
.
Let
g\in G(k)
be a semisimple element and let

\Psi\subset G(k)
be the conjugacy class of
g
.
Then for any smooth surjective map
p\colon U\to[{X^{g}}/{Z_{g}}]
with

U\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
, one has a natural weak equivalence of spectra

(1.3)

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon HH_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HH(U_{n}).

The same is also true for negative cyclic, cyclic and periodic cyclic homology.

Note that
\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HH(U_{n})
coincides with
HH((X^{g})^{\bullet}_{Z_{g}})

(the Hochschild homology of the bar construction associated to
Z_{g}
-action on

X^{g}
) if we let
U=X^{g}
. Theorem
1.10
therefore is a more general
result than Theorem
1.9
.
Note also that the special case of Theorem
1.10
when
g
is the identity
element of
G
is an analogue of part (2) of Theorem
1.1
for
equivariant homology theories.

Recall that equivariant homology theories do not satisfy equivariant étale
descent. Using that they do satisfy étale descent for schemes, a direct
application of Theorem
1.10
however is the following.

Corollary 1.11
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero.
Let
G
be a reductive group over
k
acting on a smooth
k
-scheme
X
.
Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a
G
-equivariant étale cover. Then the
pull-back map

f^{*}\colon HH_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HH_{G}(X^{\prime}_{n})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}

is a weak equivalence, where
\{X^{\prime}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is the C̆ech nerve of
f
.
In other words,
HH_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}
satisfies equivariant étale descent.
The same is also true for negative cyclic, cyclic and periodic cyclic homology.

1.6.
Results for action with finite stabilizers

In the final set of results, we consider the special case of group action in which
the group
G
acts on a scheme
X
with finite stabilizers.
Equivalently,
[X/G]
is a Deligne-Mumford stack.
In this case, we prove a stronger result, namely, that the
equivariant
KH
-theory of
X
coincides with the ordinary
KH
-theory of the Borel
and bar constructions associated to the
G
-action on the inertia scheme
I_{X}

even without passing to the completion. We prove similar results for the equivariant
homology theories. More precisely, our result is the following.

Theorem 1.12
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero.
Let
G
be a linear algebraic group over
k
acting on a
k
-scheme
X
with
finite stabilizers. Let
I_{X}
denote the inertia scheme. We now have the following.

(1)

There are natural weak equivalences of spectra

\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{G}_{X}\colon KH_{G}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\simeq}KH((I_{X})^{\bullet}_{G},k);\ \ \ \Upsilon^{G}_{X}\colon KH_{G}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\simeq}KH((I_{X})_{G},k).

(2)

If
G
is reductive and
X
is smooth over
k
, then for any smooth surjective
map
p\colon U\to[{I_{X}}/{G}]
with
U\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
,
there is a natural weak equivalence of spectra

\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{G}_{X}\colon HH_{G}(X)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HH(U_{n}).

The same also holds for negative cyclic, cyclic and periodic cyclic homology.

For the periodic cyclic homology, we prove the above result even if
X
is
a singular scheme (cf. Theorem
15.8
).

Recall that a morphism of stacks
f\colon{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is called isovariant
étale if it is an étale map of stacks such that the canonical map

I_{\mathcal{Y}}\to I_{\mathcal{X}}\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{Y}}
is an isomorphism (cf.
[
89
, § 2.5]
).
Recall also that a presheaf of spectra
F
on the category of stacks is
said to satisfy isovariant étale descent if given any isovariant étale
cover
f\colon{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
, the canonical map
F({\mathcal{X}})\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}F({\mathcal{Y}}_{n})
is a
weak equivalence, where
\{{\mathcal{Y}}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is the C̆ech nerve of
f
.
Using the fact that homology theories satisfy étale descent for schemes,
Theorem
1.12
implies the following.

Corollary 1.13
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero and let

G
be a reductive group over
k
. Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a
G
-equivariant
map between schemes over
k
with
G
-action with finite stabilizers.
Assume that the induced
map of quotient stacks
{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is an isovariant étale cover. Then the pull-back map
HH_{G}(X)\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HH_{G}(X^{\prime}_{n})
is a weak equivalence,
where
\{X^{\prime}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is the C̆ech nerve of
f
.
The same is also true for negative cyclic, cyclic and periodic cyclic homology.

From Theorem
1.12
, we also obtain the following
computation of all equivariant homology groups. To state it, we let

~{}_{s_{\leq m}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{I_{\mathcal{X}}}
and
~{}_{s_{\geq m}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{I_{\mathcal{X}}}
denote the
stupid truncations of the de Rham complex
\Omega^{\bullet}_{I_{\mathcal{X}}}
of

I_{{\mathcal{X}}}=[{I_{X}}/G]
. Let
H^{*}_{dR}(I_{\mathcal{X}})={\mathbb{H}}^{*}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(I_{\mathcal{X}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{I_{\mathcal{X}}})
.

Theorem 1.14
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero.
Let
G
be a reductive group over
k
acting on a smooth
k
-scheme
X
with
finite stabilizers. For any integer

n\geq 0
, we then have natural isomorphisms of
k
-vector spaces

\pi_{n}(HH_{G}(X))\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{0\leq i\leq d}{\bigoplus}}H^{i-n}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(I_{\mathcal{X}},\Omega^{i}_{{I_{\mathcal{X}}}/k});

\pi_{n}(HC^{-}_{G}(X))\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{0\leq i\leq d-n}{\bigoplus}}H^{n+2i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(I_{\mathcal{X}},~_{s_{\geq i+n}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{I_{\mathcal{X}}}).

If the
G
-action on
X
is proper, then we also have

\pi_{n}(HC_{G}(X))\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{-d\leq i\leq n}{\bigoplus}}H^{n-2i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(I_{\mathcal{X}},~_{s_{\leq n-i}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{I_{\mathcal{X}}});

\pi_{n}(HP_{G}(X))\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\prod}}H^{n+2i}_{dR}(I_{{\mathcal{X}}}).

When
G
is finite, Theorem
1.14
for Hochschild homology can be easily derived
from
[
4
, Prop. 3.1]
which uses a different method. It can also be derived
from
[
1
, Thm. 4.9]
. For separated Deligne-Mumford stacks, Theorem
1.14

for Hochschild homology is asserted in
[
39
, Prop. 2.13]

(but see Remark
15.6
).

1.7.
Future direction

We indicate a few directions in which the results of this paper may be extended.
First, we would like to know if the second part of Theorem
1.6
could be
extended to positive characteristic and to more general group actions.
Our proof uses equivariant resolution of singularities which is a hindrance
in positive characteristic. If we work with finite (but prime to characteristic)
coefficients, this problem
can be resolved if a version of alteration could be found for stacks but we are not
aware of its existence.

Second, we would like to appropriately extend our results on homology theories
in characteristic zero to similar results for their topological counterparts
(i.e., topological Hochschild, cyclic and periodic cyclic homology with

{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-coefficients) in characteristic
p>0
. In particular, we would like to
know if these topological homology theories satisfy the equivariant (isovariant)
étale descent for Deligne-Mumford stacks.
These questions will be taken up in a separate project.

1.8.
Layout of the paper

The most difficult among our results on equivariant
K
-theory is Theorem
1.1
.
This should not be surprising to a reader who is familiar with
Thomason’s proof of his completion
theorem in
[
86
]
(which is a very special case of
Theorem
1.1
) which spreads into too many ingenious but very intricate steps.
We explain our strategy for proving Theorem
1.1
which is
completely different from Thomason’s proof (and the latter does not work in
the general case we consider).

First of all, it is not hard to realize that there is simply no direct way to compare
the equivariant
K
-theory of
X
with the ordinary
K
-theory of its bar
construction
X^{\bullet}_{G}
.
This is in sharp contrast to the Borel construction (which is an inductive
limit of open subschemes of equivariant vector bundles over
X
and hence allows
one to use homotopy invariance and localization to directly
relate its ordinary
K
-theory with the equivariant
K
-theory of
X
).
We use the machinery of
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-homotopy theory to overcome this problem.

Our idea is to use Theorem
1.4
to derive Theorem
1.1
.
The first step do so is to show that there is a uniquely defined
motivic space
X_{G}
(called the motivic Borel space) in the

{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-homotopy category of Morel-Voevodsky
[
72
]

which does not depend on the choice of an admissible gadget we make in the
definition of Borel equivariant
K
-theory (cf. Proposition
9.3
).
This allows us to get rid of the dependency problem (on admissible gadgets)
which is essential in proof of Theorem
1.1
.

We would now like to construct a map between

X^{\bullet}_{G}
and
X_{G}
. Unfortunately, no such map exists at the level of
simplicial schemes. To get around this issue, we introduce another
motivic space, namely,
\widetilde{X}_{G}:=X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
in the

{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-homotopy category, where
{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
is the universal
G
-torsor over
the classifying motivic space
{\mathcal{B}}_{G}
for the group
G
, constructed by
Morel-Voevodsky. To be able to work with
{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
, we need
G
to be special
because maps to
{\mathcal{B}}_{G}
in the
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-homotopy category do not represent
Nisnevich local
G
-torsors unless
G
is special. This explains our dependency on

G
being special.

Having constructed
\widetilde{X}_{G}
, the next step is to construct a direct map
from
X_{G}
to it.
Using the representability of
K
-theory by an object of
Voevodsky’s stable homotopy category
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}_{k}
(cf.
[
97
]
),
we then show that this map induces a weak equivalence between their
K
-theory
spectra. Next, we show that the projection
X{\times}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}\to X
induces a canonical
map from the
I_{G}
-completion of the equivariant
K
-theory spectrum of
X
to the
ordinary
K
-theory spectrum of
\widetilde{X}_{G}
. At this stage, we invoke
Theorem
1.4
to conclude that this map is a weak equivalence.

We are now left to show that the
K
-theory of
\widetilde{X}_{G}
is canonically
equivalent to that of the bar construction
X^{\bullet}_{G}
.
For this, we construct a simplicial scheme
E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)
which is a
G
-torsor
over
X^{\bullet}_{G}
. In particular, the
K
-theory of the latter
can be realized as the equivariant
K
-theory of
E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)
.
To conclude, we show that there is a direct
G
-equivariant map

E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)\to X{\times}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
which induces a weak equivalence between
the ordinary
K
-theory of
\widetilde{X}_{G}
and the equivariant
K
-theory of

E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)
.

To prove Theorem
1.9
, our major ingredient is Theorem
8.8
.
This is a derived version of the nonabelian completion theorem of Edidin-Graham

[
31
]
and is a result of independent interest in equivariant
K
-theory.
We expect this result to have other future applications.
Even if Theorem
8.8
is directly inspired by the
nonabelian completion theorem of Edidin-Graham, its proof can not be derived from that
of the latter. One reason for this is that the homotopy groups in general behave
very poorly when one passes to the completion. In fact, this was the main
difficulty Thomason encountered in extending Atiyah-Segal theorem to algebraic

K
-theory. This forces us go to through several reduction steps to
prove Theorem
8.8
.

We prove Theorem
1.9
by combining Theorems
1.1
and

1.4
with the derived nonabelian completion theorem. We
prove these results for the homotopy
K
-theory by reduction to the
K^{\prime}
-theory
by means of an equivariant version of cdh descent, shown in
[
50
]
.

To prove the completion theorem for the equivariant homology theories, we
first deal with the Hochschild homology. We prove this case by writing the
equivariant Hochschild homology as the derived global section of Toën’s derived
loop space construction
[
91
]
.
We then use the formal HKR theorem for Hochschild homology by Ben-Zvi and
Nadler
[
7
]
to complete the proof. The proof for
negative cyclic homology is easily reduced to that of Hochschild homology.

The major difficulty lies in proving the completion theorems for cyclic and periodic
cyclic homology because it involves commuting homotopy limits and colimits.
In order to handle this issue, we use a derived HKR-filtration of the
equivariant Hochschild
homology and study the resulting graded pieces using arguments of
Bhatt-Morrow-Scholze
[
9
, § 3]
. We then show that the homotopy orbit spaces
for the
S^{1}
-action (which is a homotopy colimit) of these graded pieces commute
with latter’s homotopy limit. We then use an inductive argument and boundedness of the
derived odd tangent bundle of
[X/G]
to finish the proof of the completion theorem for
cyclic homology. The result for periodic cyclic homology then follows by a formal
argument.

We prove our results on the equivariant
K
-theory and homology theories for
action with finite stabilizers by combining Theorems
8.8
,

8.12
and
1.12
with Proposition
5.8
.

In §
2
, we establish some important results of independent interest
about the representation ring
and in §
3
, we review basic results on equivariant
K
-theory and
homology theories. In §
4
and §
5
, we
complete two key technical steps, namely, the decomposition theorem and
the construction of twisting operator. We complete the proofs of
Theorems
1.4
and
1.9
in §
7
and
§
8
. We finish the proof of Theorem
1.1
in
§
12
. We prove Theorem
1.10
in §
14
.
In §
15
, we prove Theorem
1.12

and its applications.

1.9.
Notations

Throughout the paper,
k
will denote an arbitrary base field.
We shall use the notation
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
for the category of separated and finite type

k
-schemes. An object of
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
will be called a
k
-scheme. The product of two
schemes
X
and
Y
in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
will be denoted by
X\times Y
.
We let
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
denote the subcategory of
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
consisting of smooth
k
-schemes.
For any ring extension
k\hookrightarrow R
and
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
, we let

X_{R}=X\times{\rm Spec\,}(R)
. If
X
is affine, we let
k[X]
denote its
coordinate ring.

A
k
-group
G
will mean a linear algebraic group (i.e., a smooth closed subgroup
scheme of a general linear group
GL_{n}
) over
k
.
We let
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{k}
(resp.
{\mathbf{Sm}}^{G}_{k}
) denote the
subcategory of
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
(resp.
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
) consisting of schemes with
G
-action and

G
-equivariant morphisms. An object
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{k}
will be called

G
-quasi-projective if it is a quasi-projective
k
-scheme with linear
G
-action.
We let
{\mathbf{Spc}}_{k}
denote the category of finite type separated algebraic spaces
(termed as
k
-spaces) over
k
and let

{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
denote the subcategory of
{\mathbf{Spc}}_{k}
consisting of
algebraic spaces with
G
-action and
G
-equivariant morphisms.

We shall let
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}
denote the stable homotopy category of
topological
S^{1}
-spectra and
{\mathbb{S}}\in{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}
denote the sphere spectrum.
If
E
is a presheaf on a subcategory of
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
with values in
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}
and

L
is any field of characteristic
zero, we shall let
E(X,L)
be the unique spectrum having the
property that
\pi_{i}(E(X,L))=\pi_{i}(E(X))\otimes L
for every
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. For a
positive integer
n
,
E(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n)
will denote the smash product spectrum

E(X)\wedge{{\mathbb{S}}}/n
, where
{{\mathbb{S}}}/n
is the mod-
n
Moore spectrum.
Its homotopy groups are connected to those of
E(X)
via the universal coefficient
theorem.

All rings in this paper will be unital and commutative. Given a ring
R
, we let

H_{R}\in{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}
denote the Eilenberg-MacLane ring spectrum associated to
R
.
The tensor product of two abelian groups
A
and
B
will be written as

A\otimes B
.

2.
Geometry of representation ring

Let
k
be a field. We let
{{\mathbf{Grp}}}_{k}
denote the category of
k
-groups and
group homomorphisms. For
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
, we let
R(G)
denote the
representation ring of
G
. We shall assume all representations of
G
to be

k
-rational and finite-dimensional. For any ring
\Lambda
, we let

R_{\Lambda}(G)=R(G){\otimes}\Lambda
. In this section, we collect some
results about the ring
R_{k}(G)
and its spectrum
{\rm Spec\,}(R_{k}(G))
.
When
k
is the field of complex
numbers, these results were proven in
[
30
, § 2]

using the representation theory of compact complex Lie groups.
The results of this section will be used throughout this paper.

2.1.
Finite generation

For
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
and
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{k}
, we let
[X/G]
denote the stack quotient and
let
K_{0}([X/G])
denote the Grothendieck group of
locally free sheaves on
[X/G]
. We write
{\rm pt}={\rm Spec\,}(k)
and
BG=[{{\rm pt}}/G]
.
We shall let
\overline{k}
denote an algebraic closure of
k
.
We begin with the following result which should be known to experts.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
and
{k^{\prime}}/k
an algebraic field extension. Then the pull-back
maps
R(G_{l})\to R(G_{k^{\prime}})
for finite subextensions
l\subseteq k^{\prime}
of
k
induce
an isomorphism of rings

(2.1)

\gamma^{G}_{k}\colon{\underset{l\subseteq k^{\prime},[l:k]<\infty}{\mathop{\text{\rm colim}}}}\ R(G_{l})\xrightarrow{\cong}R(G_{k^{\prime}}).

Proof.

It is clear that
\gamma^{G}_{k}
is a ring homomorphism.
To prove that this is an isomorphism, we first observe
that
R(G_{l})={F(G_{l})}/{E(G_{l})}
for every
l\subset k^{\prime}
if we let
F(G_{l})
denote
the free abelian group
on the set of isomorphism classes of representations of
G_{l}
and
E(G_{l})
denote
the subgroup of
F(G_{l})
generated by virtual representations

[V^{\prime}]+[V^{\prime\prime}]-[V]
associated to short exact sequences of representations

0\to V^{\prime}\to V\to V^{\prime\prime}\to 0
.
We next observe using the faithfully flat descent that given any finite collection
of
G_{k^{\prime}}
-representations and a finite collection of morphisms (or isomorphisms)
between them, we can find a finite subextension

l\subseteq k^{\prime}
such that these two collections are defined over
l
.
Lastly, we observe that any finite collection of short exact sequences of

G_{k^{\prime}}
-representations is also defined (and is short exact) over a finite
subextension
l\subseteq k^{\prime}
.
It is easy to check that these observations imply the claim that
\gamma^{G}_{k}
is
bijective.
∎

Recall that
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
is called split reductive if it is a connected reductive

k
-group and admits a maximal torus which splits over
k
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
and
{k^{\prime}}/k
a field extension. Then the pull-back map

R(G)\to R(G_{k^{\prime}})
is injective. This map is an isomorphism if
G
is
split reductive.

Proof.

We first assume that
{k^{\prime}}/k
is an algebraic extension.
By Lemma
2.1
, it suffices in this case to prove the lemma when

{k^{\prime}}/k
is a finite extension. In the latter case, we have the pull-back and
push-forward maps

R(G)\xrightarrow{\iota^{*}}R(G_{k^{\prime}})
and
R(G_{k^{\prime}})\cong K_{0}([{{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})}/{G}])\xrightarrow{\iota_{*}}R(G)
such that
\iota_{*}\circ\iota^{*}
is
multiplication by
[k^{\prime}:k]
.
Since
R(G)
is the free abelian group on the set of isomorphism classes of
irreducible representations of
G
(cf.
[
51
, App. C]
),
it follows that
R(G)\to R(G_{k^{\prime}})
is injective.

In the general case, we can find field extensions
k\subset l\subset k^{\prime}
, where

l/k
is a purely transcendental extension and
{k^{\prime}}/l
is an algebraic extension.
If
k
is infinite, we can write
R(G_{l})
as the filtered colimit of
K_{0}([U/G])
,
where
U\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{n}_{k}
is an open subscheme for some
n\geq 0
with the trivial

G
-action (cf.
[
56
, Thm. 2.5]
).
The pull-back maps
R(G)=K_{0}(BG)\to K_{0}([{{\mathbb{A}}^{n}_{k}}/G])
and

K_{0}([{{\mathbb{A}}^{n}_{k}}/G])\to K_{0}([U/G])
are bijective (by the homotopy invariance) and
surjective, respectively.
On the other hand, our hypothesis implies that every such
U
contains
k
-rational
points. This implies that the pull-back
R(G)\to K_{0}([U/G])
admits a section.
We deduce that this map is actually an isomorphism.
Passing to the limit, we conclude that
R(G)\to R(G_{l})
is an isomorphism.

If
k
is not necessarily infinite, we look at the commutative diagram

(2.2)

\textstyle{R(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(G_{\overline{k}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(G_{l})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(G_{l^{\prime}}),}

where
l^{\prime}=l\otimes_{k}\overline{k}
. It is easy to check that
l^{\prime}
is a field which is
a purely transcendental extension of
\overline{k}
. In particular, the right vertical
arrow is bijective. We showed above that the horizontal arrows are injective.
It follows that the left vertical arrow is injective.

If
G
is split reductive and
T
is a split maximal torus of
G
with the
associated Weyl group
W
, then
[
80
, Thm. 4]
says that
the canonical map of representation rings induces a ring isomorphism

(2.3)

R(G)\xrightarrow{\cong}{R(T)}^{W}.

Since the map
R(T)\to R(T_{k^{\prime}})
is clearly an isomorphism, a similar assertion
also holds for
G
.
∎

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
and let
H
be a closed subgroup of
G
. Assume that either
H

is connected or
{\rm char}(k)=0
. Then the restriction map of integral
representation rings
R(G)\to R(H)
is finite. In particular,
R(H)
is
Noetherian.

Proof.

It is enough to prove the proposition when
G
is a general linear group over
k
.
We divide the proof of the latter case into two cases.

Case 1:

k
is algebraically closed.

If
H
is a maximal torus
T
of
G
and
W
is the associated Weyl
group, then the natural map
{{\mathbb{Z}}}[N]\to R(T)
is
an isomorphism of rings (where
N
is the character group of
T
) which implies
that
R(T)
is a finite type algebra over
{\mathbb{Z}}
. In particular, it is finite over

{R(T)}^{W}
which is then also a finite type
{\mathbb{Z}}
-algebra
(cf. proof of
[
58
, Lem. 3.9]
) and hence Noetherian. It follows from
(
2.3
) that
R(G)
is Noetherian.

Suppose now that
H
is any closed subgroup of
G
. If
V
is an irreducible
representation of
H
and if the unipotent radical
R_{u}H
of
H
acts
non-trivially on
V
, then the invariant subspace of
V
for this action
is a non-zero subspace (cf.
[
12
, Cor. 10.5]
). Since
R_{u}H
is normal in
H
,
such a subspace must be a subrepresentation of
G
inside
V
.
But this contradicts the irreducibility of
V
. Hence

R_{u}H
must act trivially on
V
. Since
R(H)
is a free abelian group
on the set of irreducible representations of
H

(cf.
[
51
, App. C]
), this implies that the
map
R(H)\to R(H/{R_{u}H)}
is an isomorphism. Hence we can assume that

H
is reductive.

If
H
is connected, we choose a maximal
torus
S
of
H
. Since
k
is algebraically closed, there is a maximal
torus
T
of
G
containing
S
. We thus get a commutative diagram
of representation rings

\textstyle{R(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(S).}

We showed above that the horizontal arrows are finite and injective
maps. Since
S
is a subtorus of
T
, there is a decomposition
T=S\times S^{\prime}
and hence the map
R(T)\to R(S)
is surjective. In particular,

R(S)
is finite over
R(G)
. Since
R(G)
is Noetherian, we conclude that
R(H)
is
finite over
R(G)
.

Suppose now that
{\rm char}(k)=0
and
H
is not necessarily connected.
We first assume that
k\subset{\mathbb{C}}
and look at the commutative diagram
of rings

(2.4)

\textstyle{R(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(G_{{\mathbb{C}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(H_{{\mathbb{C}}}),}

where the horizontal arrows are induced by the change of fields.
The top horizontal arrow is an isomorphism of Noetherian rings and the
right vertical arrow is a finite ring homomorphism by

[
79
, Prop. 3.2]
(see also
[
30
, Prop. 2.3]
).
It follows that
R(H_{{\mathbb{C}}})
is finite over
R(G)
. It suffices therefore to
show that the base change map
R(H)\to R(H_{{\mathbb{C}}})
is injective. But this
follows from Lemma
2.2
.

If
k
is not necessarily a subfield of
{\mathbb{C}}
, we choose a finitely generated
subfield
k^{\prime}\subset k
such that
G
is defined over
k^{\prime}
. We can write

k=\varinjlim_{\lambda}k_{\lambda}
,
where
\{k_{\lambda}\}
is the filtered family of all finitely generated subfields of

k
which contain
k^{\prime}
. We now look at the commutative diagram

(2.5)

\textstyle{R(G_{k_{\lambda}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(H_{k_{\lambda}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(H).}

The lower horizontal arrow in (
2.5
) is injective for each

\lambda
by Lemma
2.2
. It now follows easily that the canonical map

\varinjlim_{\lambda}R(H_{k_{\lambda}})\to R(H)
is an isomorphism. Since
R(H)
is a
finitely generated ring by
[
89
, Prop. 4.1]
, the lower horizontal
arrow in (
2.5
) must actually be bijective for some
\lambda
.
On the other hand, the left vertical arrow is a finite ring homomorphism
since each
k_{\lambda}
admits an embedding inside
{\mathbb{C}}
. Since the top horizontal
arrow is a bijection, we deduce that the right vertical arrow is a finite ring
homomorphism.

Case 2:

k
is not algebraically closed.

We consider the commutative diagram of representation rings

\textstyle{R(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(G_{\overline{k}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R(H_{\overline{k}})}

Since the top horizontal arrow is an isomorphism and the right vertical arrow is
a finite ring homomorphism, it follows that
R(H_{\overline{k}})
is finite over
R(G)
.
We now use Lemma
2.2
to conclude that
R(H)
is finite over

R(G)
.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

If
{\rm char}(k)>0
and
H^{0}_{\overline{k}}
is the identity component of
H_{\overline{k}}
, then
Brauer’s theorem says that
R(H)
is a finitely generated abelian group.
Combining this fact with Proposition
2.3
for
H^{0}_{\overline{k}}
, it should be
possible to deduce that
R(H)
is finite over
R(G)
even if
H
is not connected.
But we do not know how to complete the proof.

Recall that the augmentation ideal
I_{G}\subset R(G)
is the kernel of the rank map

R(G)\twoheadrightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}
. If
\Lambda
is any torsion-free ring, the kernel of the augmentation
map
R(G)_{\Lambda}\twoheadrightarrow\Lambda
will also be called the augmentation ideal.
We shall need the following result about
I_{G}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

The augmentation ideal is the radical of a finitely generated ideal in
R(G)
.

Proof.

We choose a closed embedding
G\hookrightarrow GL_{n}
and let

R(GL_{n})\to R(G)
denote the induced map. Let
I^{\prime}_{G}\subset I_{G}
denote the
extension of
I_{GL_{n}}
under this ring homomorphism. One knows from

[
29
, Cor. 6.1]
that the
I_{G}
-adic topology on
R(G)
coincides with its

I^{\prime}_{G}
-adic topology. This implies that
I_{G}=\sqrt{I_{G}}=\sqrt{I^{\prime}_{G}}
.
On the other hand, one knows that
R(GL_{n})
is Noetherian
which implies that
I^{\prime}_{G}\subset R(G)
is finitely generated.
∎

2.2.
Relation with class functions

Let
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
.
Recall from
[
62
, § 2]
(see also
[
33
, Chap. 1, § 1]
) that an
action of
G
on an affine
k
-scheme
X
is equivalent to the map of
k
-algebras

(2.6)

\widehat{\alpha}:k[X]\to k[G]{\otimes}_{k}k[X]

which satisfies the commutativity of certain diagrams of
k
-algebras.

Definition 2.6
.

We shall say that an element
f
of
k[X]
is
G
-invariant for the above action
if
{\widehat{\alpha}}(f)=1{\otimes}f
.

It is easy to check that the set of
G
-invariant elements in
k[X]
is
a
k
-subalgebra. We denote it by
{k[X]}^{G}
. This is the
coordinate ring of the universal geometric quotient (assuming it exists)
of
X
for the action of

G
on
X
. In particular, if
k
is algebraically closed, then
{k[X]}^{G}

is the set of invariant elements in
k[X]={\rm Hom}_{{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}}(X,{{\mathbb{A}}}^{1}_{k})
for the
action of
G(k)
on
k[X]
given by

(2.7)

G(k)\times k[X]\to k[X]

(g,f)\mapsto f^{g},

where
f^{g}(x)=f(gx)
.

We now specialize to the adjoint (conjugation) action of
G
on itself.
In terms of the dual action of Hopf algebras, we represent this action by

k[G]\xrightarrow{\widehat{ad}}k[G]{\otimes}_{k}k[G]
and write
{k[G]}^{G}
as
C[G]
.
If
k
is algebraically closed, it follows from (
2.7
) that

C[G]
is the ring of class functions on
G
, i.e., those regular functions on
G
which
take a constant value on a conjugacy class.
In order to understand the geometry of
{\rm Spec\,}(R_{k}(G))
, we need to connect
R_{k}(G)

with
C[G]
. We do it as follows.

Let
(V,\rho)
be an
n
-dimensional representation of
G
given
by the morphism of
k
-groups
G\xrightarrow{\rho}GL(V)
.
Let
{\chi}\colon GL(V)\subset{\rm End}(V)\to{{\mathbb{A}}}^{1}_{k}
denote the
character morphism described algebraically by the composite map

(2.8)

k[t]\xrightarrow{\widehat{\chi}}k[X_{ij}]\hookrightarrow k[X_{ij},1/{\rm det}],

{\widehat{\chi}}(t)=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n}}{{\underset{i=1}{\Sigma}}}{X_{ii}}.

Composing this with
\rho\colon G\to GL(V)
, we get the character map

(2.9)

k[t]\xrightarrow{{\widehat{\chi}}_{\rho}}k[G].

It is easy to check using the standard properties of trace of matrices that the
assignment
(V,\rho)\mapsto{\widehat{\chi}}_{{\rho}}(t)

induces a
k
-algebra morphism
R_{k}(G)\xrightarrow{{\phi}_{G}}k[G]
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
G
be a split reductive
k
-group. Then the map
\phi_{G}
induces
an injective homomorphism of
k
-algebras

(2.10)

{\phi}_{G}\colon R_{k}(G)\to C[G].

It is an isomorphism if
{\rm char}(k)=0
.

Proof.

We first have to show that the image of
{\phi}_{G}
is contained in
C[G]
.
For this, it suffices to show for any representation
(V,\rho)
that

\widehat{ad}\circ{\widehat{\chi}}_{\rho}=1\otimes{\widehat{\chi}}_{\rho}
. Since the map
k[G]{\otimes}_{k}k[G]\to{\overline{k}}[G_{\overline{k}}]{\otimes}_{\overline{k}}{\overline{k}}[G_{\overline{k}}]
is injective,
it suffices to show that
\widehat{ad}_{\overline{k}}\circ{\widehat{\chi}}_{{\rho}_{\overline{k}}}=1\otimes{\widehat{\chi}}_{{\rho}_{\overline{k}}}
. Thus we can assume that

k
is algebraically closed. In this case, we can use
2.7

to reduce to showing that
{\chi}_{\rho}(ghg^{-1})={\chi}_{\rho}(h)
, where

\chi_{\rho}\colon G\to{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{k}
is induced by

{\widehat{\chi}}_{\rho}
. But this is standard.

To show that
\phi_{G}
is injective in general and bijective in characteristic zero,
we fix a split maximal torus
T\subset G
and a Borel
B
containing
T
. Then
G

is uniquely described by a root system over
k
, hence its Weyl
group
W
is a constant finite group which does not depend on the
base change of
G
by any field extension of
k
. Moreover,
W
acts on

T
and hence on
R_{k}(T)
and
C[T]\cong k[T]
such that the map
{\phi}_{T}

is
W
-equivariant. Thus we have a commutative diagram

(2.11)

\textstyle{R_{k}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\phi}_{G}}
\scriptstyle{{\eta}_{R}}
\textstyle{C[G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k[G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\eta}_{C}}
\textstyle{{R_{k}(T)}^{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\phi}^{W}_{T}}
\textstyle{{C[T]}^{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k[T],}

where
\eta_{R}
and
\eta_{C}
are the restriction maps.

One knows from
[
89
, Thm. 1.13]
that the restriction map

R(G)\to R(T)
is split injective and
[
80
, Thm. 4]
says that
its image is
R(T)^{W}
. This implies that

R_{k}(G)\to R_{k}(T)
is split injective whose image lies in
R_{k}(T)^{W}
.
If
{\rm char}(k)=0
, it follows that
R_{k}(G)\to R_{k}(T)^{W}
is a bijection
(cf.
[
58
, Lem. 3.1]
). We have thus shown that the left vertical arrow
in (
2.11
) is injective in general and bijective if
{\rm char}(k)=0
.
Since
T
is a split torus,
{\phi}^{W}_{T}
is an isomorphism.
This already shows that
\phi_{G}
is injective.
The remaining claim that it is bijective if
{\rm char}(k)=0
follows from
Lemma
2.8
.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
2.7
and notations of its proof,
the canonical map
C[G]\to C[T]
is injective and its image lies in
{C[T]}^{W}
if

{\rm char}(k)=0
.

Proof.

If
k
is finitely generated as a field, then it has an embedding into
{\mathbb{C}}
.
In this case, we can use
the inclusion
C[G]\hookrightarrow C[G_{{\mathbb{C}}}]
and the
W
-equivariant inclusion

C[T]\hookrightarrow C[T_{{\mathbb{C}}}]
to reduce the proof of the lemma to the case when
k={\mathbb{C}}
.
But this case is well known (cf.
[
30
, § 2]
).
In general, we observe using the root systems that
G
and
T
are defined over

{\mathbb{Q}}
. We let
G^{\prime}
(resp.
T^{\prime}
) be a form of
G
(resp.
T
) over

{\mathbb{Q}}
. We write
k=\varinjlim_{\lambda}k_{\lambda}
,
where
\{k_{\lambda}\}
is the filtered family of all finitely generated subfields of

k
. We let
G_{\lambda}
(resp.
T_{\lambda}
) denote the base change of
G^{\prime}
(resp.
T^{\prime}
)
via the inclusion
{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow k_{\lambda}
.

Since
\varinjlim_{\lambda}k_{\lambda}[G_{\lambda}]\to k[G]

is an isomorphism and each
k_{\lambda}[G_{\lambda}]\otimes_{k_{\lambda}}k_{\lambda}[G_{\lambda}]\to k[G]\otimes_{k}k[G]
is injective, we see using (
2.6
)
and Definition
2.6
that
\varinjlim_{\lambda}C[G_{\lambda}]\to C[G]

is also an isomorphism. The same also holds for
T
. In particular,

\varinjlim_{\lambda}C[T_{\lambda}]^{W}\to C[T]^{W}
is an isomorphism. This reduces the
proof of the lemma to the case of finitely generated fields.
∎

2.3.
Results of Edidin-Graham

If
k
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero,
one can prove Proposition
2.7
for any reductive
k
-group
using a straightforward generalization of
[
30
, Prop. 2.2]
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Assume that
k
is algebraically closed of characteristic zero
and
G
is a reductive
k
-group. Then
{\phi}_{G}\colon R_{k}(G)\to C[G]

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Mimicking the proof of
[
30
, Prop. 2.2]
, we let
\widehat{G}
denote the set of
irreducible representations of
G
and consider the map

(2.12)

\phi^{\prime}_{G}\colon{\underset{V\in\widehat{G}}{\bigoplus}}V^{*}\otimes_{k}V\to k[G],

where on each
V\in\widehat{G}
, we have

\phi^{\prime}_{G}(\lambda\otimes v)=(g\mapsto\lambda(gv))
for

\lambda\in V^{*}
and
v\in V
. Then
\phi^{\prime}_{G}
is an isomorphism of

G
-representations by
[
83
, Thm. 27.3.9]

if we let
G
act diagonally on
V^{*}\otimes V
and by conjugation on
k[G]
.
Taking the
G
-invariants on both sides and noting that

(V^{*}\otimes_{k}V)^{G}\cong{\rm Hom}_{G}(V,V)\cong k_{V}\subset R_{k}(G)
,
we get an isomorphism

\phi^{\prime}_{G}\colon{\underset{V\in\widehat{G}}{\bigoplus}}k_{V}\to C[G]
,
where
k_{V}
denotes the 1-dimensional
k
-vector space spanned by the
class of
V
in
R_{k}(G)
. But one easily checks that
\phi^{\prime}_{G}
coincides with

\phi_{G}
if we identify
{\underset{V\in\widehat{G}}{\bigoplus}}k_{V}
with
R_{k}(G)

(cf.
[
51
, App. C]
).
∎

For
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
, we let
G_{s}(k)
denote the set of
k
-rational points
g\in G

such that
g
is a semisimple closed point. For
g\in G_{s}(k)
and a
representation
(V,\rho)
of
G
, we let
{\operatorname{tr}}_{g}([V])=\chi_{\rho}(g)=(\chi\circ\rho)(g)\in k
(cf. (
2.8
)). This extends linearly
to a
k
-algebra homomorphism
{\operatorname{tr}}_{g}\colon R_{k}(G)\to k
(cf.
[
30
, § 2.2]
).
Since the trace of
g
on the 1-dimensional trivial
representation is
1\in k^{\times}
, it follows that
{\operatorname{tr}}_{g}
is surjective. In
particular,
{\rm Ker}({\operatorname{tr}}_{g})
is a maximal ideal of
R_{k}(G)
. We denote this by
{\mathfrak{m}}_{g}
.
Letting
\Omega(R_{k}(G))
denote the max-spectrum of
R_{k}(G)
, we thus get a function

{\operatorname{tr}}_{G}\colon G(k)\to\Omega(R_{k}(G))
given by
{\operatorname{tr}}_{G}(g)={\mathfrak{m}}_{g}
.
It is easy to see from the
above discussion that
{\operatorname{tr}}_{G}
is a class function on
G_{s}(k)
.
If
\Psi
is the conjugacy class of
g
, we shall use the notations
{\mathfrak{m}}_{g}

and
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}
interchangeably throughout this paper.

The following are the straightforward consequences of Proposition
2.9
.
These were proven in
[
30
, § 2]
when
k={\mathbb{C}}
.

Corollary 2.10
.

Assume that
k
is algebraically closed of characteristic zero
and
G
is a reductive
k
-group.
Then a conjugacy class
\Psi
in
G(k)
is closed if and only if it is semisimple.

Proof.

The proof is identical to that of
[
30
, Prop. 2.4]
in view of
Proposition
2.9
once we observe that
[
33
, Chap. 1, Cor. 1.2]
holds
over any algebraically closed field (in fact, over any field).
∎

Corollary 2.11
.

Assume that
k
is algebraically closed of characteristic zero
and
G
is any
k
-group. Then the correspondence

{\Psi}\mapsto{{\mathfrak{m}}}_{\Psi}
gives a bijection between the set of
conjugacy classes in
G_{s}(k)
and
\Omega(R_{k}(G))
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to that of
[
30
, Prop. 2.5]
in view of
Proposition
2.9
.
∎

Corollary 2.12
.

Assume the conditions of Corollary
2.11
.
Let
G\hookrightarrow H
be a closed embedding into a
k
-group
H
and let

{\Psi}=C_{H}(h)
be a conjugacy class in
H_{s}(k)
. Then

{R_{k}(G)}_{{{\mathfrak{m}}}_{\Psi}}
is a semilocal ring with maximal ideals

\left\{{{\mathfrak{m}}}_{{\Psi}_{1}},\cdots,{{\mathfrak{m}}}_{{\Psi}_{r}}\right\}
where

{\Psi}_{1}\amalg\cdots\amalg{\Psi}_{r}={\Psi}\cap G
is the disjoint
union of some conjugacy classes in
G
.

Proof.

Using Proposition
2.3
and Corollary
2.11
,
we only need to show that if
\Psi^{\prime}
is a
semisimple conjugacy class in
G(k)
not contained in
{\Psi}\cap G
, then

{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}R_{k}(G)\not\subset{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi^{\prime}}
. The proof of this (over
{\mathbb{C}}
) given in

[
30
, Prop. 2.6]
is slightly erroneous. We therefore write out the correct
proof.
We let
\Psi_{0}
be the conjugacy class of
\Psi^{\prime}
in
H_{s}(k)
. Then
our assumption implies that
\Psi\neq\Psi_{0}
. Hence, we can find a virtual
character
f\in R_{k}(H)
which vanishes on
\Psi
but not on
\Psi_{0}
. The
restriction of such a character on
G
can not vanish on
\Psi^{\prime}
.
Equivalently,
f\in{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}R_{k}(G)\setminus{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi^{\prime}}
.
∎

3.
Equivariant
K
-theory and Hochschild homology

In this section, we shall review the definitions and basic properties
of equivariant
K
-theory. We shall also recall equivariant Hochschild homology and
other equivariant homology theories derived from the Hochschild homology.

Throughout this paper, the word
stack
over a field
k
(equivalently, a
k
-stack)
will mean the quotient stack
[X/G]
associated to the action of a
k
-group
G
on a

k
-space
X
(cf.
[
65
, § 2.4.2]
). In particular, all stacks in this
paper will be Noetherian Artin quotient stacks with affine (hence separated)
diagonal. As a consequence, all maps from schemes to stacks will be representable
by schemes. We shall let
c_{[X/G]}\colon[X/G]\to X/G
denote the coarse moduli space
map whenever it exists. If
[X/G]
is an algebraic space (equivalently,
c_{[X/G]}
is an
isomorphism), we shall identify
[X/G]
with
X/G
via
c_{[X/G]}
and use the latter
notation for both.

Recall (cf.
[
21
, Tag 06WS]
)
that if
\pi\colon X\to[X/G]
is the quotient map, then
the pull-back
\pi^{*}
induces a natural equivalence between the
categories of quasi-coherent
sheaves of
{\mathcal{O}}_{[X/G]}
-modules (on the Lisse-étale site
of
[X/G]
) and
G
-equivariant quasi-coherent sheaves of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules on
X

(on the Zariski site). This equivalence is maintained when we restrict to the
subcategories of coherent sheaves. In this paper, we shall make no distinction between
quasi-coherent sheaves on
[X/G]
and
G
-equivariant quasi-coherent sheaves on
X

via these equivalences.

3.1.
Review of equivariant
K
-theory

We fix a field
k
and a
k
-group
G
acting on a
k
-space
X
. We let

{\mathcal{X}}=[X/G]
with the quotient map
\pi\colon X\to{\mathcal{X}}
.
We shall consider the following categories.

(1)

{{\rm Qcoh}}_{[X/G]}\ =
abelian category of quasi-coherent sheaves on
[X/G]
.

(2)

{{\rm Coh}}^{0}_{[X/G]}\ =
abelian category of coherent sheaves on
[X/G]
.

(3)

{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X}\ =
abelian category of
G
-equivariant
quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
.

(4)

{\rm Coh}_{[X/G]}\ =
complicial
bi-Waldhausen category of strictly bounded complexes of coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{[X/G]}
-modules.

(5)

{\rm Perf}_{[X/G]}\ =
complicial
bi-Waldhausen category of perfect complexes of
{\mathcal{O}}_{[X/G]}
-modules.

The bi-Waldhausen structure of the last two categories is given with
respect to the degree wise split monomorphisms as cofibrations and quasi-isomorphisms
as weak equivalences.

We let
K^{\prime}([X/G])
denote the (Thomason-Trobaugh)

K
-theory spectrum of the bi-Waldhausen category of the cohomologically bounded
complexes of sheaves of
{\mathcal{O}}_{[X/G]}
-modules with coherent cohomology
(cf.
[
90
, Defn. 3.3]
,
[
56
, § 3.1]
). We let
K^{+}([X/G])
denote the

K
-theory spectrum of the bi-Waldhausen category
{\rm Perf}_{[X/G]}
.
We let
K([X/G])
denote the non-connective
K
-theory spectrum
of perfect complexes on
[X/G]

obtained by applying Schlichting’s delooping construction
[
77
]
to

K^{+}([X/G])
. There is a natural map of ring spectra
K^{+}([X/G])\to K([X/G])
such
that the induced map
K^{+}_{i}([X/G])\to K_{i}([X/G])
is an isomorphism for
i\geq 0
and

K^{+}_{i}([X/G])=0
for
i<0
.

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let

(3.1)

\Delta^{n}={\rm Spec\,}\left(\frac{k[t_{0},\cdots,t_{n}]}{(\sum_{i}t_{i}-1)}\right).

Recall that
\Delta^{\bullet}
is a cosimplicial
k
-scheme.
The
homotopy
K
-theory
(
KH
-theory in short) of
[X/G]
is defined as

(3.2)

KH([X/G])=\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{\Delta^{n}}K([X/G]\times\Delta^{n}).

There are canonical maps
K([X/G])\to KH([X/G])\to K^{\prime}([X/G])
, where the first map is
induced by
\Delta^{0}\in\Delta^{\bullet}
and the second by the homotopy invariance of

K^{\prime}([X/G])
(cf.
[
56
, Cor. 3.19]
.
These maps are weak equivalences if
[X/G]
is regular. We refer to

[
50
, § 4C]
for basic properties of
KH
-theory of stacks.
Recall (e.g., see the proof of
[
63
, Lem. 3.1]
)
that for the
G
-action on
X
as above, the equivariant
K^{\prime}
-theory spectrum

K^{\prime}_{G}(X)
is canonically equivalent to the spectrum
K^{\prime}([X/G])
via
\pi^{*}
.
Similarly, we have
\pi^{*}\colon K^{+}([X/G])\xrightarrow{\simeq}K^{+}_{G}(X)
,

\pi^{*}\colon K([X/G])\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{G}(X)
and
\pi^{*}\colon KH([X/G])\xrightarrow{\simeq}KH_{G}(X)
.

For a commutative
{\mathbb{Q}}
-algebra
R
, we let
K([X/G],R)=K([X/G])\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}H_{R}
,
where
H_{R}
is the Eilenberg-MacLane spectrum of
R
. It
is easy to check that
\pi_{i}(K([X/G],R))\cong\pi_{i}(K([X/G]))\otimes R
.
For an integer
n\geq 1
, we let
K([X/G],{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n)=K([X/G])\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}{{\mathbb{S}}}/n
,
where
{{\mathbb{S}}}/n
is the mod-
n
Moore spectrum.
We define
K^{\prime}([X/G],R)
and
K^{\prime}([X/G],{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n)
(resp.
KH([X/G],R)
and

KH([X/G],{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n)
in a similar way.

3.2.
Review of equivariant Hochschild homology

Recall that if
k
is a field, then a
k
-group
T

is called diagonalizable if it is of the form
D_{k}(M)={\rm Spec\,}(k[M])
, where
M
is a
finitely generated abelian group and
k[M]
is its group algebra.

T
is said to be of multiplicative type if

T_{k_{s}}
is diagonalizable, where
k_{s}
is a separable closure of
k
.
Recall from §
1
that a
k
-group
G
is
nice

if it is an extension of
a finite linearly reductive group scheme over
k
by a group scheme over
k
of
multiplicative type (cf.
[
50
, § 2]
). The category of nice group
schemes includes diagonalizable groups and finite constant groups whose orders are
invertible in
k
. We shall need the following result later on in this paper.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
H\hookrightarrow G
be a closed embedding of
k
-groups such that
G
is nice.
Then so is
H
.

Proof.

We consider the short exact sequence of
k
-groups

(3.3)

1\to G^{\prime}\to G\to G^{\prime\prime}\to 1,

where
G^{\prime}
is of multiplicative type and
G^{\prime\prime}
is a finite linearly reductive
group scheme over
k
. We let
H^{\prime\prime}={\rm Image}(H\to G^{\prime\prime})
and

H^{\prime}={\rm Ker}(H\to G^{\prime\prime})
. Then
H^{\prime\prime}
is a closed subgroup of
G^{\prime\prime}
by

[
12
, Chap. 1, Cor. 1.4]
and we have a short exact sequence of
k
-groups

1\to H^{\prime}\to H\to H^{\prime\prime}\to 1.

It suffices therefore to show that

H^{\prime}
is of multiplicative type and
H^{\prime\prime}
is finite linearly reductive.
That claim that
H^{\prime}
is of multiplicative type is elementary because
it is a closed subgroup of
G^{\prime}
. So is the claim that
H^{\prime\prime}
is finite.

To show that
H^{\prime\prime}
is linearly reductive,
we note that the map
BH^{\prime\prime}\to BG^{\prime\prime}
is a torsor for the homogeneous space

{G^{\prime\prime}}/{H^{\prime\prime}}
(cf.
[
12
, Chap. 2, Thm. 6.8]
). In particular,

BH^{\prime\prime}\to BG^{\prime\prime}
is étale locally finite, and hence finite by faithfully flat
descent. It follows that the functor
{\rm Qcoh}(BH^{\prime\prime})\to{\rm Qcoh}(BG^{\prime\prime})
is exact.
Since
{\rm Qcoh}(BG^{\prime\prime})\to{\rm Qcoh}({\rm pt})
is exact (by definition of linear
reductivity), it follows that the functor
{\rm Qcoh}(BH^{\prime\prime})\to{\rm Qcoh}({\rm pt})
is exact.
∎

We shall say that
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
acts on
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}_{k}
with nice stabilizers
if all stabilizer groups (the fibers of the group scheme
I_{X}\to X

in (
3.9
)) are nice.
For a (not necessarily quotient) stack
{\mathcal{X}}
, we let
D_{\rm qc}({\mathcal{X}})
denote the
unbounded derived category of the complexes of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
-modules with quasi-coherent
cohomology. Let
D({\mathcal{X}})
denote the unbounded derived category of quasi-coherent
sheaves on
{\mathcal{X}}
. Recall from
[
6
, § 1.1]
that a stack
{\mathcal{X}}
is called perfect
if it has affine diagonal,
D({\mathcal{X}})
is compactly generated, and an object of
D({\mathcal{X}})

is compact if and only if it is perfect. We shall use the following result about
perfectness of stacks.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
k
be a field and let
G
be a
k
-group. Let

X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
and let
{\mathcal{X}}=[X/G]
be the stack quotient. Then
{\mathcal{X}}
is perfect
if either of the following two conditions is satisfied.

(1)

{\rm char}(k)=0
and
X
is normal.

(2)

{\mathcal{X}}
has nice stabilizers.

Proof.

If (2) holds, then
[
50
, Prop. 3.5, Cor. 3.17]
together say that

D_{\rm qc}({\mathcal{X}})
is compactly generated (in particular,
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
is compact) and
an object of

D_{\rm qc}({\mathcal{X}})
is compact if and only if it is perfect. It follows now from

[
38
, Thm. 1.2]
that the canonical functor
D({\mathcal{X}})\to D_{\rm qc}({\mathcal{X}})
is an
equivalence. It follows that
{\mathcal{X}}
is perfect.
If (1) holds, then we apply
[
50
, Thm. 3.14]
,

[
63
, Prop. 2.7]
and
[
37
, Thm. B]
(see its corollary)
to derive the same conclusion.
∎

3.2.1.
Homology theory of mixed complexes

Let
k
be a commutative ring. Recall (cf.
[
52
]
) that a mixed complex of

k
-modules is a datum
{\mathcal{A}}=(A_{\bullet},d,B)
, where
A_{\bullet}
is
a chain complex of
k
-modules,

d\colon A_{n}\to A_{n-1}
(
n\geq 0
) is the differential of
A_{\bullet}
and

B\colon A\to A[-1]
is a map of complexes (called the Connes operator)
such that
dB+Bd=B^{2}=0
. The structure of a mixed complex on

(A_{\bullet},d)
is the same thing as the structure of a left module on it over
the dg algebra
\Lambda=k[B]/{(B^{2})}=(kB\xrightarrow{0}k)
,
where
B
is of chain degree
1
and
d(B)=0
.

We let
{{\mathcal{M}}}ix_{k}
denote the category of mixed complexes of
k
-modules and

{{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{M}}}ix_{k}
denote the localization of
{{\mathcal{M}}}ix_{k}
with respect to
quasi-isomorphisms (equivalently, the derived category of the dg algebra
\Lambda
),
where a morphism of mixed complexes is said to be a quasi-isomorphism if it is a
quasi-isomorphism of ordinary chain complexes after forgetting the Connes operator.
In this paper, we shall use homological notations for chain complexes.
For a chain complex
M_{\bullet}
in an abelian category,
M_{\bullet}[n]
will denote the
chain complex whose
i
-th term is
M_{i-n}
.

Let
{\rm Ch}_{k}
denote the dg category of unbounded chain complexes of
k
-modules
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{k}
its derived category.
We recall (cf.
[
52
]
) that for
{{\mathcal{A}}}=(A_{\bullet},d,B)\in{{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{M}}}ix_{k}
, the
Hochschild, cyclic, negative cyclic and periodic cyclic homology are
defined to be the following complexes in
{\mathcal{D}}_{k}
.

(1)

The Hochschild homology is the complex
HH({{\mathcal{A}}})=(A_{\bullet},d)
, the cyclic
homology is the complex
HC({{\mathcal{A}}})=k\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\Lambda}{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
the negative cyclic homology is the complex

HC^{-}({{\mathcal{A}}})={\bf{R}}{\rm Hom}_{\Lambda}(k,{{\mathcal{A}}})
.

(2)

The inclusion
kB\hookrightarrow\Lambda
is
\Lambda
-linear which induces a ‘norm’ map

N\colon HC({{\mathcal{A}}})[1]\to HC^{-}({{\mathcal{A}}})
. The homotopy cofiber of this map is the
periodic cyclic homology
HP({\mathcal{A}})
. We let

T\colon HC^{-}({{\mathcal{A}}})\to HP({\mathcal{A}})
denote the induced map.

These homology theories of mixed complexes
and the canonical maps between them define functors
from
{{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{M}}}ix_{k}
to
{\mathcal{D}}_{k}
and natural transformations between these functors.

Assume now that
k
is a field and
{\mathcal{C}}
is a
k
-linear symmetric
monoidal dg category. Recall from
[
54
, § 1.3]

(see also
[
18
, Defn. 2.2.18, Rem. 2.2.19]
) that the cyclic bar complex
H({\mathcal{C}})

is the sum-total complex of the bicomplex whose
n
th column (for
n\geq 0
) is the
direct sum of complexes

(3.4)

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}}(X_{n},X_{0})\otimes{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}}(X_{n-1},X_{n})\otimes{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}}(X_{n-2},X_{n-1})\otimes\cdots\otimes{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}}(X_{0},X_{1}),

where
X_{0},\ldots,X_{n}
range through the objects of
{\mathcal{C}}
and the tensor product
is taken in the category of chain complexes of
k
-modules. The differential
of this complex is induced by maps of complexes

d(f_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{0})=f_{n-1}\otimes\cdots\cdots\otimes f_{0}f_{n}+\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}(-1)^{i}f_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{i}f_{i-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{0}.

The cyclic permutations

(3.5)

t_{n}(f_{n-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{0})=(-1)^{n}f_{0}\otimes f_{n-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{1}

endow
H({\mathcal{C}})
with the structure of a cyclic complex in the category of
k
-vector
spaces (cf.
[
67
, § 6.1]
) which in turn makes it a mixed complex

(H({\mathcal{C}}),d,B)
(cf.
[
55
, § 5.3]
). The Hochschild
(resp. cyclic, negative cyclic, periodic cyclic) homology of
{\mathcal{C}}
is defined to be the
corresponding homology of the mixed complex
(H({\mathcal{C}}),d,B)
. The monoidal
structure on
{\mathcal{C}}
uniquely defines a monoidal structure on
HH({\mathcal{C}})
. Since the
other homology theories are obtained from
HH({\mathcal{C}})
using monoidal maps,
we see that all these homology theories of
{\mathcal{C}}
are canonically endowed with
monoidal structures.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{C}}
is compactly generated
monoidal dg category with the subcategory of
compact objects
{\mathcal{C}}_{c}\subset{\mathcal{C}}
such that
{\mathcal{C}}
is the ind-completion of
{\mathcal{C}}_{c}
.
Under this hypothesis,
{\mathcal{C}}
is a dualizable object in the category
{\rm DGCat}_{k}
,
i.e., there are canonical dg functors

(3.6)

{\rm Ch}_{k}\xrightarrow{{\rm coev}}{\mathcal{C}}\otimes{\mathcal{C}}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{{\rm ev}}{\rm Ch}_{k}

which satisfy the standard properties of 1-dimensional cobordisms.
Furthermore, there is a canonical weak equivalence (cf.
[
18
, § 2.2]
)

(3.7)

\alpha_{{\mathcal{C}}}\colon HH({{\mathcal{C}}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}({\rm ev}\circ{\rm coev})(k).

We let
{\rm{\bf{dgm}}}_{k}
denote the category of all

k
-linear symmetric monoidal dg-categories (together with dg-functors) modulo Morita
equivalence (cf.
[
55
, § 3.8]
). As shown in § 5.2 of op. cit. and

[
10
, § 7,9]
, the Waldhausen-Bass construction defines a functor

K\colon{\rm{\bf{dgm}}}_{k}\to{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}
such that
K({\rm Perf}_{{\mathcal{X}}})

(cf. §
3.2.2
) coincides with

K({\mathcal{X}})
. The cyclic bar complex defines a functor

{\rm DGCat}_{k}\to{{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{M}}}ix_{k}
. By
[
55
, Thm. 5.2]
, this functor
factors through
{\rm{\bf{dgm}}}_{k}
. Composing this with the Eilenberg-MacLane
construction associated to any of the homology functors
defined above on
{\rm DGCat}_{k}
,
we get the following result due to Blumberg-Gepner-Tabuada
[
11
]
.
We let
{\mathcal{S}}(k)
denote the set
\{K,KH,HH,HC^{-},HC,HP\}
of functors on

{\rm{\bf{dgm}}}_{k}
.

Theorem 3.3
.

For any
F\in{\mathcal{S}}(k)
, we have the following.

(1)

The functor

F\colon{\rm{\bf{dgm}}}_{k}\to{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}

has the property that
F({\mathcal{C}})
is a ring spectrum for
{\mathcal{C}}\in{\rm{\bf{dgm}}}_{k}
.

(2)

There are natural transformations
{\rm tr}\colon K\to HH
and
{\rm tr_{cyc}}\colon K\to HC^{-}
such that
{\rm tr}=\iota\circ{\rm tr_{cyc}}
, where

\iota\colon HC^{-}\to HH
is the canonical natural transformation.

(3)

For every
{\mathcal{C}}\in{\rm{\bf{dgm}}}_{k}
, all arrows in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{K({\mathcal{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rm tr_{cyc}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm tr}}
\textstyle{HC^{-}({\mathcal{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{HH({\mathcal{C}})}

are morphisms of ring spectra.

(4)

For every
{\mathcal{C}}\in{\rm{\bf{dgm}}}_{k}
, the canonical maps

HH({\mathcal{C}})\to HC({\mathcal{C}})
and
HC^{-}({\mathcal{C}})\xrightarrow{T}HP({\mathcal{C}})

are morphisms of ring spectra.

Remark 3.4
.

The authors of
[
11
]
study the multiplicativity of the trace maps from

K
-theory to the topological counterparts of our homology theories (i.e.,
topological Hochschild homology et al.). However,
this does not cause a problem because the homology theories we defined above receive
canonical multipticative maps from their topological counterparts.

3.2.2.
Homology theory of stacks

We now specialize to dg categories arising out of quasi-coherent sheaves on
quotient stacks. For this, we assume that
k
is a field of characteristic zero.
Let
G
be a
k
-group and let
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
. We
let
p\colon X\to{\mathcal{X}}=[X/G]
denote the stack quotient map.
Recall (cf.
[
50
, § 3A]
,
[
53
, § 3.2]
) that

{\rm Perf}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
has the canonical structure of a dg-category
whose homotopy category is the derived category
{\mathcal{D}}_{\rm Perf}({\mathcal{X}})
of
{\rm Perf}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
.
Let
{\rm Perf}^{\rm ac}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
denote the full subcategory of
{\rm Perf}_{{\mathcal{X}}}

consisting of acyclic perfect complexes on
{{\mathcal{X}}}
. Then

({\rm Perf}_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\rm Perf}^{\rm ac}_{{\mathcal{X}}})
is a localizing pair of
k
-linear dg categories
(cf.
[
55
, § 2.2, 4.4, 4.6]
). We let
{\rm Perf}_{dg}({\mathcal{X}})
denote the
dg quotient of the inclusion
{\rm Perf}^{\rm ac}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\hookrightarrow{\rm Perf}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
.

We assume that
{\mathcal{X}}
is a perfect stack so that
D({\mathcal{X}})
is compactly generated and
its compact objects are same as the perfect complexes.
The Hochschild (resp. cyclic, negative cyclic, periodic cyclic) homology of
{\mathcal{X}}

is defined to be the Hochschild (resp. cyclic, negative cyclic, periodic cyclic)
homology of the
k
-linear dg category
{\rm Perf}_{dg}({\mathcal{X}})
and is denoted by
HH({\mathcal{X}})

(resp.
HC({\mathcal{X}})
,
HC^{-}({\mathcal{X}})
,
HP({\mathcal{X}})
). We shall also write

HH({\mathcal{X}})
(resp.
HC({\mathcal{X}})
,
HC^{-}({\mathcal{X}})
,
HP({\mathcal{X}})
) as
HH_{G}(X)

(resp.
HC_{G}(X)
,
HC^{-}_{G}(X)
,
HP_{G}(X)
). We shall call these
‘the homology theories’ of
{\mathcal{X}}
(equivalently, equivariant homology theories of
X
).

Since the pull-back
f^{*}\colon H({\rm Perf}_{dg}({\mathcal{X}}))\to H({\rm Perf}_{dg}({\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}))
of
a homology theory,
induced by a morphism of quotient stacks
f\colon{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{X}}
, clearly commutes
with the cyclic permutations, we see that
f^{*}
is
\Lambda
-linear
(i.e., equivariant for the
B
-action). In particular, all homology theories are
contravariant functorial on the category of perfect stacks.
By a homology theory of stacks, we shall mean any of the functors

HH,HC,HC^{-}
and
HP
on the category of stacks.
We shall assume these functors to be taking values in
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}
via the
Eilenberg-MacLane functor.

We recall that a derived prestack over
k
is an
\infty
-functor from

{\bf{Aff}}_{k}
to the
\infty
-category of simplicial sets
(equivalently, weakly compact Hausdorff topological spaces)
{\bf{S}}
, where

{\bf{Aff}}_{k}
is the opposite category of the
\infty
-category of derived commutative

k
-algebras (equivalently, simplicial commutative
k
-algebras or commutative
differential non-negatively graded
k
-algebras).
A derived stack over
k
is a derived prestack which
is a sheaf for the derived étale topology on
{\bf{Aff}}_{k}

(cf.
[
91
]
). We let
{\rm DSt}_{k}
denote the
\infty
-category of derived
stacks over
k
.

An ordinary stack over
k
is canonically an object of
{\rm DSt}_{k}
and we
shall treat it in this way. We shall consider a simplicial set as a locally constant
sheaf on
{\bf{Aff}}_{k}
(in particular, an object of
{\rm DSt}_{k}
). One example of this
which we shall use is the standard circle
S^{1}
, the homotopy push-out of the diagram

{\rm pt}\leftarrow{\rm pt}\coprod{\rm pt}\to{\rm pt}
. All functors between derived stacks and
between
\infty
-categories of sheaves on them will be derived. A commutative
diagram in an
\infty
-category will mean a diagram which commutes up to a
coherent homotopy.

For the quotient stack
{\mathcal{X}}=[X/G]
as above, the loop space
{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}
is defined to
be the derived mapping stack
{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}={{{\mathcal{M}}}ap}_{{\rm DSt}_{k}}(S^{1},{\mathcal{X}})
. Using that

S^{1}={\rm pt}{\underset{({\rm pt}\coprod{\rm pt})}{\coprod^{h}}}{\rm pt}
and

{{{\mathcal{M}}}ap}_{{\rm DSt}_{k}}(-,{\mathcal{X}})
takes
homotopy colimits into homotopy limits in
{\rm DSt}_{k}
, we get that

(3.8)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{X}}}

is a homotopy Cartesian diagram in
{\rm DSt}_{k}
, where
\Delta
