is the diagonal map.
The constant loop on
{\mathcal{X}}
defines a unique morphism

s\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}
such that
\pi\circ s={\operatorname{id}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
. This is the identity
element of
{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}
when the latter is considered as a derived group stack over
{\mathcal{X}}

via
\pi
.

It follows from the definition of
{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}
that

\pi_{0}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}})={\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}=[{I_{X}}/{G}]
is the ordinary inertia stack
of
{\mathcal{X}}
. Recall here that
I_{X}
is the ordinary fiber product

(3.9)

\textstyle{I_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{X}}
\textstyle{G\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X}}
\textstyle{X\times X,}

where
\phi_{X}(g,x)=(gx,x)
is the action map and
\Delta_{X}
is the diagonal.
All maps in this diagram are
G
-equivariant if we let

G
act on
G\times X
by
g^{\prime}(g,x)=(g^{\prime}gg^{\prime-1},g^{\prime}x)
and on
X\times X

diagonally. The map
t
gives a
G
-equivariant closed embedding of
I_{X}
into

G\times X
. On geometric points, this embedding is given by

(3.10)

I_{X}=\{(g,x)|gx=x\}\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ g^{\prime}\cdot(g,x)=(g^{\prime}gg^{\prime-1},g^{\prime}x).

We now recall that
S^{1}
is a group stack under the operation of loop rotations
and it acts on
{{\mathcal{M}}}ap_{{\rm DSt}_{k}}(S^{1},{\mathcal{X}})
by left multiplication on the source of
all maps.
It follows that
{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}
is equipped with a canonical
S^{1}
-action which
is natural with respect to maps of stacks.
This implies that
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}})
is canonically a comodule over the coalgebra

{\mathcal{O}}(S^{1})
. Since the topological group
S^{1}
is the geometric realization of the dual
algebra of
{\mathcal{O}}(S^{1})
, it follows that
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}})
is endowed with a canonical

S^{1}
-action (cf.
[
7
, § 3.4]
). We also note that

(3.11)

{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}})=\Gamma({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}})\simeq\Gamma({\mathcal{X}},\pi^{\prime}_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}}))\simeq\Gamma({\mathcal{X}},\pi^{\prime}_{*}\pi^{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}))

\simeq\Gamma({\mathcal{X}},\Delta^{*}\Delta_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}))\simeq{\rm pr}_{*}\Delta^{*}\Delta_{*}{\rm pr}^{*}(k),

where
{\rm pr}\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to{\rm pt}
is the projection (cf.
[
56
, Rem. 1.11]
).

On the other hand, letting
{\mathcal{C}}
be the dg category
{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{X}})

of unbounded chain complexes
of quasi-coherent sheaves in (
3.7
) so that

{\mathcal{C}}_{c}={\rm Perf}_{dg}({\mathcal{X}})
, one knows that
{\mathcal{C}}\simeq{\mathcal{C}}^{\vee}
and

{\mathcal{C}}\otimes{\mathcal{C}}^{\vee}\simeq{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{X}})

(cf.
[
6
, Thm. 4.7, Cor. 4.8]
).
Moreover,
one has
{\rm ev}={\rm pr}_{*}\Delta^{*}
and
{\rm coev}=\Delta_{*}{\rm pr}^{*}
so that
(
3.7
) yields a canonical weak equivalence
(cf.
[
18
, § 2.2, Exm. 2.2.20]
,
[
39
, Prop. 4.1]
)

(3.12)

\alpha_{{\mathcal{X}}}\colon HH({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}).

This map is natural in
{\mathcal{X}}
and
is equivariant with respect to the action of the Connes operator
B

(equivalently, the
\Lambda
-module structure) on
HH({\mathcal{X}})

and the
S^{1}
-action on
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}})
(cf.
[
8
, Rem. 4.2]
).

We remark that the
B
-action on
HH({\mathcal{X}})
is in fact equivalent to the action of the
simplicial group
B{\mathbb{Z}}
. Taking the geometric realizations, one finds that the

B
-action yields a canonical
S^{1}
-action on
HH({\mathcal{X}})
and
\alpha_{\mathcal{X}}
is then
an
S^{1}
-equivariant map between (the Eilenberg-MacLane) spectra
(cf.
[
48
]
). In the sequel, we shall interpret the

S^{1}
-action on
HH({\mathcal{X}})
in this term. Under this identification, we get

(3.13)

HC({\mathcal{X}})=HH({\mathcal{X}})_{hS^{1}},\ HC^{-}({\mathcal{X}})=HH({\mathcal{X}})^{hS^{1}}\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ HP({\mathcal{X}})=HH({\mathcal{X}})^{h{\mathbb{T}}},

which are respectively, the homotopy orbit, homotopy fixed point and homotopy
Tate construction spectra for the
S^{1}
-action on
HH({\mathcal{X}})
.

4.
A decomposition theorem

The goal of this section is to prove a decomposition theorem for the
equivariant
K
-theory and equivariant homology theories.
Apart from being of independent interest, this theorem is the first technical step in
the proof of the completion theorems.

We fix a field
k
. We also fix
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
and
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
.
Let
\pi\colon X\to[X/G]
be the quotient map. Recall that
the center of
G
is the unique closed subgroup
Z(G)
having the property that

Z(G)(A)
is the center of the group
G(A)
for all
k
-algebras
A

(cf.
[
12
, Prop. I.1.3, I.1.7]
,
[
21
, Tag 0BF6, Lem. 39.8.8]
). Let

P\subset Z(G)
be a diagonalizable closed subgroup and let
\widehat{P}
be its
character group so that
P={\rm Spec\,}(k[\widehat{P}])
.
We shall write the group operation of
\widehat{P}
additively, i.e.,

(\chi+\chi^{\prime})(x)=\chi(x)\chi^{\prime}(x)\in{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
, where the latter is the
rank one split torus over
k
. We assume that
P
acts (via its inclusion in
G
)
trivially on
X
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\widehat{P}]
be the quasi-coherent sheaf of Hopf algebras over
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
.
Since
P
acts trivially on
X
, a
P
-equivariant quasi-coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
on
X

is the same thing as a quasi-coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
on
X
together with a
homomorphism
\alpha^{P}_{{\mathcal{F}}}\colon P\to{\rm Aut}_{X}({\mathcal{F}})
between group stacks
over
k
(cf.
[
21
, Tag 08JS, Lem. 99.3.2]
).
Equivalently, a
P
-equivariant quasi-coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
on
X

is a quasi-coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
on
X
endowed with a unique structure of a comodule
over the sheaf of Hopf algebras
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\widehat{P}]
which
is compatible with the
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module structure of
{\mathcal{F}}
.
This structure of a sheaf of comodules over the sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\widehat{P}]

of Hopf algebras is equivalent
to a map of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules
\rho\colon{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\widehat{P}]\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{F}}

satisfying the usual conditions (cf.
[
62
, § 2A1]
).
For an element
\chi\in\widehat{P}
, we let
\rho_{\chi}\colon{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\widehat{P}]\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{F}}
be defined by
\rho_{\chi}(f)=\chi\otimes f
, where
U\to X
is étale and
f\in{\mathcal{F}}(U)
.
We let
{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi}={\rm Ker}(\rho-\rho_{\chi})
.

We next note that if we let
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
act trivially on
X
, then the scalar multiplication
induced by the
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module structure on
{\mathcal{F}}
defines a unique
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
-action
on
{\mathcal{F}}
given by the homomorphism of group stacks

{\operatorname{\rm can}}_{{\mathcal{F}}}\colon{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\to{\rm Aut}_{X}({\mathcal{F}})
over
k
.
For each character
\chi\colon P\to{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
, we let
{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}
be the
full subcategory of
{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X}
whose objects are given by

(4.1)

{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{C}^{\chi}_{X})=\{\mathcal{F}\in{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X}|\alpha^{P}_{{\mathcal{F}}}={\operatorname{\rm can}}_{{\mathcal{F}}}\circ\chi\}=\{\mathcal{F}\in{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X}|{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}_{\chi}\}.

We let
{\mathcal{D}}^{\chi}_{X}
be the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}
consisting of objects
which are
G
-equivariant coherent sheaves on
X
.
Recall that the coproduct
\coprod_{\lambda\in I}{\mathcal{C}}_{\lambda}
of a family

\{{\mathcal{C}}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in I}
of
categories is the filtering inductive limit of products of finite subfamilies of
I

(cf.
[
74
, § 1, p. 84]
).

Lemma 4.1
.

We have the following.

(1)

For each
{\mathcal{F}}\in{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X}
and
\chi\in\widehat{P}
,
the subsheaf
{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi}
is quasi-coherent and
G
-equivariant.

(2)

The natural map
{\underset{\chi\in\widehat{P}}{\bigoplus}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi}\to{\mathcal{F}}

is an isomorphism in
{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X}
.

(3)

For each
{\mathcal{F}}\in{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X}
and
\chi\in\widehat{P}
, the subsheaf
{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi}

is coherent (resp. locally free) if
{\mathcal{F}}
is so.

(4)

\mathcal{C}^{\chi}_{X}
is a full abelian subcategory of
{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X}
.

(5)

The natural inclusions
{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}\hookrightarrow{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X}

induce equivalences of categories

{\underset{\chi\in\widehat{P}}{\prod}}{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{{\rm Qcoh}}_{[X/G]};\ \ {\underset{\chi\in\widehat{P}}{\coprod}}{\mathcal{D}}^{\chi}_{X}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{{\rm Coh}}^{0}_{[X/G]}.

(6)

If
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is a morphism in
{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
with
P
acting trivially on
X^{\prime}

and
\chi\in\widehat{P}
, then

f^{*}({\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X})\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X^{\prime}}
and
f_{*}({\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X^{\prime}})\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}
.

(7)

For each pair
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\widehat{P}
, the tensor product in
{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X}
defines a
functor

{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}\times{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi^{\prime}}_{X}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi+\chi^{\prime}}_{X}.

Proof.

Except for (3), the proof of items (1) to (5) is a trivial extension of that of

[
64
, Lem. 4.1]
to quasi-coherent sheaves, where one has to use

[
23
, Exp. I, Prop. 4.7.3]

instead of
[
85
, Lem. 5.6]
in the proofs of (2) and (5).
The item (3) follows from (2). To prove the first part of item (6), we can
reduce to the case when both
X
and
X^{\prime}
are affine in which case the claim
is obvious. The second part of (6) is clear from the definitions.
The item (7) is a direct verification.
∎

By Lemma
4.1
(5), we get projection functors

{\rm pr}_{\chi}\colon{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}
such that the composition

{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}\hookrightarrow{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X}\xrightarrow{{\rm pr}_{\chi}}{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}
is isomorphic to the
identity functor. These functors commute with
f^{*}
and
f_{*}
.

We let
{\rm Perf}^{c}_{[X/G]}
be the full subcategory of
{\rm Perf}_{[X/G]}
whose objects
are strictly bounded perfect complexes
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
such that
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
is a

G
-equivariant coherent sheaf on
X
for each
n
. For
\chi\in\widehat{P}
,
we let
{\rm Perf}^{c,\chi}_{[X/G]}
be the full subcategory of
{\rm Perf}^{c}_{[X/G]}
whose objects
are the chain complexes
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
such that
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{\chi}_{X}
for
every
n
. We let
{\rm Coh}^{\chi}_{[X/G]}
be the full subcategory of
{\rm Coh}_{[X/G]}

whose objects are the strictly bounded chain complexes
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
such that

{\mathcal{F}}_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{\chi}_{X}
for every
n
.
It is clear that
{\rm Perf}^{c}_{[X/G]}
and
{\rm Perf}^{c,\chi}_{[X/G]}
are
k
-linear
differential graded full subcategories of
{\rm Perf}_{[X/G]}
with respect to the latter’s
cofibrations and weak equivalences.
Similarly,
{\rm Coh}^{\chi}_{[X/G]}
is a differential graded
full subcategory of
{\rm Coh}_{[X/G]}
. We let
K^{\chi}([X/G])
(resp.
K^{\prime\chi}([X/G])
)
denote the non-connective
K
-theory spectrum of
{\rm Perf}^{c,\chi}_{[X/G]}

(resp.
{\rm Coh}^{\chi}_{[X/G]}
) as defined in
[
10
]
and
[
77
]
.
We similarly define
F^{\chi}([X/G])
for
F\in\{HH,HC^{-},HC,HP\}
following

[
55
]
.

Lemma
4.1
implies that for

{\mathcal{F}}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}
and
{\mathcal{G}}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi^{\prime}}_{X}
, one has

{\rm Hom}_{[X/G]}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}})=0
unless
\chi=\chi^{\prime}
. It follows that every
map
\alpha\colon{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{G}}
in
{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X}
decomposes
into direct sum of maps
\alpha_{\chi}\colon{\mathcal{F}}_{\chi}\to{\mathcal{G}}_{\chi}
where
\chi
runs
through
\widehat{P}
. We deduce from Lemma
4.1
(5) that

{\underset{\chi\in\widehat{P}}{\coprod}}{\rm Perf}^{c,\chi}_{[X/G]}\to{\rm Perf}^{c}_{[X/G]}

and
{\underset{\chi\in\widehat{P}}{\coprod}}{\rm Coh}^{\chi}_{[X/G]}\to{\rm Coh}_{[X/G]}
are
equivalences of dg categories. We denote the induced maps between their
non-connective
K
-theory spectra by
\Phi^{P}_{[X/G]}
and
\Psi^{P}_{[X/G]}
,
respectively. Recall that a stack
{\mathcal{Y}}
is said to have the
resolution property
if every coherent sheaf on
{\mathcal{Y}}
is a quotient of a locally free coherent sheaf.
Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a morphism in
{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
such that

P
acts trivially on
X^{\prime}
.

We let
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(k)={\mathcal{S}}(k)\cup\{K^{\prime}\}
, considered as functors on the
category of quotient stacks over
k
. For
F\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(k)
and
\chi\in\widehat{P}
,
we let
F^{\chi}([X/G])=F({\rm Perf}^{\chi}_{[X/G]})
.
For
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
F\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(k)
, we let
F_{i}(-)=(\pi_{i}\circ F)(-)
on

{\rm DGCat}_{k}
.

Proposition 4.2
.

We have the following.

(1)

The canonical maps of spectra

\hskip 28.45274pt\Phi^{P}_{[X/G]}\colon\coprod_{\chi\in\widehat{P}}K^{\chi}([X/G])\to K([X/G])\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \Psi^{P}_{[X/G]}\colon\coprod_{\chi\in\widehat{P}}K^{\prime\chi}([X/G])\to K^{\prime}([X/G])

are weak equivalences.

(2)

If
f
is proper, then the push-forward map on equivariant
K^{\prime}
-theory
induces a map
f_{*}\colon K^{\prime\chi}([X^{\prime}/G])\to K^{\prime\chi}([X/G])
for each
\chi\in\widehat{P}
such that
f_{*}\circ\Psi^{P}_{[X^{\prime}/G]}
is homotopic to

\Psi^{P}_{[X/G]}\circ f_{*}
.

(3)

If
f
is flat, then the pull-back map on equivariant
K^{\prime}
-theory
induces a map
f^{*}\colon K^{\prime\chi}([X/G])\to K^{\prime\chi}([X^{\prime}/G])
for each
\chi\in\widehat{P}
such that
f^{*}\circ\Psi^{P}_{[X/G]}
is homotopic to

\Psi^{P}_{[X^{\prime}/G]}\circ f^{*}
.

(4)

The pull-back
map on equivariant
K
-theory induces
f^{*}\colon K^{\chi}([X/G])\to K^{\chi}([X^{\prime}/G])
for each
\chi\in\widehat{P}
such that
f^{*}\circ\Phi^{P}_{[X/G]}
is homotopic to
\Phi^{P}_{[X^{\prime}/G]}\circ f^{*}
.

(5)

If
f
is a proper lci (local complete intersection) morphism, then the
push-forward map on equivariant

K
-theory induces
f_{*}\colon K^{\chi}([X^{\prime}/G])\to K^{\chi}([X/G])
for each

\chi\in\widehat{P}
such that
f_{*}\circ\Phi^{P}_{[X^{\prime}/G]}
is homotopic to

\Phi^{P}_{[X/G]}\circ f_{*}
.

(6)

For any
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\widehat{P}
, the tensor
product of equivariant quasi-coherent sheaves and the pull-back

f^{*}\colon K^{\chi}([X/G])\to K^{\chi}([{X^{\prime}}/G])
together induce maps of spectra

\hskip 28.45274ptK^{\chi}([X/G])\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}K^{\chi^{\prime}}([{X^{\prime}}/G])\to K^{\chi+\chi^{\prime}}([{X^{\prime}}/G])\ \ \mbox{and}

K^{\chi}([X/G])\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}K^{\prime\chi^{\prime}}([{X^{\prime}}/G])\to K^{\prime\chi+\chi^{\prime}}([{X^{\prime}}/G]).

Proof.

Using the decomposition
{\underset{\chi\in\widehat{P}}{\coprod}}{\rm Perf}^{c,\chi}_{[X/G]}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\rm Perf}^{c}_{[X/G]}
and

[
74
, § 1, (4), § 2, (8), (9)]
,
it follows that the canonical map of spectra

\coprod_{\chi\in\widehat{P}}K^{\chi}([X/G])\to K({\rm Perf}^{c}_{[X/G]})
is a weak equivalence.
To prove that
\Phi^{P}_{[X/G]}
is a weak equivalence, it remains therefore to show
that the inclusion functor
{\rm Perf}^{c}_{[X/G]}\to{\rm Perf}_{[X/G]}
induces a weak
equivalence between their
K
-theory spectra.
To show the latter claim, we let
{\rm Perf}^{qc}_{[X/G]}
be the full
subcategory of
{\rm Perf}_{[X/G]}
whose objects
are perfect complexes
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
such that
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
is a

G
-equivariant quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
for each
n
.
We let
{\rm Perf}^{c^{\prime}}_{[X/G]}
be the full
subcategory of
{\rm Perf}_{[X/G]}
whose objects
are perfect complexes
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
such that
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
is a

G
-equivariant coherent sheaf on
X
for each
n
.

We look at the inclusions of dg categories

(4.2)

{\rm Perf}^{c}_{[X/G]}\hookrightarrow{\rm Perf}^{c^{\prime}}_{[X/G]}\hookrightarrow{\rm Perf}^{qc}_{[X/G]}\hookrightarrow{\rm Perf}_{[X/G]}.

It is straightforward to check that the first inclusion induces an equivalence
of derived categories. In particular, it induces a weak equivalence of

K
-theory spectra (cf.
[
77
, § 12.3, Prop. 3]
).
It follows from
[
63
, Lem. 3.1]

that the map of spectra
K({\rm Perf}^{qc}_{[X/G]})\to K({\rm Perf}_{[X/G]})=K([X/G])
is a weak
equivalence.

To show that
K({\rm Perf}^{c^{\prime}}_{[X/G]})\to K({\rm Perf}^{qc}_{[X/G]})
is
a weak equivalence, we let
\phi\colon{\mathcal{F}}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
be a surjective map of

G
-equivariant quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
such that
{\mathcal{G}}
is coherent. By

[
88
, Lem. 1.4]
,
{\mathcal{F}}
is the filtered colimit of its
G
-equivariant
coherent subsheaves. Since
X
is Noetherian, one of these coherent subsheaves
(say,
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
) will surject onto
{\mathcal{G}}
. We thus get a
G
-equivariant coherent
subsheaf
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{F}}
such that
\phi
restricts to a surjection

\phi\colon{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
. We now apply
[
90
, Lem. 1.9.5]

with
{\mathcal{A}}={{\rm Qcoh}}_{[X/G]}
,
{\mathcal{D}}={\rm Coh}^{0}_{[X/G]}
,

{\mathcal{C}}=
the category of cohomologically bounded complexes
in
{\mathcal{A}}
and
D=0
. It follows that the inclusion

{\rm Perf}^{c^{\prime}}_{[X/G]}\hookrightarrow{\rm Perf}^{qc}_{[X/G]}

induces an equivalence of their derived categories. We conclude as above
(cf.
[
90
, Thm. 1.9.8]
) that this inclusion induces a weak equivalence

(4.3)

K({\rm Perf}^{c^{\prime}}_{[X/G]})\xrightarrow{\simeq}K({\rm Perf}^{qc}_{[X/G]}).

This concludes the proof of the claim that
\Phi^{P}_{[X/G]}
is a weak equivalence.

An identical argument shows that the natural map of spectra

(4.4)

K({\rm Coh}_{[X/G]})\to K^{\prime}([X/G])

is a weak equivalence and this easily implies as above
that
\Psi^{P}_{[X/G]}
is a weak equivalence. This proves item (1).
The item (3) follows directly from Lemma
4.1
(6) using
(
4.4
).

To prove (4), we let
{\rm Perf}^{f}_{[X/G]}\subset{\rm Perf}^{qc}_{[X/G]}
be the full subcategory
consisting of bounded above perfect complexes
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
such that each
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}

is a quasi-coherent flat
{\mathcal{O}}_{[X/G]}
-module. We let
{\rm Perf}^{f,\chi}_{[X/G]}\subset{\rm Perf}^{f}_{[X/G]}
be the full subcategory whose objects
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
have the property
that each
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
lies in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}
. We let
{\rm Perf}^{{qc},\chi}_{[X/G]}\subset{\rm Perf}^{qc}_{[X/G]}
be the full subcategory whose objects
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
have the property
that each
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
lies in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}
. We then have a commutative diagram
of inclusions of dg categories

(4.5)

\textstyle{{\rm Perf}^{c,\chi}_{[X/G]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Perf}^{{qc},\chi}_{[X/G]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Perf}^{f,\chi}_{[X/G]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Perf}^{c}_{[X/G]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Perf}^{qc}_{[X/G]}}
\textstyle{{\rm Perf}^{f}_{[X/G]}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

We showed in the proof of item (1) that the left horizontal arrow on the bottom row
induces a weak equivalence between the
K
-theory spectra. Using this and
Lemma
4.1
, it follows that the left horizontal arrow on the
top row also induces a weak equivalence between the
K
-theory spectra.
It follows from
[
35
, Thm. 3.5.5]
that
the right horizontal arrow on the bottom row induces an equivalence of the derived
categories and hence a weak equivalence of the
K
-theory spectra.
An application of Lemma
4.1
shows again that
right horizontal arrow on the top row also induces a weak equivalence of the

K
-theory spectra. It suffices therefore to show that
f^{*}
takes

{\rm Perf}^{f}_{[X/G]}
to
{\rm Perf}^{qc}_{[X^{\prime}/G]}
such that
f^{*}({\rm Perf}^{f,\chi}_{[X/G]})\subset{\rm Perf}^{\chi}_{[{X^{\prime}}/G]}
. But this is immediate from Lemma
4.1
(6).
This proves item (4).

To prove (5), we let
{\rm Perf}^{i}_{[X/G]}\subset{\rm Perf}^{qc}_{[X/G]}
be the full subcategory
consisting of bounded below perfect complexes
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
such that each
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}

is a quasi-coherent injective
{\mathcal{O}}_{[X/G]}
-module. We let

{\rm Perf}^{i,\chi}_{[X/G]}\subset{\rm Perf}^{i}_{[X/G]}
be the full subcategory whose objects
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
have the property
that each
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
lies in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}
. We then have a commutative diagram
of inclusions of dg categories

(4.6)

\textstyle{{\rm Perf}^{c,\chi}_{[X^{\prime}/G]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Perf}^{{qc},\chi}_{[X^{\prime}/G]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Perf}^{i,\chi}_{[X^{\prime}/G]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Perf}^{c}_{[X^{\prime}/G]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Perf}^{qc}_{[X^{\prime}/G]}}
\textstyle{{\rm Perf}^{i}_{[X^{\prime}/G]}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Since
{{\rm Qcoh}}^{G}_{X^{\prime}}
has enough injectives, we see that the right horizontal arrow
on the bottom row induces an equivalence of the derived categories and hence a
weak equivalence of the
K
-theory spectra.
It follows as before that the right horizontal arrow
on the top row also induces a weak equivalence of the
K
-theory spectra.
We observed above that
the left horizontal arrows on both rows induce weak equivalences of
K
-theory
spectra. On the other hand, for any
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
in
{\rm Perf}^{i}_{[X^{\prime}/G]}
,
the complex
f_{*}({\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet})
lies in

{\rm Perf}^{i}_{[X/G]}
since
f
is a proper and lci morphism. Furthermore,
Lemma
4.1
(6) implies that
f_{*}({\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet})
lies in

{\rm Perf}^{i,\chi}_{[X/G]}
if
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
lies in
{\rm Perf}^{i,\chi}_{[X^{\prime}/G]}
.
This implies item (5). The proof of item (2) is similar to that of (5).

Finally, to prove (6), we can work with
{\rm Perf}^{f,\chi}_{[X/G]}
instead of

{\rm Perf}^{\chi}_{[X/G]}
as shown in the proof of item (4).
The desired claim then follows immediately by combining items
(6) and (7) of Lemma
4.1
(cf.
[
90
, § 3.15]
).
∎

Corollary 4.3
.

For
F\in\{HH,HC\}
, there is a natural weak equivalence of spectra

\kappa^{P}_{[X/G]}\colon\coprod_{\chi\in\widehat{P}}F^{\chi}([X/G])\xrightarrow{\simeq}F([X/G])

such that item (4) of Proposition
4.2
holds after substituting

F
for
K
and the first part of item (6) holds after substituting
F
for
K
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to that of Proposition
4.2
. Using that the
functors
HH
and
HC
are continuous (i.e., preserve filtered homotopy colimits),
the only thing one needs to
observe is that the canonical functors
{\rm Perf}^{c}_{[X/G]}\hookrightarrow{\rm Perf}^{qc}_{[X/G]}\hookrightarrow{\rm Perf}_{[X/G]}
induce weak equivalences between the homology functors. This is
because we showed in the proof of Proposition
4.2
that
they have equivalent derived categories. We can therefore apply
Theorem
3.3
which says that each of the homology functors
factors through Morita equivalence, and hence through derived equivalence
(cf.
[
55
, § 4.2]
).
∎

By Proposition
4.2
(4), the assignment
X\mapsto K^{\chi}([X/G])
is
a functor from
({\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k})^{\rm op}
to
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}
for every
\chi\in\widehat{P}
.
Letting
G
act trivially on each
\Delta^{n}
(cf. (
3.1
))
and noting that
[X/G]\times\Delta^{n}\cong[(X\times\Delta^{n})/G]
,
it follows that
n\mapsto K^{\chi}([(X\times\Delta^{n})/G])
is a simplicial
diagram in
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}
. Taking the homotopy colimit and using
Proposition
4.2
(1), we get a weak equivalence

\theta^{P}_{[X/G]}\colon\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{\Delta^{n}}(\coprod_{\chi\in\widehat{P}}K^{\chi}([(X\times\Delta^{n})/G]))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{\Delta^{n}}K([X/G]\times\Delta^{n})
.
We let
KH^{\chi}([X/G])=\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{\Delta^{n}}K^{\chi}([(X\times\Delta^{n})/G])
.
Since homotopy colimits commute with coproducts, we get the following.

Corollary 4.4
.

There is a natural weak equivalence of spectra

\theta^{P}_{[X/G]}\colon\coprod_{\chi\in\widehat{P}}KH^{\chi}([X/G])\xrightarrow{\simeq}KH([X/G])

such that items (4), (5) and (6) of Proposition
4.2
hold.

As a special case of Proposition
4.2
, assume that
G
is a diagonalizable

k
-group and it acts trivially on
X
. Then we have the canonical map of
spectra
K(X)\to K([X/G])
, induced by the functor
{\rm Perf}(X)\to{\rm Perf}([X/G])

which endows a sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules with the trivial action of
G
.
In particular, we get a natural map

K_{i}(X)\otimes R(G)\to K_{i}([X/G])
. A similar map exists between the
K^{\prime}
-groups.
The second isomorphism in the following corollary was proven by
Thomason
[
85
, Lem. 5.6]
when
X
is affine.

Corollary 4.5
.

The canonical maps

K_{i}(X)\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}[\widehat{P}]\to K_{i}([X/G]);\ K^{\prime}_{i}(X)\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}[\widehat{P}]\to K^{\prime}_{i}([X/G]);

KH_{i}(X)\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}[\widehat{P}]\to KH_{i}([X/G])

are isomorphisms for all
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

We fix
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. By Proposition
4.2
(1), the map

(\Phi^{P}_{[X/G]})_{*}\colon\bigoplus_{\chi\in\widehat{P}}\pi_{i}(K^{\chi}([X/G]))\to K_{i}([X/G])
is an isomorphism. To prove the first isomorphism, it
suffices therefore to show that there is a
canonical isomorphism
K_{i}(X)\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}[\widehat{P}]\cong\bigoplus_{\chi\in\widehat{P}}\pi_{i}(K^{\chi}([X/G]))
. To that end, we consider the functor

\phi_{\chi}\colon{{\rm Qcoh}}_{X}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\chi}_{X}
given by
\phi_{\chi}({\mathcal{F}})={\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
, where
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
is the sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
on which
G
acts via
the character
\chi
. Thus for any étale
open
U
over
X
and
x\in{\mathcal{F}}(U)
, we have

g\cdot x=\chi(g)x\in{\mathcal{F}}(U)
. It is clear that
\phi_{\chi}
is an equivalence
of categories. In particular,
\phi_{\chi}
defines an equivalence of the corresponding
categories of chain complexes which preserves perfect complexes.
Considering the induced map on the
K
-theory spectra, we get that
the map
\phi^{*}_{\chi}\colon K(X)\to K^{\chi}({[X/G]})
is a weak equivalence.
Under this equivalence, the induced map

\phi^{*}_{\chi}\colon K_{i}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}K_{i}(X)
on the homotopy groups is
given by
\phi^{*}_{\chi}(x)=\chi\otimes x
. The arguments for the second and third
isomorphisms are identical.
∎

An identical argument implies the following.

Corollary 4.6
.

For
F\in\{HH,HC\}
, the canonical map
F_{i}(X)\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}[\widehat{P}]\to F_{i}([X/G])

is an isomorphism for every
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

5.
The twisting operator

In this section, we shall define the twisting operator on equivariant
K
-theory
and homology theories and describe its effect when we pass to the derived localization
and completion (in the sense of Lurie) of the equivariant
K
-theory spectra.
The construction of this operator is the second technical step in the proof of the
completion theorems.
We begin with a review of Lurie’s derived localization and completion.

5.1.
Lurie’s localization and completion

We briefly recall Lurie’s construction of derived localization and
completion of modules over a commutative ring spectrum
(also called an
E_{\infty}
ring)
and list some basic properties that are relevant to this paper.
We refer the reader to
[
69
, § 7.2.3]
(for localization) and

[
70
, Chap. 7.3]
(for completion) for full details.
Let
R
be a connective
E_{\infty}
ring and let
S\subset\pi_{0}(R)
be
a multiplicatively closed subset which contains the unit.
Let
{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
denote the (derived) category of
R
-module spectra.
Note that
{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
closed under homotopy limits and colimits.

As
\pi_{0}(R)
acts on
R
, it acts any object
M\in{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
.
One says that
M
is
S
-local if the map

M\xrightarrow{x}M
(defining the action of
x
on
M
)
is a weak equivalence for every
x\in S
.
We let
{{\mathbf{Mod}}}^{S{\rm-loc}}_{R}
be the full subcategory of
S
-local objects in

{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
. The
S
-localization functor
S^{-1}(-)\colon{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}\to{{\mathbf{Mod}}}^{S{\rm-loc}}_{R}

is the left adjoint of the inclusion functor
{{\mathbf{Mod}}}^{S{\rm-loc}}_{R}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
. For
every
M\in{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
, the canonical map
\delta_{M}\colon M\to S^{-1}M
between

R
-modules induces an isomorphism
S^{-1}\pi_{i}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{i}(S^{-1}M)

between
\pi_{0}(R)
-modules for every
i
(cf.
[
69
, Prop. 7.2.3.20]
).
In particular,
S^{-1}M
is connective if

M
is so. If
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset\pi_{0}(R)
is a prime ideal and
S=\pi_{0}(R)\setminus{\mathfrak{p}}
, we shall write
S^{-1}M
as
M_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
.

For
x\in\pi_{0}(R)
and
M\in{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
, we let
M[x^{-1}]
be the homotopy colimit of
the diagram
(M\xrightarrow{x}M\xrightarrow{x}M\xrightarrow{x}\cdots)
. We then
have the natural map
\delta^{\prime}_{M}\colon M\to M[x^{-1}]
. If we let

M[x^{-1}]\xrightarrow{x}M[x^{-1}]
be the multiplication map, then it induces the
multiplication map
\pi_{*}(M[x^{-1}])\xrightarrow{x}\pi_{*}(M[x^{-1}])
. Using the
isomorphism
\pi_{*}(M[x^{-1}])\cong\pi_{*}(M)[x^{-1}]
(since the homotopy groups
commute with filtered colimits), we see that the latter map is an
isomorphism. It follows that the map
M[x^{-1}]\xrightarrow{x}M[x^{-1}]
is a weak
equivalence. In other words,
M[x^{-1}]
is
S
-local if we
let
S=\{x^{n}|n\geq 0\}
.
In particular,
\delta^{\prime}_{M}
uniquely factors through
\delta_{M}\colon M\to S^{-1}M
.
Let
\phi_{M}\colon S^{-1}M\to M[x^{-1}]
be the induced map. By looking at
the induced map between the homotopy groups, we observe the following.

Lemma 5.1
.

There is a natural weak equivalence
\phi_{M}\colon S^{-1}M\xrightarrow{\simeq}M[x^{-1}]
.

If
y\in\pi_{0}(R)
is another element and we let
T=\{(xy)^{n}|n\geq 0\}
, then
one checks by passing to the homotopy groups that
T^{-1}M
is
S
-local. Hence,
Lemma
5.1
implies that there is a canonical map
M[x^{-1}]\to M[(xy)^{-1}]
.
It follows that
\{M[x^{-1}]|x\in\pi_{0}(R)\}
is a filtered family of localizations.
Given a multiplicatively closed subset
S\subset\pi_{0}(R)
containing the unit, we
let
M[S^{-1}]=\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{x\in S}M[x^{-1}]
. The following result generalizes
Lemma
5.1
and provides a description of Lurie’s derived localization.

Proposition 5.2
.

There is a natural weak equivalence
S^{-1}M\xrightarrow{\simeq}M[S^{-1}]
.

Proof.

This follows because the canonical map

S^{-1}M\xrightarrow{\simeq}M[S^{-1}]
induces isomorphism between the
homotopy groups.
∎

We next consider Lurie’s derived completion functor.
We let
x\in\pi_{0}(R)
be an element and let
M\in{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
. We say that
M
is

x
-nilpotent if the action of
x
on
\pi_{*}(M)
is locally nilpotent:
that is, if for each
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
y\in\pi_{i}(M)
, there exists an integer

n\geq 0
such that
x^{n}y=0
in the abelian group
\pi_{i}(M)
.
If
I\subset\pi_{0}(R)
is an ideal, we say that
M
is
I
-nilpotent if it is

x
-nilpotent for each
x\in I
. We say that
M
is
I
-local if for every

I
-nilpotent
R
-module
N
, the mapping space
{\rm Map}_{{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}}(N,M)
is
contractible. We say that
M
is
I
-complete if for every

I
-local
R
-module
N
, the mapping space
{\rm Map}_{{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}}(N,M)
is
contractible. We let
{\mathbf{Mod}}^{I{\rm-comp}}_{R}
denote the full subcategory of
{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}

consisting of
I
-complete
R
-modules.

Suppose there is a finitely generated ideal
J\subset\pi_{0}(R)
such that

\sqrt{J}=\sqrt{I}
. Then the inclusion
{\mathbf{Mod}}^{I{\rm-comp}}_{R}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
admits
a left adjoint
(-)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I}\colon{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}^{I{\rm-comp}}_{R}

(cf.
[
70
, Rem. 7.3.1.2, Prop. 7.3.1.4]
. We
shall call this the
I
-completion functor.
This functor is described as follows. We let
M\in{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
.

For
x\in\pi_{0}(R)
, there is a canonical weak
equivalence
M^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{(x)}\simeq\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{M}/{x^{n}M}
, where
{M}/{xM}
is
the homotopy cofiber of the map
M\xrightarrow{x}M
. Equivalently,
M^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{(x)}

is the homotopy cofiber of the augmentation map
T(M)\to M
, where

T(M)
is the homotopy limit of the sequence
(\cdots\xrightarrow{x}M\xrightarrow{x}M\xrightarrow{x}M)
(cf.
[
70
, Prop. 7.3.2.1]
).
Suppose now that
\sqrt{I}=\sqrt{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})}\subset\pi_{0}(R)
.
For
i=0,1,\ldots,r
, we let
M_{0}=M
and let
M_{i}
denote the

(x_{i})
-completion of
M_{i-1}
for
i\geq 1
.
We then have a canonical weak equivalence (cf.
[
70
, Prop. 7.3.3.2]
)

(5.1)

M^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I}\simeq M_{r}\simeq\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}}\frac{M}{(x^{i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,x^{i_{r}}_{r})M},

where each term inside the homotopy limit is an iteration of homotopy cofibers.
Furthermore,
M
is
I
-complete if and only if the canonical map

M\to M^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I}
is a weak equivalence.

Proposition 5.3
.

Assuming
\sqrt{I}=\sqrt{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})}
, the
I
-completion functor has the
following properties.

(1)

If
M
is connective, then so is
M^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I}
.

(2)

If
{\mathfrak{m}}\subset\pi_{0}(R)
is a maximal ideal which is the radical of a
finitely generated ideal, then the map

M\to M^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}}
factors through the localization
M\to M_{{\mathfrak{m}}}\to M^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}}
. Furthermore, the map
M^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}}\to(M_{{\mathfrak{m}}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}}

is a weak equivalence.

(3)

If
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset\pi_{0}(R)
is a prime ideal, then
\pi_{i}(M_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\cong(\pi_{i}(M))_{\mathfrak{p}}
for every
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(4)

M
is
I
-complete if and only if it is

(x_{i})
-complete for every
i
.

(5)

If
\sqrt{I}
is the radical of the extension of a finitely generated ideal

I^{\prime}\subset\pi_{0}(R^{\prime})
under a morphism
R^{\prime}\to R
of connective
E_{\infty}
-rings,
then for every
R
-module
M
,
the canonical map
M\to M^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I}
exhibits
M^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I}
as the

I^{\prime}
-completion of
M
(when regarded as an
R^{\prime}
-module). In particular,
M
is

I^{\prime}
-complete (when regarded as an
R^{\prime}
-module) if and only if it is

I
-complete.

(6)

M^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I}\simeq(M^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{J})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{(x)}
if
I=J+(x)
.

(7)

If
\{M_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in J}
is a diagram in
{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
and each
M_{\alpha}

is
I
-complete, then
\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{\alpha\in J}M_{\alpha}
is also
I
-complete.

Proof.

All items except (7) are proven in
[
70
, § 7.3.2, 7.3.3, 7.3.4]
. To prove
(7), we let
M=\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{\alpha\in J}M_{\alpha}
.
By assumption, the completion map

M_{\alpha}\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}}\frac{M_{\alpha}}{(x^{i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,x^{i_{r}}_{r})M_{\alpha}}
is a weak equivalence
for every
\alpha
and this map is natural in
\alpha\in J
. By passing to the
limit, we therefore get

\begin{array}[]{lll}M&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{\alpha\in J}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}}\frac{M_{\alpha}}{(x^{i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,x^{i_{r}}_{r})M_{\alpha}}\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{\alpha\in J}\frac{M_{\alpha}}{(x^{i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,x^{i_{r}}_{r})M_{\alpha}}\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}}\frac{M}{(x^{i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,x^{i_{r}}_{r})M}\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&M^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I},\end{array}

where the second weak equivalence follows from the fact that two homotopy limits
commute with each other (cf.
[
17
, Thm. 24.9 and § 31.5]
)
and the third weak equivalence follows from the fact that
a homotopy limit commutes a homotopy cofiber (which coincides with homotopy
fiber up to a shift). This finishes the proof.
∎

5.2.
Definition and properties of twisting operator

We fix a field
k
. We let
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
and
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
.
Let
g\in Z(G)(k)
be a semisimple element which acts trivially
on
X
. We let
P
denote the closure in
G
of the cyclic subgroup

\langle g\rangle\subset G(k)
generated by
g
.
Since the stabilizers of closed points are closed in
G
, it
follows that
P
acts trivially on
X
.
The reader may keep in mind that throughout this section,
the notation
F([X/G],k)
will simply mean

F([X/G])
(as defined in §
3.2.2
) for
F\in\{HH,HC^{-},HC,HP\}
.
But that is not the case with
K
-theory.

Lemma 5.4
.

P
is a closed diagonalizable subgroup of
G
contained in its center.

Proof.

The assertion that
P
is a closed subgroup of
G
contained in its center follows
from
[
12
, Prop. I.1.3, I.1.7]
. To see that
P
is diagonalizable, consider

G
as a closed subgroup of some
GL_{n}
. As
g
is a semisimple element of

G(k)
, it is so as an element of
GL_{n}(k)
too. In particular, it is contained
in a (split) maximal torus
T
of
GL_{n}
. Intersecting
T
with
G
, we get
that
\langle g\rangle
lies in a diagonalizable closed subgroup of
G
and therefore so does
its closure. It follows that
P
is diagonalizable.
∎

To define the twisting operator on
G
-equivariant
K
-theory of
X
, we assume
that
{\rm char}(k)=0
. Recall
that the structure map of
X
induces a morphism of ring spectra

K(BG)\to K([X/G])
. This yields a natural map of ring spectra

\gamma_{X}\colon K(BG)\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}K([X/G])\to K([X/G])
satisfying the standard
properties of modules over ring spectra, where
BG=[{{\rm pt}}/G]
is the
classifying stack of
G
(cf. §
2.1
). In particular,
we get a natural map of spectra
K(BG)\to{\rm Map}(K([X/G]),K([X/G]))
, where

{\rm Map}(A,B)
is the mapping spectrum (the internal
hom in the category of spectra). Applying the
\pi_{0}
functor, we get a natural
homomorphism
\lambda_{X}\colon R(G)\to[K([X/G]),K([X/G])]:={\operatorname{\text{End}}}_{{\operatorname{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}}}}(K([X/G]))={\rm Hom}_{{\operatorname{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}}}}(K([X/G]),K([X/G]))
. Giving an element
x\in R(G)
is equivalent to
giving a map
{\mathbb{S}}\xrightarrow{x}K(BG)
which takes the unit to
x
at the level of

\pi_{0}
. The map
\lambda_{X}(x)\colon K([X/G])\to K([X/G])
is then the composition

(5.2)

K([X/G])\simeq{\mathbb{S}}\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}K([X/G])\xrightarrow{x\wedge{\operatorname{id}}}K(BG)\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}K([X/G])\xrightarrow{\gamma_{X}}K([X/G]).

By the same token, we get a natural
k
-linear map
\lambda_{X}\colon R_{k}(G)\to{\operatorname{\text{End}}}_{{\operatorname{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}}}}(K([X/G],k))
.

Since
KH([X/G])
and
K^{\prime}([X/G])
are also modules over the spectrum
K(BG)
, we get
similar
k
-linear maps
\lambda^{h}_{X}\colon R_{k}(G)\to{\operatorname{\text{End}}}_{{\operatorname{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}}}}(KH([X/G],k))

and
\lambda^{\prime}_{X}\colon R_{k}(G)\to{\operatorname{\text{End}}}_{{\operatorname{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}}}}(K^{\prime}([X/G],k))
such that

\lambda_{X},\lambda^{h}_{X}
and
\lambda^{\prime}_{X}
are compatible with each other under the
canonical maps of
K(BG)
-modules
K([X/G],k)\to KH([X/G],k)\to K^{\prime}([X/G],k)
.
By Theorem
3.3
, we get similar maps for the homology theories of
[X/G]
.
The ring spectrum structure of
H_{k}
defines a homomorphism
\lambda_{0}\colon k\cong\pi_{0}(H_{k})\to{\operatorname{\text{End}}}_{{\operatorname{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}}}}(H_{k})
.

Given
\alpha\in k
and
\chi\in\widehat{P}
, there is a map of spectra

\widetilde{\alpha}\colon K^{\chi}([X/G])\wedge_{\mathbb{S}}H_{k}\to K^{\chi}([X/G])\wedge_{\mathbb{S}}H_{k}
, given
by
\widetilde{\alpha}={\operatorname{id}}_{K^{\chi}([X/G])}\wedge\lambda_{0}(\alpha)
. As

K^{\chi}([X/G],k)=K^{\chi}([X/G])\wedge_{\mathbb{S}}H_{k}
(note that
{\rm char}(k)=0
),
this yields an element
\widetilde{\alpha}\in{\operatorname{\text{End}}}_{{\operatorname{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}}}}(K^{\chi}([X/G],k))
.
Since
g,g^{-1}\in G(k)
, we have that
\chi(g^{-1})\in k^{\times}={\mathbb{G}}_{m}(k)
.
We define the twisting operator
t_{g}
on
K^{\chi}([X/G],k)
by

(5.3)

t_{g}:=\widetilde{\chi(g^{-1})}\colon K^{\chi}([X/G],k)\to K^{\chi}([X/G],k).

The twisting operator
t_{g}
on

HH^{\chi}([X/G])
and
HC^{\chi}([X/G])
is defined in an analogous way using
Corollary
4.3
.
Using the weak equivalences
\Phi^{P}_{[X/G]},\ \Psi^{P}_{[X/G]},\kappa^{P}_{[X/G]}
and

\theta^{P}_{[X/G]}
given by Proposition
4.2
, Corollary
4.3
and
Corollary
4.4
, we define for
F\in\{K,KH,K^{\prime},HH,HC\}
,
the operator
t_{g}
on
F([X/G],k)

to be the coproduct of its restrictions on
F^{\chi}([X/G],k)

as
\chi
runs through
\widehat{P}
. Note that
F([X/G])=F([X/G],k)
for
F\in\{HH,HC^{-},HC,HP\}
.

To define
t_{g}
on
HC^{-}
and
HP
, we argue slightly differently because we can
not use Corollary
4.3
directly.
To define
t_{g}
on
HC^{-}([X/G])
, we first note that for
every
\chi\in\widehat{P}
, the twisting operator
t_{g}
on
HH^{\chi}([X/G])

is
\Lambda
-linear (cf. §
3.2.1
) since it is simply multiplication by

\chi(g^{-1})\in\Lambda^{\times}
. Equivalently, the
t_{g}
-action on each

HH^{\chi}([X/G])
is
S^{1}
-equivariant.
We next note that for
every
\chi\in\widehat{P}
, the inclusion
HH^{\chi}([X/G])\to HH([X/G])
is
clearly
S^{1}
-equivariant because it is induced by the inclusion of dg categories.
This shows that the decomposition of
HH([X/G])
given in Corollary
4.3

is
S^{1}
-equivariant. It follows from these two observations that the
t_{g}
-action
on
HH([X/G])
is
S^{1}
-equivariant.
We now apply the
(-)^{hS^{1}}
functor to get the operator

t_{g}\colon HC^{-}([X/G])\to HC^{-}([X/G])
.

To define
t_{g}
on
HP([X/G])
, we note that the map

N\colon HC([X/G])[1]\to HC^{-}([X/G])
commutes with
t_{g}
.
To see this, we use the commutative diagram

(5.4)

\textstyle{\coprod_{\chi\in\widehat{P}}HC^{\chi}([X/G])[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{HC([X/G])[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{\coprod_{\chi\in\widehat{P}}(HC^{-})^{\chi}([X/G])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{HC^{-}([X/G]),}

where the left vertical arrow is obtained by taking the coproduct of the norm map on
each
HC^{\chi}([X/G])[1]
.

Since the horizontal arrows commute with the
t_{g}
-actions by latter’s construction
and the one on the top row is a weak equivalence, it suffices to
check that
N\colon HC^{\chi}([X/G])[1]\to(HC^{-})^{\chi}([X/G])
commutes with

t_{g}
. But this is clear since
N
is
\Lambda
-linear (cf. §
3.2.1
)
and
t_{g}
is multiplication by
\chi(g^{-1})\in\Lambda^{\times}
.
It follows that
t_{g}
induces unique
k
-linear maps

t_{g}\colon HP^{\chi}([X/G])\to HP^{\chi}([X/G])
and

t_{g}\colon HP([X/G])\to HP([X/G])
which are commute with the canonical
maps
HP^{\chi}([X/G])\to HP([X/G])
.

We let
t^{*}_{g}\colon F_{*}([X/G],k)\to F_{*}([X/G],k)
denote the induced maps on the
homotopy groups.

The following results summarize the basic properties of
t_{g}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
F\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(k)
.
Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a morphism in
{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
such that
P
acts trivially on

X^{\prime}
. Then the twisting operator
t_{g}
satisfies the following.

(1)

t_{g}
commutes with the pull-back maps
f^{*}\colon F([X/G],k)\to F([{X^{\prime}}/G],k)

except when
F=K^{\prime}
. If
f
is flat, the same is also true for
F=K^{\prime}
.

(2)

If
f
is proper, then
t_{g}
commutes with the push-forward map

f_{*}\colon K^{\prime}([{X^{\prime}}/G],k)\to K^{\prime}([X/G],k)
. If
f
is moreover an lci map,
the same is also true for
K
-theory and
KH
-theory.

(3)

t_{g}
is an automorphism of
F([X/G],k)
in
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}
.

(4)

t^{*}_{g}\colon R_{k}(G)\to R_{k}(G)
is a ring homomorphism.

(5)

If
k
is algebraically closed and
G
is reductive, then the trace map

R_{k}(G)\to HH_{0}(BG)
is an isomorphism of
k
-algebras and
HH_{i}(BG)=0
for

i\neq 0
.

Proof.

The items (1) and (2) are immediate from the construction of
t_{g}
,
Proposition
4.2
, Corollary
4.3
,
Corollary
4.4
and the fact that
f^{*}
and
f_{*}
(whenever defined)
are maps of
K(BG)
-modules.
It is clear from the construction of
t_{g}
that

t^{*}_{g}\colon\pi_{i}(F^{\chi}([X/G],k))\to\pi_{i}(F^{\chi}([X/G],k))
is
multiplication
by the element
\chi(g^{-1})\in k^{\times}
for every
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
In particular,
t^{*}_{g}
is an isomorphism. It follows that
t_{g}\colon F^{\chi}([X/G],k)\to F^{\chi}([X/G],k)
is a weak equivalence. Taking the sum over

\chi\in\widehat{P}
, we get that
t_{g}
is an automorphism of
F([X/G],k)
.
This proves (3), and (4) is shown in
[
64
, (4.5)]
.

To prove (5), note that
HH(BG)\simeq{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{L}}_{BG})\simeq{\mathcal{O}}([G/G])
for the
adjoint action by (
3.12
) (cf.
[
18
, Exm. 3.1.7]
).
Since
{\mathcal{O}}([G/G])
coincides with the
ring of regular functions on the affinization
{\rm Spec\,}(k[G]^{G})
of
[G/G]
,
we get that the canonical map
k[G]^{G}\to HH(BG)
is a weak equivalence
(in particular,
HH(BG)
is a discrete
K(BG)
-module).
But the canonical map
R_{k}(G)\to k[G]^{G}
is an isomorphism under our assumption
by Proposition
2.7
.
∎

Proposition 5.6
.

For
F\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(k)
and
x\in R_{k}(G)
, one has a commutative diagram

(5.5)

\textstyle{F([X/G],k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{X}(x)}
\scriptstyle{t_{g}}
\textstyle{F([X/G],k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{g}}
\textstyle{F([X/G],k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{X}(t^{*}_{g}(x))}
\textstyle{F([X/G],k).}

Proof.

We shall prove this for
K
-theory as the proof is same for

F\in\{HH,HC,K^{\prime},KH\}
, and
for
HC^{-}
and
HP
, it follows directly from
that of
HH
and
HC
since the maps in (
5.5
) are

S^{1}
-equivariant.

Assuming
F=K
, we see by
(
5.2
) that (
5.5
) is the
outer square in the diagram

(5.6)

\textstyle{{\mathbb{S}}\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}K([X/G],k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x\wedge{\operatorname{id}}}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}\wedge t_{g}}
\textstyle{K(BG,k)\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}K([X/G],k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{X}}
\scriptstyle{t_{g}\wedge t_{g}}
\textstyle{K([X/G],k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{g}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{S}}\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}K([X/G],k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{*}_{g}(x)\wedge{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{K(BG,k)\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}K([X/G],k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{X}}
\textstyle{K([X/G],k).}

It suffices therefore to show that (
5.6
) is commutative. Since
its left square commutes merely by the definitions of
t_{g}
and
t^{*}_{g}
, we only
need to show that the right square is commutative.

We now look at the diagram

(5.7)

\textstyle{K^{\chi}(BG,k)\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}K^{\chi^{\prime}}([X/G],k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{X}}
\scriptstyle{t_{g}\wedge t_{g}}
\textstyle{K^{\chi+\chi^{\prime}}([X/G],k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{g}}
\textstyle{K^{\chi}(BG,k)\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}K^{\chi^{\prime}}([X/G],k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{X}}
\textstyle{K^{\chi+\chi^{\prime}}([X/G],k).}

Proposition
4.2
(6) implies that the horizontal arrows in this diagram are
defined. Using Proposition
4.2
(1), it suffices to check that this is a
commutative diagram for every
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\widehat{P}
. By the construction of

t_{g}
, this reduces to showing that the diagram

(5.8)

\textstyle{H_{k}\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}H_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{0}(\chi(g^{-1}))\wedge\lambda_{0}(\chi^{\prime}(g^{-1}))}
\textstyle{H_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{0}((\chi+\chi^{\prime})(g^{-1}))}
\textstyle{H_{k}\wedge_{{\mathbb{S}}}H_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{H_{k}}

is commutative, where each of the horizontal arrows is the product operation of

H_{k}
. But this follows easily because (
5.8
) is induced by the
diagram of commutative rings (where the horizontal arrows are the multiplication
maps)

(5.9)

\textstyle{k\otimes k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(g^{-1})\otimes\chi^{\prime}(g^{-1})}
\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\chi+\chi^{\prime})(g^{-1})}
\textstyle{k\otimes k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k,}

whose commutativity is clear using the identity

(\chi+\chi^{\prime})(g^{-1})=\chi(g^{-1})\cdot\chi^{\prime}(g^{-1})
.
∎

Let
I_{G}={\rm Ker}(R(G)\xrightarrow{{\rm rank}}{\mathbb{Z}})
be the augmentation ideal
(cf. §
2.1
). In this paper, we shall write

{\rm Ker}(R_{k}(G)\xrightarrow{{\rm rank}}k)
also as
I_{G}
while working with

K
-theory with
k
-coefficients and homology theories.
Note that
I_{G}\subset R_{k}(G)
is the maximal
ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}
corresponding to the conjugacy class of the identity element of
G
.

Proposition 5.7
.

For
F\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(k)
, the operator

t_{g^{-1}}\colon F([X/G],k)\to F([X/G],k)
induces weak equivalences

(5.10)

t_{g^{-1}}\colon F([X/G],k)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{g}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}F([X/G],k)_{I_{G}};\ t_{g^{-1}}\colon F([X/G],k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{g}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}F([X/G],k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}.

Proof.

We shall show this for
K
-theory as the proof is the same for all functors.
We showed in the proof of Proposition
5.5
that
t^{*}_{g}
is multiplication
by
\chi(g^{-1})
on
K^{\chi}_{0}(BG,k)
for each
\chi\in\widehat{P}
.
One checks using this that
t^{*}_{g^{-1}}\colon R_{k}(G)\to R_{k}(G)
maps
{\mathfrak{m}}_{g}
isomorphically onto
I_{G}
and it maps

R_{k}(G)\setminus{\mathfrak{m}}_{g}
isomorphically onto
R_{k}(G)\setminus I_{G}

(cf.
[
64
, § 4.2]
or
[
30
, § 6.2]
).
We conclude from Proposition
5.6
that
t_{g^{-1}}

induces a weak equivalence

(5.11)

t_{g^{-1}}\colon\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{x\in R_{k}(G)\setminus{\mathfrak{m}}_{g}}K([X/G],k)[x^{-1}]\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{y\in R_{k}(G)\setminus I_{G}}K([X/G],k)[y^{-1}].

The first weak equivalence of (
5.10
) now follows
by Proposition
5.2
which says that the left hand side of
(
5.11
) is
K([X/G],k)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{g}}
while the right hand side
is
K([X/G],k)_{I_{G}}
.

To prove the weak equivalence of the completions, we first note by
Proposition
2.3
that
R_{k}(G)
is a Noetherian ring. We can thus
write
{\mathfrak{m}}_{g}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
. If we let
y_{i}=t^{*}_{g^{-1}}(x_{i})
, it follows
that
I_{G}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})
. Since
t^{*}_{g^{-1}}
is a ring homomorphism on

R_{k}(G)
by Proposition
5.5
, we conclude from Proposition
5.6

that
t_{g^{-1}}
induces a weak equivalence

(5.12)

t_{g^{-1}}\colon\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}}\frac{K([X/G],k)}{(x^{i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,x^{i_{r}}_{r})K([X/G],k)}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}}\frac{K([X/G],k)}{(y^{i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,y^{i_{r}}_{r})K([X/G],k)}.

We now apply (
5.1
) to finish the proof.
∎

5.3.
Group action with finite stabilizers

Let
k
be a field and
G
a
k
-group acting on

X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
. Let
\phi_{X}\colon G\times X\to X\times X
denote the action map
(cf. (
3.9
)).
Recall that the
G
-action on
X
is said to have finite
stabilizers if the stabilizers of geometric points are finite.
Equivalently, the projection map
I_{X}\to X
is quasi-finite, where

I_{X}\subset G\times X
is the inertia scheme (cf. (
3.9
)).
In other words,
[X/G]
is a quasi-Deligne-Mumford (equivalently, Deligne-Mumford
if
{\rm char}(k)=0
) stack (cf.
[
21
, Tag 06MC]
).
Recall as well that the
G
-action is said to have finite
inertia if the projection map
I_{X}\to X
is finite and it is called
proper if
\phi_{X}
is a proper map. It is clear that proper action implies that it
has finite inertia, which in turn implies that it has finite stabilizers.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
k
be algebraically closed of characteristic zero. Assume that the

G
-action on
X
has finite stabilizers. Then the following hold.

(1)

There exists a finite set of semisimple conjugacy classes

\Sigma^{G}_{X}=\{\Psi_{1},\ldots,\Psi_{n}\}
in
G(k)
such that for any

g\in G(k)
, one has that
X^{g}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
g\in\Psi_{i}

for some
1\leq i\leq n
.

(2)

There exists an ideal

J\subset R_{k}(G)
such that
{R_{k}(G)}/J
has finite support containing

\Sigma^{G}_{X}
and

J(K^{\prime}_{i}([X/G],k))=0=J(KH_{i}([X/G],k))\ \ \forall\ \ i\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

Proof.

Except for the vanishing of
J(KH_{*}([X/G],k))
, everything else in the
proposition is proven in
[
64
, Prop. 3.6, Lem. 3.9]
when

k={\mathbb{C}}
and the
G
-action is proper. But we leave it for the reader to check
that the proof of op. cit. does not require properness of
G
-action. The only
condition it uses is that this action has finite stabilizers.
One also does not require
k={\mathbb{C}}
anymore in view of Proposition
2.3
and
Corollary
2.11
.

We shall prove the vanishing of
J(KH_{*}([X/G],k))
in item (2) by
induction on the dimension of
X
.
If
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}^{G}_{k}
, then
we are already home by the vanishing of
J(K^{\prime}_{*}([X/G],k))

since the canonical map
KH([X/G],k)\to K^{\prime}([X/G],k)
is
a weak equivalence. If
\text{\rm dim}(X)=0
, then
X_{\rm red}
must be regular.
In particular, the claim holds in this case because of the
nil-invariance property of equivariant
K^{\prime}
-theory (well known) and
equivariant
KH
-theory (follows by
[
50
, Thm. 6.2]
).

To prove the claim when
X
is arbitrary, we apply

[
50
, Thm. 6.2]
once again to assume that
X
is reduced. We next
choose a
G
-equivariant resolution of singularities
(which is well known to exist)
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
. We let
Y=X_{\rm sing}
and

Y^{\prime}=f^{-1}(Y)
, each having the reduced closed subscheme structure. It follows
again from
[
87
, Lem. 2.5]
that there is a commutative diagram

(5.13)

\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{X,}

where all schemes lie in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{k}
and all maps are
G
-equivariant.

Since
G
acts on
X
with finite stabilizers, it must act on
X^{\prime},Y
and

Y^{\prime}
also with finite stabilizers.
We now apply
[
50
, Thm. 6.2]
one more time to conclude that
(
5.13
) induces a commutative diagram of spectra

(5.14)

\textstyle{KH([X/G])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{*}}
\textstyle{KH([Y/G])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime*}}
\textstyle{KH([{X^{\prime}}/G])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\prime*}}
\textstyle{KH([{Y^{\prime}}/G])}

which is homotopy Cartesian.
The same is true for
KH
-theory with coefficients in
k

as well.
The desired claim is true for
X^{\prime}
because it is smooth
over
k
. The claim is true for
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
by induction on the dimension.
Using the long exact sequence of homotopy groups resulting from
(
5.14
), we deduce the claim for
X
.
We note here that the ideal
J
is not independent of
X
.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
k
be of characteristic zero. Let
G
act on a

k
-scheme
X
with finite stabilizers. Then we have the following.

(1)

The canonical maps
K^{\prime}([X/G],L)_{I_{G}}\to K^{\prime}([X/G],L)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}
and
KH([X/G],L)_{I_{G}}\to KH([X/G],L)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}

are weak equivalences for any field
L
of characteristic zero.

(2)

The canonical maps

K^{\prime}([X/G],k)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\to K^{\prime}([X/G],k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}
and

KH([X/G],k)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\to KH([X/G],k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}
are weak equivalences for any semisimple
conjugacy class
\Psi\subset G(k)
.

Proof.

As
R_{L}(G)
is Noetherian by Proposition
2.3
, we can write

I_{G}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
. To prove (1), we let

M\in\{K^{\prime}([X/G],L)_{I_{G}},KH([X/G],L)_{I_{G}}\}
. By Proposition
5.3
(2),
it suffices to show that
M
is
(x_{i})
-complete for every
i
.
By
[
70
, Cor. 7.3.2.2]
, this is equivalent to showing that the
homotopy limit of the tower
T^{\bullet}_{i}:=(\cdots\xrightarrow{x_{i}}M\xrightarrow{x_{i}}M\xrightarrow{x_{i}}M)
is weakly contractible.
By the Milnor exact sequence

(5.15)

0\to{\varprojlim}^{1}\pi_{n+1}(T^{\bullet}_{i})\to\pi_{n}(\mathop{{\rm holim}}T^{\bullet}_{i})\to\varprojlim\pi_{n}(T^{\bullet}_{i})\to 0,

it suffices to show that the sequence
\pi_{n}(T^{\bullet}_{i}):=(\cdots\xrightarrow{x_{i}}\pi_{n}(M)\xrightarrow{x_{i}}\pi_{n}(M)\xrightarrow{x_{i}}\pi_{n}(M))

is pro-zero for every
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
1\leq i\leq r
.
Since a homotopy group functor commutes with localizations (cf.
Proposition
5.3
(3)), we are therefore
reduced to showing that for every
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, there exists
m\gg 0

such that

(5.16)

I^{m}_{G}(K^{\prime}_{n}([X/G],L)_{I_{G}})=0=I^{m}_{G}(KH_{n}([X/G],L)_{I_{G}}).

For
K^{\prime}_{n}([X/G],L)_{I_{G}}
, (
5.16
) is shown in the proof of

[
58
, Lem. 7.3]
. In particular, (
5.16
) holds
if
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}^{G}_{k}
. This conclusion for
KH_{n}([X/G],L)_{I_{G}}
in the general case
is now deduced by simply repeating the proof of Proposition
5.8
.

To prove (2), we repeat the argument of (1) which reduces us to showing
that for every
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, there exists
m\gg 0

such that

(5.17)

({\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi})^{m}(K^{\prime}_{n}([X/G],k)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}})=0=({\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi})^{m}(KH_{n}([X/G],k)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}).

We shall prove this for
KH_{n}([X/G],k)
as the other case is identical.
We fix an algebraic closure
\overline{k}
of
k
.
We let
{X^{\prime}}=X_{\overline{k}}
and
{G^{\prime}}=G_{\overline{k}}
. If
{k^{\prime}}/k
is
a finite field extension, then the composition of the pull-back and
push-forward maps
KH_{n}([X/G])\to KH_{n}([{X_{k^{\prime}}}/{G_{k^{\prime}}}])\to KH_{n}([X/G])
is multiplication by
[k^{\prime}:k]
.
It follows by passing to the limit (cf.
[
56
, Thm. 2.22]
) that
the pull-back map
KH_{n}([X/G],k)\to KH_{n}([{{X^{\prime}}}/{{G^{\prime}}}],k)
is injective.
In particular, the pull-back map
\lambda\colon KH_{n}([X/G],k)\to KH_{n}([{{X^{\prime}}}/{{G^{\prime}}}],\overline{k})
is also injective.

We let
{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}_{\Psi}\subset R_{\overline{k}}(G^{\prime})
denote the maximal ideal corresponding
to
g\in G^{\prime}_{s}(\overline{k})
.
By the definition of
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}
, it is clear that

\lambda(({\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi})^{m}KH_{n}([X/G],k))\subset({\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}_{\Psi})^{m}KH_{n}([{{X^{\prime}}}/{{G^{\prime}}}],\overline{k})
. It suffices therefore to
show that
({\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}_{\Psi})^{m}KH_{n}([{{X^{\prime}}}/{{G^{\prime}}}],\overline{k})=0
for
m\gg 0
.
We can thus assume that
k
is algebraically closed. In the latter case,
the claim follows directly from Proposition
5.8
(2).
∎

6.
Borel equivariant
K
-theory

The goal of this section is to define Borel equivariant
K
-theory of algebraic
spaces with a group action. This will be the target of the derived Atiyah-Segal
completion theorem for equivariant
K
-theory (also called Bredon
equivariant
K
-theory). We begin with some basic results related to group action.

6.1.
Some results on group quotients

We fix a field
k
and a
k
-group
G
.
We let
{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{{\rm free}}/k
be the full subcategory of
{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
whose objects are those
algebraic spaces over
k
on which
G
acts freely.
We let
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{{\rm free}}/k
be the full subcategory
of
{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{{\rm free}}/k
consisting of those objects which are schemes.
For
X,Y\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
, we consider
X\times Y
as an object of
{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}

via the diagonal action of
G
. It is clear that if either of
X
and
Y
lies in

{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{{\rm free}}/k
, then
X\times Y\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{{\rm free}}/k
. In this case, the quotient

(X\times Y)/G
lies in
{\mathbf{Spc}}_{k}
by
[
28
, Prop. 22]
. We shall denote this
quotient space by
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Y
. We shall use the following special
case of this construction very frequently in this text.

Suppose
G\subset G^{\prime}
is a closed embedding of

k
-groups and
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
. We let

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}G^{\prime}
denote the quotient by the diagonal
action of
G
, where the latter acts on
G^{\prime}
by the right multiplication
(
g\cdot g^{\prime}=g^{\prime}g^{-1}
). If we let
G^{\prime}
act trivially on
X
and
by left multiplication on itself, then one checks that its diagonal action on

X\times G^{\prime}
descends to its action on
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}G^{\prime}
.
That is,
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}G^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G^{\prime}}_{k}
. We shall call it the

Morita space
associated to the
G
-action on
X
and the
embedding
G\hookrightarrow G^{\prime}
.
Letting
X^{\prime}
denote this Morita space, one checks that the composition

(6.1)

\delta_{X}\colon X\xrightarrow{\iota}X\times G^{\prime}\xrightarrow{p}X^{\prime}

(where
\iota(x)=(x,e)
and
p
is the quotient map) is
G
-equivariant if
we let
G
act on
X^{\prime}
via the inclusion
G\hookrightarrow G^{\prime}
.
Passing to the stack quotients by the
G
-actions, we get a map

\widetilde{\delta}_{X}\colon[X/G]\to[{X^{\prime}}/{G}]
.
Precomposing this with the canonical quotient
[{X^{\prime}}/G]\to[{X^{\prime}}/{G^{\prime}}]
, we get

{\rm morita}_{X}\colon[X/G]\to[{X^{\prime}}/{G^{\prime}}]
. We now have the following.

Lemma 6.1
.

The map
{\rm morita}_{X}\colon[X/G]\to[{X^{\prime}}/{G^{\prime}}]
is an isomorphism of
stacks.

Proof.

See
[
64
, Lem. 2.4]
.
∎

We shall need the following result
(see §
1.9
for the definition of
G
-quasi-projective schemes).

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
be a reduced algebraic space.
If
G
is connected and
X
is a normal
k
-scheme, then it is covered by

G
-invariant open subschemes which are
G
-quasi-projective. In general,

X
contains a
G
-invariant dense open subscheme

U\subset X
such that
U
is a regular and
G
-quasi-projective
k
-scheme.

Proof.

Under the assumption of the first part,
[
13
, Thm. 1]

says that
X
is covered by
G
-invariant open subschemes which are
quasi-projective
k
-schemes. It follows from
[
89
, § 5.7]
that
these open subschemes must be
G
-quasi-projective.

We now prove the second part.
By
[
57
, Prop. 6.6]
,
X
contains a unique dense
open subspace
X^{\prime}\subset X

(called the schematic locus of
X
) having the property that
X^{\prime}
is a scheme
and any point
x\in X
is schematic (i.e., has an affine neighborhood in
X
) if
and only if
x\in X^{\prime}
(cf.
[
21
, Tag 03JG]
).
Since
gX^{\prime}\subset X
is again a scheme for every
g\in G
, it follows
that
X^{\prime}
must be
G
-invariant. Replacing
X
by
X^{\prime}
, we can thus assume that

X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{k}
. Since
X
is reduced, its regular locus
X_{\rm reg}
is
G
-invariant
dense open. We can thus assume that
X
is also regular.

If
G
is connected, then every connected component of
X
is
G
-invariant
and the result follows by
[
13
, Thm. 1]
and

[
89
, § 5.7]
. In general,
we choose an embedding
G\hookrightarrow GL_{n}
and let

Y=X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}GL_{n}
be the associated Morita space with

GL_{n}
-action. Since
X
is regular
and
GL_{n}
is smooth over
k
, we see that
X\times GL_{n}
is regular.
Since the quotient map
X\times GL_{n}\to Y
is a
G
-torsor and
G\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
,
it follows that
Y
is a regular algebraic space with
GL_{n}
-action.
Since
GL_{n}
is connected, every connected component of

Y
is a regular
GL_{n}
-invariant open algebraic subspace.
In particular, its schematic locus is
GL_{n}
-invariant and hence contains
a
GL_{n}
-invariant dense open subspace which is
quasi-projective over
k
. We let
W\subset Y
be the disjoint union of
these open subschemes. Then
W
is a
GL_{n}
-invariant dense open subspace of

Y
which is quasi-projective over
k
.

We now let
p\colon X\times GL_{n}\to Y
be the quotient map and let

W^{\prime}=p^{-1}(W)
. As
p\colon W^{\prime}\to W
is a
G
-torsor,
Lemma
6.3
implies that
W^{\prime}
is quasi-projective over
k
.
As
\iota
is a closed immersion, it follows that

U:=(\delta_{X})^{-1}(W)=\iota^{-1}(W^{\prime})
is a quasi-projective
k
-scheme.
Meanwhile, Lemma
6.1
implies that
\delta^{-1}_{X}(W)
is
G
-invariant
dense open in
X
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
H
be a
k
-group and let
p\colon W^{\prime}\to W
be an
H
-torsor in

{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
. Then
p
is an affine morphism. In particular, it is quasi-projective.

Proof.

If
p
is affine, it is quasi-projective by

[
36
, Prop. 5.1.6]
. To show that
p
is affine, it suffices to show that

W^{\prime}
is affine assuming
W
is. By faithfully flat descent, it suffices to show
that
p
is étale locally affine. But this is clear since
H
is affine and
every
H
-torsor is étale locally trivial.
∎

For
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{{\rm free}}/k
, one knows that the coarse moduli space map

c_{[X/G]}\colon[X/G]\to X/G
is an isomorphism (cf.
[
28
, Prop. 22]
).
That is,
[X/G]
is an algebraic space.
However, it may not be a scheme even if
X
is. We shall use the
following result in geometric invariant theory
to conclude that
[X/G]\cong X/G
is actually a scheme in certain cases.

Proposition 6.4
.

(
[
33
, Prop. 7.1]
)

Let
\phi\colon X\to Y
be a morphism in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{{\rm free}}/k
such that
Y/G
is a

k
-scheme and there is a
G
-equivariant
\phi
-ample line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
on
X
.
Then
X/G
is also a
k
-scheme. Furthermore, there exists a unique

\phi^{\prime}
-ample line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
on
X/G
such that
\pi^{*}_{X}({\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})\cong{\mathcal{L}}
,
where
\pi_{X}\colon X\to X/G
is the quotient map and

\phi^{\prime}\colon X/G\to Y/G
is the map induced by
\phi
on the quotients.

Corollary 6.5
.

Suppose that
G
is a closed
subgroup of another
k
-group
G^{\prime}
and
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{k}
is
G
-quasi-projective
(e.g.,
X
is normal and quasi-projective) over
k
. Then

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}G^{\prime}\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G^{\prime}}_{k}
is a quasi-projective
k
-scheme.
In particular, it is
G^{\prime}
-quasi-projective if
X
is normal.

Proof.

We let
Y=X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}G^{\prime}
and let
\phi\colon X\times G^{\prime}\to G^{\prime}

denote the projection and let
\phi^{\prime}\colon Y\to{G^{\prime}}/G
denote the induced map
on quotients. Since
X
admits a
G
-equivariant ample line bundle, it follows that

X\times G^{\prime}
admits a
G
-equivariant
\phi
-ample line bundle. Since

{G^{\prime}}/G
is a quasi-projective
k
-scheme by
[
12
, Thm. 6.8]
, we conclude
from Proposition
6.4
that
Y\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G^{\prime}}_{k}
and it admits a

\phi^{\prime}
-ample line bundle. In particular, it is quasi-projective over
k
.
Moreover, smoothness of
G
and
G^{\prime}
implies that
Y
is normal if
X
is
so and hence is
G^{\prime}
-quasi-projective over
k
by
[
89
, § 5.7]
.
∎

Corollary 6.6
.

(
[
28
, Prop. 23]
)

Suppose that
U\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{{\rm free}}/k
such that
U/G\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
.
Let
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{k}
and assume that one of the following conditions hold.

(1)

G
is connected and
X
is normal.

(2)

G
is special.

(3)

X
is
G
-quasi-projective.

Then
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}U\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
. It is normal (resp. smooth) if
X
is.
It is quasi-projective if (3) holds and
U/G
is quasi-projective.

Proof.

The first claim is already proven in
[
28
, Prop. 23]
and the second claim is
obvious.
If
U/G
is quasi-projective and condition (3) holds, then
we repeat the proof of Corollary
6.5
with
G^{\prime}
replaced
by
U
to get the desired claim.
∎

6.2.
K
-theory of Borel construction

Let
k
be a field and
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
. Recall from
[
60
, § 3]
that
a pair
(V,U)
of smooth
k
-schemes is a
good pair
for
G
if
V
is a
representation of
G
and

U\subsetneq V
is a
G
-invariant open subset which lies in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{{\rm free}}/k

such that
U/G\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
and

U(k)\neq\emptyset
(this is automatic if
k
is infinite).
It is known (cf.
[
93
, Rem. 1.4]
) that a good pair for
G
always exists.
We also recall the following from
[
60
, Defn. 3.1]
.

Definition 6.7
.

A sequence of pairs
\rho={\left(V_{i},U_{i}\right)}_{i\geq 1}
of

k
-schemes is called an
admissible gadget
for
G
, if there exists a
good pair
(V,U)
for
G
such that
V_{i}=V^{\oplus i},U_{1}=U
and

U_{i}\subsetneq V_{i}
is
G
-invariant open and
the following are satisfied for each
i\geq 1
.

(1)

\left(U_{i}\oplus V\right)\cup\left(V\oplus U_{i}\right)\subseteq U_{i+1}
as
G
-invariant open subsets.

(2)

{\rm codim}_{U_{i+2}}\left(U_{i+2}\setminus\left(U_{i+1}\oplus V\right)\right)>{\rm codim}_{U_{i+1}}\left(U_{i+1}\setminus\left(U_{i}\oplus V\right)\right)
.

(3)

{\rm codim}_{V_{i+1}}\left(V_{i+1}\setminus U_{i+1}\right)>{\rm codim}_{V_{i}}\left(V_{i}\setminus U_{i}\right)
.

(4)

U_{i}\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{{\rm free}}/k
and
{U_{i}}/G\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
.

We shall say that
\rho={\left(V_{i},U_{i}\right)}_{i\geq 1}

is a
good admissible gadget

for
G
if it also satisfies the following additional condition.

(5) Each
{U_{i}}/G
is a smooth quasi-projective
k
-scheme.

The following result guarantees the existence of admissible gadgets.

Lemma 6.8
.

Assume that
G
is a special reductive group and let
H\subset G
be a closed
subgroup. Then the following hold.

(1)

If
\rho={\left(V_{i},U_{i}\right)}_{i\geq 1}
is an admissible gadget for
G
, then
it is also an admissible gadget for
H
under the restriction of the
action from
G
to
H
.

(2)

If
H
is reductive and
\rho
is a good admissible gadget for
G
, then it is a
good admissible gadget for
H
.

(3)

A good admissible gadget for
G
exists.
In particular, every
k
-group admits an admissible gadget.

Proof.

To prove (1), we only need to show that if
\rho={\left(V_{i},U_{i}\right)}_{i\geq 1}

is an admissible gadget for
G
, then each
{U_{i}}/H
is a smooth
k
-scheme.
To that end, we fix
i\geq 1
and look at the quotient maps

U_{i}\xrightarrow{p}{U_{i}}/H\xrightarrow{q}{U_{i}}/G
. We let
\pi=q\circ p
.
Since
\pi
is a
G
-torsor of schemes and
G
is special, we get that there exists
an affine Zariski open cover
\{W_{j}\}
of
{U_{i}}/G
such that

\pi^{-1}(W_{j})\cong W_{j}\times G
and
\pi
is the projection to
W_{j}
. It follows
that
q^{-1}(W_{j})\cong W_{j}\times({G/H})
. As
G/H
is a quasi-projective

k
-scheme (cf.
[
12
, Thm. 6.8]
), it follows that
{U_{i}}/H
has a Zariski
open cover by
k
-schemes. We conclude that
{U_{i}}/H\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
. The smoothness of

{U_{i}}/H
is directly deduced from that of
U_{i}
.

To prove (2), we need to show in the proof of (1) that
{U_{i}}/H
is quasi-projective
if
H
is reductive. To that end, we first observe that
G/H
is an affine

k
-scheme. This follows from
[
75
, Thm. A]
because we can
always pass to an algebraic closure of
k
to check affineness.
We next note that
{U_{i}}/H
is Zariski locally product of
{U_{i}}/G
with

G/H
, as shown above. It follows that
q
is an affine map. In particular, it is
quasi-projective. We are now done because
{U_{i}}/G
is
quasi-projective over
k
by the hypothesis in item (2).

To prove (3), we first define our
\rho
. We choose a faithful
representation
W
of
G
of dimension
n
and let

V=W^{\oplus n}\simeq{\rm End}_{k}(W)
.
If we let
U\subset V
be the largest
G
-invariant open on which
G
acts freely,
then the faithfulness implies that
U(k)\neq\emptyset
. We let

Z=V\setminus U
. We take
V_{i}=V^{\oplus i},U_{1}=U
and

U_{i+1}=\left(U_{i}\oplus V\right)\cup\left(V\oplus U_{i}\right)
for
i\geq 1
.
Setting
Z_{1}=Z
and
Z_{i+1}=U_{i+1}\setminus\left(U_{i}\oplus V\right)
for

i\geq 1
, one checks that
V_{i}\setminus U_{i}=Z^{i}
and
Z_{i+1}=Z^{i}\oplus U
.
In particular,
{\rm codim}_{V_{i}}\left(V_{i}\setminus U_{i}\right)=i({\rm codim}_{V}(Z))
and

{\rm codim}_{U_{i+1}}\left(Z_{i+1}\right)=(i+1)d-i(\text{\rm dim}(Z))-d=i({\rm codim}_{V}(Z))
,
where
d=\text{\rm dim}(V)
. To show that
\rho={\left(V_{i},U_{i}\right)}_{i\geq 1}
is a
good admissible gadget for
G
, it remains to prove that each
{U_{i}}/G
is
a quasi-projective
k
-scheme.

To that end, we let
U^{\prime}_{i}\subset V_{i}
be the largest
G
-invariant open subscheme
of
V_{i}
where the
G
-action is free. Note that
U^{\prime}_{i}\neq\emptyset
because

V
has a non-empty
G
-invariant open with free
G
-action.
We let
V^{s}_{i}\subset V_{i}
be the largest

G
-invariant open subscheme such that the
G
-orbit of every closed
point
x\in V^{s}_{i}

is closed and the stabilizer group
G_{x}
is finite. We then have
G
-invariant
open immersions
U^{\prime}_{i}\subset V^{s}_{i}\subset V_{i}

(cf.
[
72
, § 4.2]
,
[
33
, Chap. 1, App. B]
). We now apply

[
33
, Thm. 1.10]
(with
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}
) to conclude that the quotient

{V^{s}_{i}}/G
exists as a quasi-projective
k
-scheme.
On the other hand, there is a
G
-invariant open inclusion
U_{i}\subset U^{\prime}_{i}

by our choice of
U_{i}
. It follows that there are open immersions

{U_{i}}/G\subset{U^{\prime}_{i}}/G\subset{V^{s}_{i}}/G
. In particular,
{U_{i}}/G
and

{U^{\prime}_{i}}/G
are quasi-projective
k
-schemes.
∎

Given an
admissible gadget

\rho={\left(V_{i},U_{i}\right)}_{i\geq 1}
for
G

and
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
, we let
X^{i}_{G}(\rho)
denote the quotient space

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}U_{i}
.
We shall often write
X^{i}_{G}(\rho)
as
X^{i}(\rho)
if the underlying
group
G
is fixed throughout the context.
We can say the following about
X^{i}(\rho)
.

Corollary 6.9
.

Under any of the three conditions in Corollary
6.6
,
we have
X^{i}(\rho)\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
. It is smooth over
k
if
X
is so.
If
\rho
is a good admissible gadget and
X
is

G
-quasi-projective over
k
, then
X^{i}(\rho)
is quasi-projective over
k
.

Proof.

Follows from Corollary
6.6
.
∎

Given an admissible gadget
\rho
as above and
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
,
the locally closed immersions
U_{i}=U_{i}\times\{0\}\hookrightarrow U_{i+1}
for
i\geq 1

yield an ind-object
``{\varinjlim_{i}}"X^{i}(\rho)
in
{\mathbf{Spc}}_{k}
.
We shall denote the limit of this ind-space (as an étale sheaf of sets
on
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
) by
X^{\bullet}_{G}(\rho)
and call it
a ‘motivic Borel space’. If
k={\mathbb{C}}
, then
X^{\bullet}_{G}(\rho)_{{\rm an}}

is homotopy equivalent to the homotopy orbit space for the
G_{{\rm an}}
-action on

X_{{\rm an}}
.

Since the inclusion
U_{i}\hookrightarrow U_{i+1}
has factorization
U_{i}\hookrightarrow U_{i}\oplus V\hookrightarrow U_{i+1}
, where the first inclusion is a regular closed immersion and
the second is an open immersion, it follows that
U_{i}\hookrightarrow U_{i+1}
is a regular
immersion (in particular, an lci map). As
X
is
k
-flat, it follows that
the canonical inclusion

\alpha_{i}\colon X^{i}(\rho)\hookrightarrow X^{i+1}(\rho)
is also a regular immersion.
In particular,
{\bf{L}}\alpha^{*}_{i}
sends cohomologically bounded
pseudo-coherent complexes on

X^{i+1}(\rho)
to cohomologically bounded
pseudo-coherent complexes on
X^{i}(\rho)
(cf.

[
90
, § 2.5.1]
). We thus have the pull-back maps

\alpha^{*}_{i}\colon K^{\prime}(X^{i+1}(\rho))\to K^{\prime}(X^{i}(\rho))
which satisfy the
usual composition law. These maps together define a pro-spectrum

\{K^{\prime}(X^{i}(\rho))\}_{i\geq 1}
.
We similarly have a pro-spectrum
\{KH(X^{i}(\rho))\}_{i\geq 1}
.
In order to define the
K^{\prime}
-theory of motivic Borel spaces using these pro-spectra,
we need to prove the following result.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
\rho={(V_{i},U_{i})}_{i\geq 1}
and
\rho^{\prime}={(V^{\prime}_{i},U^{\prime}_{i})}_{i\geq 1}
be two
admissible gadgets for
G
and let

X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
. Then there is a canonical weak equivalence

\kappa^{\prime}_{X}\colon\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{i}K^{\prime}(X^{i}(\rho))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{i}K^{\prime}(X^{i}(\rho^{\prime})).

If
X
is either

(1)

a toric variety on which
G
acts as the dense torus or

(2)

{\rm char}(k)=0
and
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{k}
,

then there is a canonical weak equivalence

\kappa_{X}\colon\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{i}KH(X^{i}(\rho))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{i}KH(X^{i}(\rho^{\prime})).

The weak equivalences
\kappa_{X}
and
\kappa^{\prime}_{X}
are functorial in
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
.

Proof.

For
i,j\geq 1
, we consider the algebraic spaces
{\mathcal{V}}_{i,j}={\left(X\times U_{i}\times V^{\prime}_{j}\right)}/G;\ {\mathcal{W}}_{i,j}={\left(X\times V_{i}\times U^{\prime}_{j}\right)}/G
and their common open
subspace
{\mathcal{U}}_{i,j}={\left(X\times U_{i}\times U^{\prime}_{j}\right)}/G
.
For a fixed
i\geq 1
, this yields a sequence
{\left({\mathcal{V}}_{i,j},{\mathcal{U}}_{i,j},f_{i,j}\right)}_{j\geq 1}
, where
{\mathcal{V}}_{i,j}\xrightarrow{\pi_{i,j}}X^{i}(\rho)
is a vector bundle,
{\mathcal{U}}_{i,j}\subseteq{\mathcal{V}}_{i,j}
is an open
subspace of this vector bundle and
f_{i,j}:\left({\mathcal{V}}_{i,j},{\mathcal{U}}_{i,j}\right)\to\left({\mathcal{V}}_{i,j+1},{\mathcal{U}}_{i,j+1}\right)
is the natural map of pairs
of spaces over
X^{i}(\rho)
. For a fixed
j\geq 1
, we similarly get a
sequence
{\left({\mathcal{W}}_{i,j},{\mathcal{U}}_{i,j},g_{i,j}\right)}_{i\geq 1}
of spaces over

X^{j}(\rho^{\prime})
.

Since
f_{i,j}\colon{\mathcal{U}}_{i,j}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{i,j+1}
and

g_{i,j}\colon{\mathcal{U}}_{i,j}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{i+1,j}
are lci maps, we get a pro-spectrum

\{K^{\prime}({\mathcal{U}}_{i,j})\}_{i,j}
. Taking the limits, we get the maps
of spectra

(6.2)

\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{i}K^{\prime}(X^{i}(\rho))\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{i,j}K^{\prime}({\mathcal{U}}_{i,j})\xleftarrow{g^{\prime}}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{j}K^{\prime}(X^{j}(\rho^{\prime})).

We similarly have the maps of spectra

(6.3)

\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{i}KH(X^{i}(\rho))\xrightarrow{f}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{i,j}KH({\mathcal{U}}_{i,j})\xleftarrow{g}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{j}KH(X^{j}(\rho^{\prime})).

It is clear that all these maps are natural in
X
. We shall show that
each of these maps is a weak equivalence under the hypotheses of the proposition.
Letting
\kappa^{\prime}_{X}=g^{\prime-1}\circ f^{\prime}
and
\kappa_{X}=g^{-1}\circ f
will then
finish the proof of the proposition, including the naturality claim.
Using the symmetry between
f
and
g
(resp.
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
), the problem is reduced
to showing that
f
and
f^{\prime}
are weak equivalences under the hypotheses of the
