B^{\bullet}_{G}

Given
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
, we let
E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)
denote the simplicial algebraic
space

(9.5)

E_{G}^{\bullet}(X):=\ \left(\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{\underset{\rightarrow}{\rightrightarrows}}}G\times G\times G\times X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{\underset{\rightarrow}{\to}}}G\times G\times X\rightrightarrows G\times X\right)

with the face maps
d^{i}_{n}:G^{n+1}\times X\to G^{n}\times X
(
0\leq i\leq n
)
given by the projections

d^{i}_{n}(g_{0},\cdots,g_{n},x)=(g_{0},\cdots,g_{i-1},\widehat{g_{i}},g_{i+1},\cdots,g_{n},x),

where
\widehat{g_{i}}
means that this coordinate is omitted. The degeneracy maps

s^{i}_{n}\colon G^{n+1}\times X\to G^{n+2}\times X
(
0\leq i\leq n
) are the various
diagonals on
G
and the identity map on
X
. In particular, the face maps of

E_{G}^{\bullet}(X)
are smooth and the degeneracy maps are regular closed
immersions.

G
acts on
E_{G}^{\bullet}(X)
via the levelwise action

g\cdot\left(g_{0},\cdots,g_{n},x)=(g_{0}g^{-1},\cdots,g_{n}g^{-1},gx\right)
. It is easy to check that all the
face and degeneracy maps are
G
-equivariant with respect to this action
so that
E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)
is a simplicial object in
{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
. Furthermore, this
action is levelwise free.

It is also easy to verify that there is a natural morphism of simplicial spaces

(9.6)

\pi_{X}\colon E_{G}^{\bullet}(X)\to X_{G}^{\bullet};

\pi^{n}_{X}\left(g_{0},\cdots,g_{n},x\right)=\left(g_{1}g^{-1}_{0},g_{2}g^{-1}_{1},\cdots,g_{n}g^{-1}_{n-1},g_{0}x\right)

which makes
X_{G}^{\bullet}
the quotient of
E_{G}^{\bullet}(X)

by the free
G
-action. Hence,
\pi_{X}
is a
G
-torsor of
simplicial spaces. Letting
E^{\bullet}_{G}=E_{G}^{\bullet}({\rm pt})
, we see that

E_{G}^{\bullet}(X)\cong E_{G}^{\bullet}{\times}X
with the diagonal action of

G
and the canonical map
E_{G}^{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}X\to X_{G}^{\bullet}

is an isomorphism of simplicial algebraic spaces.
Let
\pi_{0}\colon E^{\bullet}_{G}\to B^{\bullet}_{G}
denote the quotient map.
We will see that
\pi_{0}
represents the universal
G
-torsor over the classifying
space of
G
in
{\mathcal{H}}(k)
.

9.3.
Bar construction vs. Borel construction

We shall assume now that
G
is a special
k
-group.
Recall from
[
72
, Chap. 4]
that if
G
is a sheaf of groups on

{\rm Nis}/k
, then a left (resp. right) action of
G
on a motivic space

Y
is a morphism
\mu\colon G\times Y\to Y
(resp.

\mu\colon Y\times G\to Y
) such that the usual diagrams commute.
For a
G
-action on
Y
, the quotient
Y/G
is the motivic space such that

(9.7)

\textstyle{G\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{p_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{Y/G}

is a pushout diagram of motivic spaces. In particular,
Y/G
coincides with
the coarse moduli space (if it exists) for the
G
-action on
Y
if

Y\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{k}
(cf.
[
33
, Defn. 0.1]
). A (left) action as above is called
(categorically) free if the morphism

G\times Y\to Y\times Y
of the form
(g,y)\mapsto(gy,y)

is a monomorphism. We shall let
{\rm Msp}^{G}_{\rm fr}(k)
denote the category of
motivic spaces with free
G
-actions.
In the case of free
G
-action, the quotient
Y/G
has the following additional
properties.

Lemma 9.1
.

Assume that the
G
-action on
Y
is free. We then have the following.

(1)

The quotient map

p\colon Y\to Y/G
is a Nisnevich local fibration with fiber
G
, i.e., for every
smooth Henselian local
k
-algebra
R
, the map
Y(R)\to(Y/G)(R)
is a Kan
fibration of simplicial sets with fiber
G(R)
.

(2)

If
Y\in{\mathbf{Sm}}^{G}_{k}
, then
Y/G
is the (étale) sheaf represented by the
algebraic space
[Y/G]
.

Proof.

By definition of
Y/G
in (
9.7
) and
[
98
, Exc. 8.2.6]
,
the presheaf quotient map
Y\to(Y/G)^{\rm pr}
is a pointwise Kan fibration of
presheaves of simplicial sets on
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
with fiber
G
. In particular,
the sheaf quotient map
p\colon Y\to Y/G
is a stalkwise Kan fibration
(called local fibration in
[
72
, Chap. 2, Defn. 1.11]
) in
the Nisnevich topology of
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
. Since every smooth Henselian local
k
-algebra

R
is the Nisnevich stalk of a point on a smooth
k
-scheme, we conclude that
the map
Y(R)\to(Y/G)(R)
is a Kan fibration of
simplicial sets with fiber
G(R)
. The second part of the lemma follows from

[
28
, Prop. 22]
which says that the stack quotient
[Y/G]
is an
algebraic space and it coincides with the categorical quotient
(cf.
[
33
, Defn. 0.1]
) under the given hypotheses.
∎

One can now generalize the construction of
E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)
as follows.
We let
{\rm Et}/k
denote the big étale site of
{\rm Spec\,}(k)
.
Given a sheaf of sets
F
on
{\rm Et}/k
with a free
G
-action, we consider the
simplicial sheaf of sets

(9.8)

E^{\bullet}_{F}=\left(\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{\underset{\rightarrow}{\rightrightarrows}}}F\times F\times F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{\underset{\rightarrow}{\to}}}F\times F\rightrightarrows F\right),

where the face and the degeneracy maps are defined exactly as they are defined
for
E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)
in (
9.5
).
Then
G
acts freely on
E^{\bullet}_{F}
via its
diagonal action on each
F^{n}
.
Letting
B^{\bullet}_{F}
denote the quotient, we get a
G
-torsor

\pi_{F}\colon E^{\bullet}_{F}\to B^{\bullet}_{F}
. Recall here that a
G
-torsor
(or a principal
G
-bundle) over a motivic
space
S
is a morphism
Y\to S
of motivic spaces
together with a free
G
-action on
Y
over
S

such that the map
Y/G\to S
is an isomorphism.

Let
B^{\bullet}_{G}\xrightarrow{\phi}{\mathcal{B}}_{G}
be a trivial
cofibration of motivic spaces with
{\mathcal{B}}_{G}
fibrant.
Since
G
is special, it follows from

[
72
, Chap. 4, Prop. 1.15, Lem. 1.18]
that there is a
universal
G
-torsor
{\mathcal{E}}_{G}\to{\mathcal{B}}_{G}
such that for any sheaf of
sets
F
on
{\rm Nis}/k
with free
G
-action, there are Cartesian squares
of motivic spaces

(9.9)

\textstyle{E^{\bullet}_{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\overline{\phi}}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{F}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\overline{\phi}}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{X}}
\textstyle{B^{\bullet}_{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{F}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}_{G}}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{G},\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X}}

where
\phi_{F}
is well-defined up to a simplicial homotopy. The map

\phi_{X}
is the composition of the projection
X^{\bullet}_{G}\to B^{\bullet}_{G}
followed by

\phi
. The following result is a
consequence of
[
72
, Chap. 4, Prop. 1.20]
.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
\rho=\left(V_{i},U_{i}\right)_{i\geq 1}
be an admissible gadget for
G

(cf. Definition
6.7
)
and let
F={\rm colim}_{i}\ U_{i}\in{\rm Nis}/k
. Then the maps
\phi_{F}
and

\overline{\phi}_{F}
in (
9.9
) are simplicial weak equivalences.

Proof.

Assume that
\rho
is given by a good pair
(V,U)
and let
x\in U
be
a
k
-rational point (which exists by definition of a good pair).
We first show that
\phi_{F}
is a simplicial weak equivalence.
According to the proof of
[
72
, Chap. 4, Prop. 1.20]
, we
need to show that if
E\to S
is a
G
-torsor over a smooth Henselian local

k
-scheme
S
, then the morphism
E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}U_{i}\to E/G\cong S

splits for some
i\gg 0
. Since
G
is special, any such torsor must be trivial. In
particular, it is the projection
S\times G\to S
.
To find a splitting, it suffices to find a
G
-equivariant
morphism
G\to U_{i}
. But this is given by the
G
-orbit
Gx\hookrightarrow U=U_{1}
.

To show that
{\overline{\phi}}_{F}
is a simplicial weak equivalence, we need to show
using
[
72
, Chap. 3, Lem. 1.11]
that for every smooth Henselian
local
k
-algebra
R
, the map
E^{\bullet}_{F}(R)\to{\mathcal{E}}_{G}(R)
is a
weak equivalence of simplicial sets.
To that end, we note that the vertical maps in (
9.9
) are local
fibrations with fiber
G
by Lemma
9.1
.
These local fibrations yield for us a commutative diagram of simplicial sets

(9.10)

\textstyle{G(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{\bullet}_{F}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{\bullet}_{F}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}_{G}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}_{G}(R),}

where the rows are fibration sequences of simplicial sets.
It follows from
[
46
, Prop. 3.6.1]
that the rows remain
fibration sequences upon taking the geometric realizations.

We have shown above that the right vertical arrow in (
9.10
) is a
simplicial weak equivalence and hence a weak equivalence of geometric realizations.
The left vertical map is an isomorphism of simplicial sets.
We conclude from the long exact sequence of homotopy groups of
fibrations that the middle vertical map is a weak equivalence of
geometric realizations. Hence, the map
E^{\bullet}_{F}(R)\to{\mathcal{E}}_{G}(R)
is a
weak equivalence of simplicial sets.
∎

Let
\rho=\left(V_{i},U_{i}\right)_{i\geq 1}
be an admissible gadget for
G

and let
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}^{G}_{k}
. We let
X_{G}(\rho)
denote the motivic space

{\rm colim}_{i}X^{i}_{G}(\rho)
where
X^{i}_{G}(\rho)=X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}U_{i}

is a smooth
k
-scheme by Corollary
6.9
and
the colimit is taken in the category
{\rm Nis}/k
.
We call
X_{G}(\rho)
a motivic Borel space.
The following result will be a key step in the solution of
Thomason’s completion problem.

Proposition 9.3
.

There is a canonical isomorphism
X_{G}(\rho)\cong X^{\bullet}_{G}
in
{\mathcal{H}}(k)
.
In particular,
X_{G}(\rho)
does not depend on the choice of the admissible
gadget
\rho
.

Proof.

We let
F={\rm colim}_{i}U_{i}
as in Lemma
9.2
. Since colimits
commute with quotient maps and finite products, the canonical map
X_{G}(\rho)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}F
is an isomorphism of motivic spaces.
We let
\phi^{X}_{F}\colon X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{F}\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
be the map induced on the quotients by the

G
-equivariant map
\overline{\phi}^{X}_{F}=({\operatorname{id}}_{X}\times\overline{\phi}_{F})\colon X{\times}E^{\bullet}_{F}\to X{\times}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
. We first show that
\phi^{X}_{F}
is a simplicial weak equivalence.

For this, we note that
\overline{\phi}_{F}\colon E^{\bullet}_{F}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{G}

is a simplicial weak equivalence by Lemma
9.2
. This implies that
the map
\overline{\phi}^{X}_{F}
is also a simplicial weak equivalence.
We now let
R
be a smooth Henselian local
k
-algebra
and consider the commutative diagram of simplicial sets

(9.11)

\textstyle{G(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(X{\times}E^{\bullet}_{F})(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}^{X}_{F}(R)}
\textstyle{\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{F}\right)(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{X}_{F}(R)}
\textstyle{G(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(X{\times}{\mathcal{E}}_{G})(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}\right)(R),}

whose rows are fibration sequences of simplicial sets, as shown in the
proof of Lemma
9.1
.
As in the proof of Lemma
9.2
, we need to show that the right vertical
arrow is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets. But this follows by
repeating the argument of the proof of Lemma
9.2
and noting that the left
and the middle vertical arrows are weak equivalences.

We next observe that since the map
\phi\colon B^{\bullet}_{G}\to{\mathcal{B}}_{G}
is a
simplicial weak equivalence, a repetition of the above arguments
shows that
\overline{\phi}\colon E^{\bullet}_{G}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
and

\phi_{X}\colon X^{\bullet}_{G}\cong X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{G}\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}

are simplicial weak equivalences. Combining
\phi^{X}_{F}
and
\phi_{X}
, we get
simplicial weak equivalences

(9.12)

\phi_{X}\colon X^{\bullet}_{G}\xrightarrow{\simeq}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}\xleftarrow{\simeq}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{F}\colon\phi^{X}_{F}.

We now think of
F
as a constant simplicial sheaf and
consider the
G
-equivariant map of simplicial sheaves
u:F\to E^{\bullet}_{F}

given in degree
n
by
u_{n}(a)=(a,\cdots,a)
. This in turn gives a map

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}F\xrightarrow{u_{X}}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{F}
. To finish the proof of the proposition,
it suffices to show that this map is an
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-weak equivalence.
By
[
72
, Chap. 2, Prop. 2.14]
, we can further reduce to showing
that each map
u_{X,n}:X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}F\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}F^{n+1}
is an
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-weak equivalence.
In order to do so, it suffices to show that the projection

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}F^{n+1}\xrightarrow{p_{X,n}}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}F^{n}
is an
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-weak equivalence for each
n>0
.

To show the last assertion, we observe that the assumption that the base
scheme is a field and
G
is special guarantees that
\rho
is a nice admissible
gadget for
G
in the sense of
[
72
, Chap. 4, Defn. 2.4]
.
We can therefore apply Lemma 2.9 (in Chapter 4) of op. cit. to conclude that
the map
\left(X\times(U_{i})^{n}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}F\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}(U_{i})^{n}

is an
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-weak equivalence for each
n,i\geq 1
.
Passing to the colimit as
i\to\infty
, we see from
[
22
, Prop. 19]

that
p_{X,n}
is an
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-weak equivalence. This completes the proof of the
proposition.
∎

Given a motivic space
Y\in{\rm Msp}(k)
, we let
K(Y)
be the mapping spectrum

(9.13)

K(Y)={\rm Maps}_{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}(Y_{+}),KGL_{k}),

where
KGL_{k}\in{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}(k)
is the
algebraic
K
-theory spectrum (cf.
[
97
, § 6.2]
,

[
61
, § 7.2]
) and
\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}(Y_{+})
is the image of
Y

under the composite functor

(9.14)

{\mathcal{H}}(k)\xrightarrow{(-)_{+}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(k)\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}(-)}{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}(k).

For
Y\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
, this coincides with the Thomason-Trobaugh
K
-theory spectrum
of
Y
. In general, we can write
Y=\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{\alpha\in I}Y_{\alpha}
, where

I
is a locally small diagram category together with a functor

F\colon I\to{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
such that
Y_{\alpha}=F(\alpha)
. Since each motivic space is
cofibrant and the
T
-suspension functor
\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}(-)
preserves
homotopy colimits, we get

(9.15)

\begin{array}[]{lll}K(Y)&=&K(\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{\alpha\in I}Y_{\alpha})\\
&=&{\rm Maps}_{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}(\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{\alpha}(Y_{\alpha})_{+}),KGL_{k})\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Maps}_{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}(k)}(\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{\alpha}\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}((Y_{\alpha})_{+}),KGL_{k})\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{\alpha}{\rm Maps}_{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}((Y_{\alpha})_{+}),KGL_{k})\\
&\simeq&\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{\alpha}K(Y_{\alpha}),\end{array}

where the arrow on the third line is a weak equivalence by Lemma
10.1
.
If
Y\in{\rm Msp}(k)
is a motivic space equipped with a free
G
-action and
Y/G

is the quotient motivic space, we let
K_{G}(Y):=K(Y/G)
. If
Y
and
Y/G
are
both smooth schemes, then
K_{G}(Y)
coincides with the classical equivariant
K
-theory
of
Y
(cf. §
3.1
).

As a consequence of Proposition
9.3
, we get the following.
This provides another proof of Proposition
6.10
in a special case.

Corollary 9.4
.

If
G
is special and
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}^{G}_{k}
, then
K(X_{G})\simeq K(X_{G}(\rho))

for any admissible gadget
\rho
for
G
. That is, the Borel equivariant

K
-theory of
X
is representable in
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}(k)
.

10.
Some results on motivic spaces and their
K
-theory

In this section, we collect some technical results related to motivic spaces
and their
K
-theory that we shall need for proving our main result on
Thomason’s completion problem.
We let
k
be a field,
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
a special group and
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}^{G}_{k}
.

10.1.
K
-theory of quotient motivic spaces

For
{\mathcal{F}}\in{\rm Msp}(k)
, we let
\int_{\mathcal{F}}
denote the category whose objects are maps
of sheaves
f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{F}}
with
Y\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
and the hom set between

f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{F}}
and
f^{\prime}\colon Y^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{F}}
consists of maps

\phi\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
such that
f^{\prime}\circ\phi=f
.
Let
\Delta
denote the standard simplicial indexing category.
We shall use the following known results.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a simplicial sheaf on
{\operatorname{Nis}}/k
which sends

[n]\in\Delta
to
{\mathcal{G}}^{n}
and let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a discrete sheaf of sets on

{\operatorname{Nis}}/k
. Then the canonical maps of simplicial sheaves

\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{[n]\in\Delta}{\mathcal{G}}^{n}\to{\mathcal{G}}\ \mbox{and}\ \mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{Y\in\int_{\mathcal{F}}}Y\to\mathop{\text{\rm colim}}_{Y\in\int_{\mathcal{F}}}Y\to{\mathcal{F}}

are simplicial weak equivalences, where each
{\mathcal{G}}^{n}

is considered as a constant simplicial sheaf.

Proof.

The first weak equivalence is well known (cf.
[
27
, Rem. 2.1]
).
It is also well known that
\mathop{\text{\rm colim}}_{Y\in\int_{\mathcal{F}}}Y\to{\mathcal{F}}
is a simplicial weak
equivalence (cf.
[
25
, (2.1.1)]
).
The claim that
\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{Y\in\int_{\mathcal{F}}}Y\to{\mathcal{F}}
is a simplicial weak
equivalence is a direct consequence (in fact, equivalent to) of

[
25
, Lem. 2.7]
, as explained in
[
24
, Rem. 3.2.7]
.
∎

We let
d\colon{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
be a morphism in
{\rm Msp}^{G}_{\rm fr}(k)
.
For
i=1,2
, we let
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
be a
subcategory of
\int_{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}
such that all objects

f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
in
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
are morphisms in
{\rm Msp}^{G}_{\rm fr}(k)
and all morphisms
between two objects of
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
are
G
-equivariant.
We assume that for any
f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
in
{\mathcal{I}}_{1}
, the composition

d\circ f:Y\to{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is an object of
{\mathcal{I}}_{2}
so that
d
defines a functor

\psi_{d}\colon{\mathcal{I}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{I}}_{2}
. For
i=1,2
, the product of a map

(f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{F}}_{i})\in\int_{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}
with
{\operatorname{id}}_{X}

and subsequent quotient by
G
-action yields

\overline{f}\colon X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Y\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{F}}_{i}

which is compatible with the morphisms in
\int_{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}
. Taking the homotopy colimit,
we thus get a map
\overline{\phi}_{i}\colon\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{Y\in{\mathcal{I}}_{i}}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Y\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
.

For every
f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
in
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
, we let
{\rm pr}_{Y}\colon X\times Y\to X

be the projection. Then
{\rm pr}_{Y}
commutes with all maps

X\times Y\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{id}}_{X}\times\alpha}X\times Y^{\prime}

with
Y\xrightarrow{\alpha}Y^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{I}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{2}
. Since

\psi_{d}(f)=d\circ f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is an
object of
{\mathcal{I}}_{2}
for every
f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
in
{\mathcal{I}}_{1}
, we get a
commutative diagram of spectra

(10.1)

\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{Y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{2}}K_{G}(X\times Y^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(d\circ f)^{*}}
\textstyle{K_{G}(X\times Y)}
\textstyle{K_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{Y}}

where
p^{*}_{2}
is obtained by taking the homotopy limit of the diagram

{\rm pr}_{Y^{\prime}}^{*}\colon K_{G}(X)\to K_{G}(X\times Y^{\prime})
with
f^{\prime}\colon Y^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
in

{\mathcal{I}}_{2}
. This diagram is clearly compatible with respect to maps in
{\mathcal{I}}_{1}
.
Passing to the limit over
{\mathcal{I}}_{1}
, we get the pull-back

d^{*}\colon\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{Y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{2}}K_{G}(X\times Y^{\prime})\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{Y\in{\mathcal{I}}_{1}}K_{G}(X\times Y)
.

Letting
p^{*}_{1}
denote the map obtained by taking the homotopy limit of the diagram

{\rm pr}_{Y}^{*}\colon K_{G}(X)\to K_{G}(X\times Y)
with
f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
in

{\mathcal{I}}_{1}
, we get the following.

Lemma 10.2
.

The diagram

(10.2)

\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{Y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{2}}K_{G}(X\times Y^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{Y\in{\mathcal{I}}_{1}}K_{G}(X\times Y)}
\textstyle{K_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{1}}

is commutative.

We now assume that
Y/G\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
for each
f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
in
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
.
In this case, the quotient
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Y
is a smooth
k
-scheme by
Corollary
6.6
and the pull-back

K(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Y)\to K_{G}(X\times Y)
by the quotient map

X\times Y\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Y
is a weak equivalence.
Letting
\phi_{i,X}\colon\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{Y\in{\mathcal{I}}_{i}}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Y\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
denote the map induced by
\phi_{i}
and
{\operatorname{id}}_{X}
,
we have a homotopy commutative diagram
of motivic spaces

(10.3)

\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{Y\in{\mathcal{I}}_{1}}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\phi}_{1,X}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d}}
\textstyle{X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{Y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{2}}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\phi}_{2,X}}
\textstyle{X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{F}}_{2}.}

Considering the induced maps of
K
-theory spectra, we get the following.

Lemma 10.3
.

One has a homotopy commutative diagram

(10.4)

\textstyle{K_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{Y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{2}}K_{G}(X\times Y^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{Y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{2}}K(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Y^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d}^{*}}
\textstyle{K(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{F}}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{{\phi}^{*}_{2,X}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{Y\in{\mathcal{I}}_{1}}K_{G}(X\times Y)}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{Y\in{\mathcal{I}}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces K(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Y)}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{K(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\phi}^{*}_{1,X}}

of spectra in which the middle horizontal arrows are weak equivalences.

Proof.

The left triangle in (
10.4
) commutes by Lemma
10.2
,
the middle square clearly commutes and the right square commutes by
(
10.3
). The middle horizontal arrows are weak equivalences
by
[
43
, Thm. 11.4]
and
[
47
, § 6]
since

K_{G}(X\times Y)
and
K(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Y)
as well
as
K_{G}(X\times Y^{\prime})
and
K(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Y^{\prime})
are
\Omega
-spectra
for all
Y\in{\mathcal{I}}_{1}
and
Y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{2}
.
∎

10.2.
Approximation of
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}

We now look at the universal
G
-torsor
\pi\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{G}\to{\mathcal{B}}_{G}

(cf. (
9.9
)). If
{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
is the simplicial sheaf which sends

[n]
to
{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}
, then
\pi_{n}\colon{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}
is
a
G
-torsor for every
n
. Furthermore, the canonical map

\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{[n]\in{\Delta}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
is a simplicial weak equivalence
by Lemma
10.1
. For any
n\geq 0
and any map

f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}
of discrete sheaves, we let
{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)

denote the pull-back of the
G
-torsor
{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}
via
f
and
let
\pi_{n}(f)\colon{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\to{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}
be the induced
G
-equivariant map.
It follows that
{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)
is a motivic space with a free
G
-action, which is
a smooth
k
-scheme if
Y
is.

We let
\int^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}}
denote the category whose objects are the

G
-equivariant maps
\pi_{n}(f)\colon{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)

\to{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}
where
(f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G})\in\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}
. The morphisms
between
the objects of
\int^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}}
are the base change of morphisms in
\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}
.
Recall that if
F\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
is a functor between two categories
and
A\in{\mathcal{D}}
, then
A/F
is the comma category whose objects are maps

g\colon A\to F(B)
in
{\mathcal{D}}
with
B\in{\mathcal{C}}

and the morphisms between
g
and
g^{\prime}
are maps

f\colon B\to B^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{C}}
such that
F(f)\circ g=g^{\prime}
.

Lemma 10.4
.

For every
n\geq 0
, the canonical maps

(1)

\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{Y\in\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\to\mathop{\text{\rm colim}}_{Y\in\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\to{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}
;

(2)

\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{Y\in\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}}X{\times}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\to\mathop{\text{\rm colim}}_{Y\in\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}}X{\times}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\to X{\times}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G};

(3)

\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{Y\in\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\to\mathop{\text{\rm colim}}_{Y\in\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}

are simplicial weak equivalences.

Proof.

We fix
n\geq 0
and prove (2) first.
We let
T=X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}
and let
F\colon\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}\to\int_{T}
be the
functor which sends
f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}
to

({\operatorname{id}}_{X}\times\pi_{n}(f))\colon X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\to T
.
Using Lemma
10.1
,
[
43
, Thm. 10.8]
and

[
21
, Tag 09WN, Lem. 4.17.2]
, it suffices to show that the functor

F
is right cofinal as well as homotopy right cofinal. In other words, we need to
show for every
f\colon Z\to T
in
\int_{T}
that the nerve of the comma
category
{f}/F
is connected as well as contractible
(cf.
[
43
, Defn. 10.6]
). By
[
74
, Cor. 2, p. 84]
and

[
21
, Tag 09WN, Defn. 4.17.1]
, it suffices to show that
{f}/F
has an
initial object.

To that end, we let
{\rm pr}_{X}\colon T\to X
and
{\rm pr}_{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}}\colon T\to{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}

be the projections.
We let
f_{1}={\rm pr}_{X}\circ f\colon Z\to X
and

f_{2}={\rm pr}_{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}}\circ f\colon Z\to{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}
so that
f=(f_{1},f_{2})
.
Letting
g=\pi_{n}\circ f_{2}\colon Z\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}
, we see that there is a unique
map
\alpha\colon Z\to{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)
such that the diagram

(10.5)

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}(g)}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}

commutes and the right square is Cartesian.
We claim that
\widetilde{\alpha}=(f_{1},\alpha)\colon Z\to X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)
is an
initial object of
{f}/F
.

To prove the claim, we let
F^{\prime}\colon\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}\to\int_{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}}
be the functor
which sends
f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}
to the pull-back map

\pi_{n}(f)\colon{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\to{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}
. We first show that

\alpha\colon Z\to{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)

is an initial object of the comma category
{f_{2}}/{F^{\prime}}
.
To prove this, we let
(h\colon Y\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G})\in\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}
and let

{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)
be the pull-back of
h
via
\pi_{n}
.
We let
\alpha^{\prime}\colon Z\to{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)
be any object of

{f_{2}}/{F^{\prime}}
and look at the commutative diagram

(10.6)

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}(h)}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\prime}\circ\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}.}

Since the two squares in this diagram commute and the right square is Cartesian,
it follows that there is a unique morphism
\psi\colon{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)\to{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)

such that the diagram

(10.7)

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}

is commutative in which

and

\pi_{n}(h)\circ\psi=\pi_{n}(g)
and the two squares are Cartesian.
Since
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}
are
fixed, it also follows that
\beta^{\prime}\circ\alpha^{\prime}\colon Z\to Y
is the
unique morphism in
\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}
such that
\alpha\circ F^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime}\circ\alpha^{\prime})=\alpha\circ\psi=\alpha^{\prime}
. We have thus shown that
\alpha\colon Z\to{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)
is an initial object of
{f_{2}}/{F^{\prime}}
.

To finish the proof of the claim, we let

\widetilde{\alpha^{\prime}}=(f^{\prime}_{1},\alpha^{\prime})\colon Z\to X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)
be any object of

{f}/F
such that
{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)
is the pull-back of
(h\colon Y\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G})\in\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}
via
\pi_{n}
.
Since
\alpha\colon Z\to{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)
is an initial object of
{f_{2}}/{F^{\prime}}
,
we get a unique morphism
\gamma\colon(g\colon Z\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G})\to(h\colon Y\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G})
in
\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}

such that both (
10.6
) and (
10.7
)
are commutative and
\gamma=\beta^{\prime}\circ\alpha^{\prime}
.
Letting

\widetilde{\gamma}=({\operatorname{id}}_{X},\psi)\colon X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)\to X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)
,
we see that
\widetilde{\gamma}
is the unique morphism
such that
\widetilde{\alpha^{\prime}}=\widetilde{\gamma}\circ\widetilde{\alpha}
.
This proves the claim and hence item (2) of the lemma.
Item (1) is a special case of item (2) where we take
X={\rm pt}
.

To prove item (3), we let
W=X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\cong T/G

and let
\overline{F}\colon\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}\to\int_{W}
be the functor which sends

(u\colon Y\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G})
to the map
\overline{u}\colon X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}=W
induced by
the product

({\operatorname{id}}_{X}\times\pi_{n}(\overline{u}))\colon X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\to X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}
.
We let
f\colon Z\to W
be an object of
\int_{W}
. As in the previous cases, it
suffices to show that the comma category
{f}/{\overline{F}}
has an initial object.

To that end, we let
f_{2}\colon Z\to W\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}
be the composite map
where the second map is the canonical projection. We let
{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)
be the

G
-torsor over
Z
obtained by the pull-back of
\pi_{n}
by
f_{2}
. We then note that
the diagram

(10.8)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n,X}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}

is commutative in which the squares are Cartesian, where
f_{2}={\rm pr}^{\prime}_{2}\circ f
.

Letting
f^{\prime}_{2}=\pi_{n}(f_{2})
and
f^{\prime}=(f^{\prime}_{1},f^{\prime}_{2})
, we get a commutative diagram

(10.9)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{X}}
\textstyle{X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n,X}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}}

\textstyle{X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G},}

where
\beta_{X}
is the quotient map,
g_{2}\circ g_{1}=f
and
g^{\prime}_{2}\circ g^{\prime}_{1}=f^{\prime}
.
Moreover,

g^{\prime}_{1}=(f^{\prime}_{1},{\operatorname{id}})
,
g^{\prime}_{2}=({\operatorname{id}},f^{\prime}_{2})
and
g_{1}
(resp.
g_{2}
) is the
map on quotients induced by
g^{\prime}_{1}
(resp.
g^{\prime}_{2}
). It is clear that the left square
in (
10.9
) is Cartesian. It remains to show that
g_{1}\colon Z\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)
is an initial object of
{f}/{\overline{F}}
.

We let
h_{1}\colon Z\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)
be an
object of
{f}/{\overline{F}}
, where
(\alpha\colon Y\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G})\in\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}
and

{\mathcal{E}}^{n}(Y)
is the pull-back of
\alpha
via
\pi_{n}
.
Since
\beta\colon{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)\to Z
is a
G
-torsor and

G
-torsors are preserved under pull-back, it follows that there is a commutative
diagram

(10.10)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

whose squares are Cartesian.

Letting
\delta^{\prime}
denote the composite map
{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Z)\xrightarrow{h^{\prime}_{1}}X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\xrightarrow{{\rm pr}_{2}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)
, we see that

\delta^{\prime}
is a
G
-equivariant map of smooth
k
-schemes with free
G
-actions.
Letting
\delta\colon Z\to Y
be the induced map between the quotients, we get
a map
\delta\colon(f_{2}\colon Z\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G})\to(\alpha\colon Y\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G})

in
{f}/{\overline{F}}
such that

. It is clear that

\delta
is the unique map such that
\overline{F}(\delta)\circ g_{1}=h_{1}
.
This proves (3) and concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

11.
Thomason’s problem for special groups

In the next two sections, we shall prove our main results on
Thomason’s completion problem. Our strategy is to factorize
Thomason’s pull-back map
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}
(cf. (
9.3
))
into a composition of two maps.
We shall then show separately that each of these two maps induces a weak equivalence
after we pass to the completion of equivariant
K
-theory.
Before we do this, we first show that
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}
indeed factors through the
completion.

11.1.
Factorization through the completion

We let
k
be any field,
G
any
k
-group and
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
.
The
G
-equivariant projection map
\tau_{X}\colon E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)\to X

(cf. (
9.5
)) induces the pull-back map

\tau^{*}_{X}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X)\to K^{\prime}_{G}(E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)):=\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}K^{\prime}_{G}(G^{n+1}\times X)
.
On the other hand, the quotient map

\pi_{X}\colon E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)\to X^{\bullet}_{G}
yields the pull-back map

(11.1)

K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G})\simeq\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}K^{\prime}(G^{n}{\times}X)\xrightarrow{\pi^{*}_{X}}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}K^{\prime}(G^{n+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}X)\simeq K^{\prime}_{G}(E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)).

The map
\pi^{*}_{X}
is a levelwise weak equivalence, and hence a weak equivalence
between the homotopy limits (see the proof of Lemma
10.3
).

Lemma 11.1
.

The diagram of spectra

(11.2)

\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{*}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G}(E^{\bullet}_{G}(X))}

is commutative.

Proof.

We need to show that for every
n\geq 0
, the diagram

(11.3)

\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{*}_{X,n}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{n}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(G^{n}\times X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{*}_{X,n}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G}(G^{n+1}\times X)}

commutes up to homotopy and is compatible with the pull-back maps between
the
K
-theory spectra induced by the bonding maps of
E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)
and

X^{\bullet}_{G}
.

To prove the latter claim, it suffices to show that

(11.4)

\textstyle{G^{n+1}\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{X,n}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{X,n}}
\textstyle{G^{n}\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}_{n}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{[X/G]}

is a commutative diagram of flat maps which commutes with the
bonding maps of
E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)
and
X^{\bullet}_{G}
. Since

\widetilde{\pi}_{n}=\pi\circ p^{i_{1}}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ p^{i_{n}}_{n}

does not depend on the
choice of
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})
, it suffices to show for the
commutativity of (
11.4
) that

\pi\circ p^{1}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ p^{n}_{n}\circ\pi_{X,n}=\pi\circ\tau_{X,n}
. But this clear since we have

\tau_{X,n}(g_{0},\ldots,g_{n},x)=x
and

p^{1}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ p^{n}_{n}\circ\pi_{X,n}(g_{0},\ldots,g_{n},x)=g_{0}x
for any
(g_{0},\ldots,g_{n},x)\in G^{n+1}\times X
.
The commutativity of
(
11.4
) with the
bonding maps of
E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)
and
X^{\bullet}_{G}
is checked similarly.
∎

The map
\tau^{*}_{X}
is clearly
K(BG)
-linear.
Since
\pi^{*}_{X}
is canonically a weak equivalence, the
K(BG)
-module structure
of
K^{\prime}(E^{\bullet}_{G}(X))
endows the same on
K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G})
with respect to which

\pi^{*}_{X}
is
K(BG)
-linear. It follows from Lemma
11.1
that

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}
is
K(BG)
-linear. Since the
K(BG)
-module structures of

K^{\prime}_{G}(E^{\bullet}_{G}(X))
and
K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G})
factor through the canonical map

K(BG)\to K({\rm pt})
(cf.
[
60
, Lem. 8.1]
),
it follows that each
K^{\prime}_{G}(G^{n+1}\times X)
(resp.

K^{\prime}(G^{n}\times X)
) is
I_{G}
-adically complete. In particular,
K^{\prime}_{G}(E^{\bullet}_{G}(X))

and
K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G})
are
I_{G}
-adically complete by Proposition
5.3
(7).
It follows that
{\tau^{*}_{X}}
and
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}
factor through

(11.5)

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G});\ \ \tau^{*}_{X}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to K^{\prime}_{G}(E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)).

11.2.
Key step: factorization of
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}

We shall now show the factorization of
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}
into a composition of two maps.
We assume in this subsection that
k
is any field,
G\in{\mathbf{Grp}}_{k}
is special and

X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}^{G}_{k}
.

We let
\rho=(V_{i},U_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be an admissible gadget for
G
and let

F=\mathop{\text{\rm colim}}_{i}U_{i}\in{\rm Msp}(k)
. We let
\Delta_{F}\colon F\to E^{\bullet}_{F}

denote the diagonal map (cf. proof of Proposition
9.3
). The commutative
diagram of
G
-equivariant maps between simplicial ind-schemes
(where
p_{X}
and
{\rm pr}_{2}
are the projections)

(11.6)

\textstyle{X\times F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{F,X}}
\scriptstyle{p_{X}}
\textstyle{X\times E^{\bullet}_{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}_{2}}
\textstyle{X}

(where
\Delta_{F,X}={\operatorname{id}}_{X}\times\Delta_{F}
)
induces a commutative diagram of spectra

(11.7)

\textstyle{K_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{([n],i)\in\Delta\times{\mathbb{N}}}K_{G}(X\times(U_{i})^{n+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta^{*}_{F,X}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}K_{G}(X\times U_{i}).}

Since each
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}(U_{i})^{n}
is a smooth
k
-scheme by
Corollary
6.9
, we see that
K_{G}(X\times(U_{i})^{n})
coincides with

K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}(U_{i})^{n}\right)
.
By (
9.15
), the above diagram
therefore gives rise to the commutative diagram

(11.8)

\textstyle{K_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{F}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta^{*}_{F,X}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}F\right)}

of
K
-theories of smooth simplicial
k
-schemes.

Our next task is to define a pull-back map

K_{G}(X)\to K(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}_{G})
and relate it to
(
11.8
).
We fix
n\geq 0
. For every
(f\colon Y\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G})
in
\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}
, we let

{\rm pr}_{Y}\colon X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\to X
be the projection.
As this is a
G
-equivariant map between smooth schemes with
G
-actions,
we get the pull-back map

{\rm pr}^{*}_{Y}\colon K_{G}(X)\to K_{G}(X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y))
. Since
G
acts freely on

X{\times}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)
and the quotient

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)
lies in
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
by Corollary
6.6
,
the pull-back map
K(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y))\to K_{G}(X\times{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y))

is a weak equivalence.
Since the projection maps
{\rm pr}_{Y}
commute with all maps of the kind
{\operatorname{id}}_{X}\times\phi

with
\phi\in\int^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}}
, we get canonical maps

(11.9)

{\rm pr}^{*}_{1,n}\colon K_{G}(X)\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{Y\in\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}}K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\right)\xleftarrow{\simeq}K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\right),

where the second map is a weak equivalence by Lemma
10.4
and
(
9.15
).

If we now vary
n
and apply Lemma
10.3
to all face and degeneracy maps
(which are all
G
-equivariant) of
{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
, we deduce that the maps
{\rm pr}^{*}_{1,n}

commute with the maps induced on the
K
-theory spectra by the face and degeneracy
maps of
{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
as
n
varies. By passing to the limit, (
11.9
)
therefore yields the maps of spectra

(11.10)

{\rm pr}^{*}_{1}\colon K_{G}(X)\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[n]\in\Delta}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{Y\in\int_{{\mathcal{B}}^{n}_{G}}}K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}(Y)\right)\xleftarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[n]\in\Delta}K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\right)\xleftarrow{\simeq}K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}\right),

where the last map is a weak equivalence by Lemma
10.1
and
(
9.15
).

Recall that
\phi^{X}_{F}\colon X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{F}\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
is the map induced on the quotients by the

G
-equivariant map

({\operatorname{id}}_{X}\times\overline{\phi}_{F})\colon X{\times}E^{\bullet}_{F}\to X{\times}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}

(see the proof of Proposition
9.3
)).
For any
n\geq 0
, we let

{\mathcal{C}}_{E^{n}_{F}}
denote the category whose objects are the inclusions

(U_{i})^{n+1}\hookrightarrow E^{n}_{F}
for
i\geq 1
and morphisms are the canonical inclusions

(U_{i})^{n+1}\hookrightarrow(U_{i+1})^{n+1}
.

Lemma 11.2
.

The diagram

(11.11)

\textstyle{K_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{2}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\phi^{X}_{F})^{*}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{F}\right)}

is commutative.

Proof.

We let
n\geq 0
and let
d_{i}\colon[n]\to[n+1]
be any face map.
We consider the diagram

(11.12)

\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\phi^{X}_{F,n})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{d^{*}_{i}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{n}_{F}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{*}_{i}}
\textstyle{K_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{1,n}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{2,n}}
\textstyle{K_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{1,n+1}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{2,n+1}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n+1}_{G}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\phi^{X}_{F,n+1})^{*}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{n+1}_{F}\right).}

The top and the bottom triangles in the middle commute by Lemma
10.3

and we showed above in the construction of
{\rm pr}^{*}_{1}
and
{\rm pr}^{*}_{2}
that the left
and the right faces of (
11.12
) commute. The same holds for
any degeneracy map
s_{i}\colon[n+1]\to[n]
. Since (
11.11
) is
obtained by taking
the cosimplicial limit of the triangles in the middle of (
11.12
),
it suffices to show that the diagram

(11.13)

\textstyle{K_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{1,n}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{2,n}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\phi^{X}_{F,n})^{*}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{n}_{F}\right)}

is commutative for every
n\geq 0
.
But this easily follows by applying Lemma
10.3
with

{\mathcal{F}}_{1}=E^{n}_{F},\ {\mathcal{F}}_{2}={\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G},\ {\mathcal{I}}_{1}={\mathcal{C}}_{E^{n}_{F}}
,
{\mathcal{I}}_{2}=\int^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}}
and

d=\overline{\phi}_{F,n}
.
∎

Let
\overline{\phi}\colon E^{\bullet}_{G}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
be the
G
-equivariant map
induced on the
G
-torsors by
\phi\colon B^{\bullet}_{G}\to{\mathcal{B}}_{G}
and let

\overline{\phi}_{X}=({\operatorname{id}}_{X},\overline{\phi})\colon X\times E^{\bullet}_{G}\to X\times{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
.
We now note that
\tau^{*}_{X}\colon K_{G}(X)\to K(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{G})

is defined similarly to
{\rm pr}^{*}_{2}\colon K_{G}(X)\to K(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{F})
where we replace
F
by
G

(cf, (
11.2
)).
By repeating the argument of Lemma
11.2
with
F
replaced
by
G
(and
{\mathcal{C}}_{E^{n}_{F}}
replaced by the category having one object

G^{n+1}
and the identity map), we get the following.

Lemma 11.3
.

The diagram

(11.14)

\textstyle{K_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{G}\right)}

is commutative.

11.3.
Main result for special groups

We let
k
be any field and
G
any
k
-group.
We shall use the following lemma in the proofs of our main results.

Lemma 11.4
.

Given a proper map
f\colon Z\to X
and a flat
map
g\colon U\to X
in
{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
, the diagram of spectra

(11.15)

\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{Z}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G}(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{U}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(Z^{\bullet}_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(U^{\bullet}_{G})}

is commutative.

Proof.

We first note that
f_{*}
on the bottom level
is defined by
[
60
, Lem. 5.3]
.
The commutativity of the right square is clear because all face and
degeneracy maps of the bar
constructions are all smooth or regular closed immersions.
To see that the left square is also commutative,
we only need to observe that the square

(11.16)

\textstyle{Z^{\bullet}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}_{Z}}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}_{X}}
\textstyle{[Z/G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{[X/G]}

is levelwise Cartesian in which the vertical arrows are levelwise
smooth and the horizontal arrows are levelwise proper.
The desired commutativity therefore follows from
[
56
, Prop. 3.8]
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 11.5
.

Assume that
G
is special. Then for any
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
, the pull-back map

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X)\to K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G})
induces a weak equivalence of spectra

(11.17)

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G}).

Proof.

We first reduce the proof of the theorem to the case where
X
is a smooth
quasi-projective
k
-scheme using Noetherian induction.
If
X
is a
G
-quasi-projective
k
-scheme,
we can find a
G
-equivariant closed embedding

\iota\colon X\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}
, where
X^{\prime}
is smooth and
G
-quasi-projective.
We let
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}
be the open complement
and consider the diagram of spectra

(11.18)

\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G}(X^{\prime})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G}(U)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{U}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(X^{\prime\bullet}_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(U^{\bullet}_{G})}

which is commutative by Lemma
11.4
.

The top row of the above diagram is a homotopy fiber
sequence because it is obtained by
applying the derived completion functor to the localization sequence of equivariant

K^{\prime}
-theory. The bottom row is a homotopy fiber sequence because it is obtained by
applying the homotopy limit functor to the localization sequences of the ordinary

K^{\prime}
-theory. The middle and the right vertical arrows are weak equivalences
if the theorem is true for smooth
G
-quasi-projective
k
-schemes. It follows that

{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{X}}
is a weak equivalence.

We now suppose that
X
is an arbitrary object of
{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
. We can assume
X
to be
reduced. By
Lemma
6.2
, we can find a
G
-invariant dense open subspace

U\subset X
which is a
G
-quasi-projective
k
-scheme.
In particular, the assertion is true for
U
as shown above.
We now let
Z=X\setminus U

and repeat the above argument using the localization sequence and Noetherian
induction to conclude that
{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{X}}
is a weak equivalence. It remains now
to show that the theorem is true when
X
is a smooth and
G
-quasi-projective

k
-scheme. We assume this to be the case in the rest of the proof
and proceed as follows.

We look at the diagram

(11.19)

\textstyle{K_{G}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{*}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}}
\textstyle{K(X^{\bullet}_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}F\right)}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{F}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta^{*}_{F,X}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\phi^{X}_{F})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{K_{G}\left(E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)\right).}

Using (
11.8
) together with Lemmas
11.1
,

11.2
and
11.3
, it follows that this
diagram is commutative.

We next note that all arrows in the above diagram are induced by
G
-equivariant
maps between schemes with

G
-actions where
G
acts trivially on each of the spaces

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}F,\ X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{n}_{F},\ X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{G}
and
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{n}_{G}
.
In particular, all spectra on the bottom row are homotopy limits of

I_{G}
-complete
K(BG)
-module spectra.
It follows from Lemma
2.5
and Proposition
5.3
(7) that
each of the spectra on the bottom row is
I_{G}
-complete. By
(
11.5
), the same holds for
K(X^{\bullet}_{G})
. We thus get a
commutative diagram of
I_{G}
-complete spectra

(11.20)

\textstyle{K_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{*}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{*}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}}
\textstyle{K(X^{\bullet}_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}F\right)}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{F}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta^{*}_{F,X}}
\textstyle{K\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\phi^{X}_{F})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\phi}^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{K_{G}(E^{\bullet}_{G}(X)).}

The map
\pi^{*}_{X}
is a weak equivalence by (
11.1
).
The maps
(\phi^{X}_{F})^{*}
and
\overline{\phi}^{*}_{X}
are weak equivalences by
(
9.12
).
It follows from the proof of Proposition
9.3
that
\Delta^{*}_{F,X}
is a weak
equivalence. Finally, we note that
p^{*}_{X}
is the same map as the Atiyah-Segal
completion map
\Phi_{[X/G]}
of (
7.1
). In particular, it is a
weak equivalence by Theorem
7.5
. We conclude that
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}
is a weak
equivalence. This finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Remark 11.6
.

Using the fact that the Bott-inverted ordinary
K
-theory of smooth
k
-schemes
is representable in
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}(k)
(e.g., see
[
3
]
),
one easily checks from its proof that Theorem
11.5
also
holds for the Bott-inverted equivariant
K^{\prime}
-theory with finite coefficients
(prime to
{\rm char}(k)
) if
k
contains all roots of unity.

12.
Thomason’s problem for other groups

In this section, we shall extend Theorem
11.5
to other
k
-groups.
For equivariant
K
-theory of spaces with group actions, this is usually
done by reducing to the case of special groups via Morita equivalence.
However, one can not expect a version of Morita equivalence for the integral

K
-theory of the bar construction on account of its failure to
satisfy étale descent. We shall show however that Morita equivalence holds for
the rationalized
K
-theory of the bar construction and use this to deduce
Theorem
11.5
for other groups. Note that one can’t expect
Theorem
11.5
to hold for all
k
-groups with integral coefficients
due to the failure of Galois descent for
K
-theory of fields.

12.1.
Morita equivalence for
K
-theory of bar construction

Let
k
be a field and
G
a
k
-group. We let
p\colon G\times Y\to Y
be the
trivial
G
-torsor over
Y\in{\mathbf{Spc}}_{k}
and let
X=G\times Y
. We let
X^{\bullet}_{G}

denote the bar construction for the
G
-action on
X
where
G
acts on itself
by left multiplication and trivially on
Y
. The projection map
p

defines an augmentation
p\colon X^{\bullet}_{G}\to Y
of the simplicial algebraic
space
X^{\bullet}_{G}
. We refer the reader to
[
5
]
for the homotopic terminology
that we shall use about simplicial objects in any category.

Lemma 12.1
.

There is a simplicial morphism
q\colon Y\to X^{\bullet}_{G}
such that
p\circ q={\operatorname{id}}_{Y}
and
q\circ p
is homotopic to
{\operatorname{id}}_{X^{\bullet}_{G}}
.

Proof.

Letting
q_{n}(y)=(e,y)
where
e
is the identity element of
G^{n+1}
, it is
clear that
q
is a simplicial map such that
p\circ q={\operatorname{id}}_{Y}
and

q_{n}=(s^{0})^{n}\circ q_{0}
for
n\geq 0
, where
s^{0}
is the zeroth degeneracy map
of
X^{\bullet}_{G}
. To construct
a simplicial homotopy between
q\circ p
and
{\operatorname{id}}_{X^{\bullet}_{G}}
, we note that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{1}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{0}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}

is a coequalizer diagram. By
[
5
, Thm. 4.3]
therefore, it suffices to
show that
X^{\bullet}_{G}
admits an extra degeneracy. But we leave it for the reader
to check that the map

(12.1)

s_{n}\colon G^{n}\times X\cong G^{n}\times(G\times Y)\to G^{n+1}\times(G\times Y)\cong G^{n+1}\times X;

\ \ s_{n}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n},g_{0},y)=(g_{0},g_{1},\ldots,g_{n},e,y);\ n\geq 0

is the required extra degeneracy.
∎

Lemma 12.2
.

Let
p\colon X=G\times Y\to Y
be the trivial
G
-torsor as above. Then the
flat pull-back
p^{*}\colon K^{\prime}(Y)\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[n]\in\Delta}K^{\prime}(G^{n}\times X)
is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

By Lemma
12.1
, there are morphisms of simplicial algebraic spaces

Y\xrightarrow{q}X^{\bullet}_{G}\xrightarrow{p}Y
such that
p\circ q={\operatorname{id}}_{Y}
and

q\circ p
is homotopic to
{\operatorname{id}}_{X^{\bullet}_{G}}
. Applying the
K^{\prime}
-theory functor,
we get morphisms of cosimplicial spectra
K^{\prime}(Y)\xrightarrow{p^{*}}K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G})\xrightarrow{q^{*}}K^{\prime}(Y)
such that
q^{*}\circ p^{*}
is identity and
p^{*}\circ q^{*}

is homotopic to identity
(see the last part of the proof of
[
85
, Lem 2.9]
).
It follows that the induced map between the homotopy
limits
p^{*}\colon K^{\prime}(Y)\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[n]\in\Delta}K^{\prime}(G^{n}\times X)
is a weak equivalence.
∎

Lemma 12.3
.

Let
p\colon X\to Y
be any
G
-torsor over
Y\in{\mathbf{Spc}}_{k}
. Then the pull-back
map
p^{*}\colon K^{\prime}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[n]\in\Delta}K^{\prime}(G^{n}\times X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is a
weak equivalence. If
{\rm char}(k)\nmid m
and
k
contains all roots of unity,
then the pull-back map

p^{*}\colon K^{\prime}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)[\beta^{-1}]\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[n]\in\Delta}(K^{\prime}(G^{n}\times X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)[\beta^{-1}])

is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

We can find an étale cover
f\colon Y^{\prime}\to Y
such that the pull-back

p^{\prime}\colon X\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
is a trivial
G
-torsor. We let

X^{\prime}=X\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
and let
f^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be the projection.
It is straightforward to check that the map
G^{n}\times X^{\prime}\to G^{n}\times X

is an étale cover for every
n\geq 0
.
We let
C_{\bullet}(Y^{\prime})
(resp.
C_{\bullet}(G^{n}\times X^{\prime})
) denote the C̆ech
nerve of the étale cover
Y^{\prime}\to Y
(resp.
G^{n}\times X^{\prime}\to G^{n}\times X
).
We then get a commutative diagram of bisimplicial algebraic spaces

(12.2)

\textstyle{C_{\bullet}(X^{\prime\bullet}_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{p^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{C_{\bullet}(Y^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y.}

Applying the
K^{\prime}
-theory functor, we get a commutative diagram of spectra

(12.3)

\textstyle{K^{\prime}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[n]\in\Delta}K^{\prime}(G^{n}\times X,{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[m]\in\Delta}K^{\prime}(Y^{\prime m},{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\prime*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[n]\in\Delta}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[m]\in\Delta}K^{\prime}(G^{n}\times X^{\prime m},{\mathbb{Q}}),}

where
G^{n}\times X^{\prime m}
(resp.
Y^{\prime m}
) is the
m
-th level of

C_{\bullet}(G^{n}\times X^{\prime})
(resp.
C_{\bullet}(Y^{\prime})
).
By
[
84
, Thm. 2.15, Cor. 2.16]
, the rationalized
K^{\prime}
-theory satisfies
étale descent for schemes. Since an algebraic space has a dense
open subspace which is a scheme, it follows from the localization theorem for

K^{\prime}
-theory that the rationalized
K^{\prime}
-theory satisfies étale descent for
algebraic spaces (cf.
[
56
, Thm. 5.1]
). In particular, the
left vertical arrow in (
12.3
) is a weak equivalence.
By the same reason, the map
K^{\prime}(G^{n}\times X,{\mathbb{Q}})\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[m]\in\Delta}K^{\prime}(G^{n}\times X^{\prime m},{\mathbb{Q}})
is a weak equivalence for every
n\geq 0
.
It follows by
[
43
, Thm. 11.4]
and
[
47
, § 6]
that
the right vertical arrow in (
12.3
) is also a weak equivalence.

We next note that on one hand, the spectrum on the bottom right corner of
(
12.3
) is canonically weakly equivalent to

\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[m]\in\Delta}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[n]\in\Delta}K^{\prime}(G^{n}\times X^{\prime m},{\mathbb{Q}})
by

[
17
, § 31.5]
. On the other hand, the map

K^{\prime}(Y^{\prime m},{\mathbb{Q}})\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{[n]\in\Delta}K^{\prime}(G^{n}\times X^{\prime m},{\mathbb{Q}})
is a weak
equivalence for every
m\geq 0

by Lemma
12.2
since
X^{\prime m}\to Y^{\prime m}
is a trivial

G
-torsor. It follows again by
[
43
, Thm. 11.4]
and

[
47
, § 6]
that the bottom horizontal arrow in (
12.3
)
is a weak equivalence. We conclude that the top horizontal arrow is also a weak
equivalence. This finishes the proof of the first part of the lemma.

The proof of the second part is identical using the fact that the Bott-periodic

K^{\prime}
-theory with finite coefficients (prime to
{\rm char}(k)
)
satisfies étale descent by

[
84
]
in a special case and
[
66
, Cor. 13.1]

in the general case (see also
[
3
]
).
∎

Lemma 12.4
.

Let
H\subset G
be a closed subgroup and
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{H}_{k}
. Let

Y=X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle H}}{{\times}}G
be the associated Morita space.
Let
G^{\prime}=G\times H
act on
X^{\prime}=X\times G
by

(g,h)\star(x,g^{\prime})=(hx,gg^{\prime}h^{-1})
. Then the projection map

p_{1}\colon X^{\prime}\to X
and the
H
-torsor map
p_{2}\colon X^{\prime}\to Y
induce
weak equivalences of spectra

(12.4)

p^{*}_{1}\colon K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{H},{\mathbb{Q}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}K^{\prime}(X^{\prime\bullet}_{G^{\prime}},{\mathbb{Q}})\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ p^{*}_{2}\colon K^{\prime}(Y^{\bullet}_{G},{\mathbb{Q}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}K^{\prime}(X^{\prime\bullet}_{G^{\prime}},{\mathbb{Q}}).

If
{\rm char}(k)\nmid m
and
k
contains all roots of unity, then the same is also
true for the Bott-periodic
K^{\prime}
-theory
K^{\prime}(-,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)[\beta^{-1}]
.

Proof.

For
p,q\geq 0
, we let
A_{p,q}=X\times G^{p+1}\times H^{q}
.
We let the face maps
d^{I}_{i}\colon A_{p,q}\to A_{p-1,q}
and

d^{II}_{i}\colon A_{p,q}\to A_{p,q-1}
be given by

(12.5)

d^{I}_{i}(x,g_{0},\ldots,g_{p},h_{1},\ldots,h_{q})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(x,g_{0},\ldots,g_{i-1},g_{i+1}g_{i},g_{i+2},\ldots,g_{p},h_{1},\ldots,h_{q})&\mbox{if $0\leq i<p$}\\
(x,g_{0},\ldots,g_{p-1},h_{1},\ldots,h_{q})&\mbox{if $i=p$.}\end{array}\right.

d^{II}_{i}(x,g_{0},\ldots,g_{p},h_{1},\ldots,h_{q})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(h_{1}x,g_{0}h^{-1}_{1},g_{1},\ldots,g_{p},h_{2},\ldots,h_{q})&\mbox{if $i=0$}\\
(x,g_{0},\ldots,g_{p},h_{1},\ldots,h_{i-1},h_{i+1}h_{i},\ldots,h_{q})&\mbox{if $0<i<q$}\\
(x,g_{0},\ldots,g_{p},h_{1},\ldots,h_{q-1})&\mbox{if $i=q$.}\end{array}\right.

The degeneracy maps
s^{I}_{i}\colon A_{p,q}\to A_{p+1,q}
and

s^{II}_{i}\colon A_{p,q}\to A_{p,q+1}
are given by

(12.6)

s^{I}_{i}(x,g_{0},\ldots,g_{p},h_{1},\ldots,h_{q})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(x,g_{0},\ldots,g_{i},e,g_{i+1},\ldots,g_{p},h_{1},\ldots,h_{q})&\mbox{if $0\leq i<p$}\\
(x,g_{0},\ldots,g_{p},e,h_{1},\ldots,h_{q})&\mbox{if $i=p$.}\end{array}\right.

s^{II}_{i}(x,g_{0},\ldots,g_{p},h_{1},\ldots,h_{q})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(x,g_{0},\ldots,g_{p},e,h_{1},\ldots,h_{q})&\mbox{if $i=0$}\\
(x,g_{0},\ldots,g_{p},h_{1},\ldots h_{i},e,h_{i+1},\ldots,h_{q})&\mbox{if $0<i<q$}\\
(x,g_{0},\ldots,g_{p},h_{1},\ldots,h_{q},e)&\mbox{if $i=q$.}\end{array}\right.

It is a straightforward verification that
A_{\bullet,\bullet}=\{A_{p,q}\}_{p,q\geq 0}

together with the above face and degeneracy maps is a bisimplicial algebraic space.
Note that one only needs to check the commutativity between
d^{I}_{0}
and
d^{II}_{0}
as
the other identities are automatic by the above definition.

We next note that the maps
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
define augmentation maps

\epsilon_{1}\colon A_{\bullet,\bullet}\to X^{\bullet}_{H}
and
\epsilon_{2}\colon A_{\bullet,\bullet}\to Y^{\bullet}_{G}
such that for each
q\geq 0
, the map

\epsilon_{1}\colon A_{\bullet,q}\to X\times H^{q}
coincides with the augmentation

(G\times(X\times H^{q}))^{\bullet}_{G}\to X\times H^{q}
corresponding to the trivial

G
-torsor
G\times(X\times H^{q})\to X\times H^{q}
.
Furthermore, the map
\epsilon_{1}\colon A_{0,\bullet}\to X^{\bullet}_{H}
is the
map induced between the bar constructions by the
H
-equivariant map

G\times X\to X
.
On the other side, the map
\epsilon_{2}\colon A_{p,\bullet}\to G^{p}\times Y

coincides with the augmentation

(G^{p}\times(X\times G))^{\bullet}_{H}\to G^{p}\times Y
corresponding to the
H
-torsor

G^{p}\times(X\times G)\to G^{p}\times Y
. Furthermore, the map

\epsilon_{2}\colon A_{\bullet,0}\to Y^{\bullet}_{G}
is the map induced between the bar
constructions by the
G
-equivariant map
G\times X\to Y
.
We now apply Lemma
12.3
to conclude that there are weak equivalences
of spectra

(12.7)

\epsilon^{*}_{1}\colon K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{H},{\mathbb{Q}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{p,q}K^{\prime}(A_{p,q},{\mathbb{Q}})\xleftarrow{\simeq}K^{\prime}(Y^{\bullet}_{G},{\mathbb{Q}})\colon\epsilon^{*}_{2}.

On the other hand, if we let
K_{p,q}=K^{\prime}(A_{p,q},{\mathbb{Q}})
with the coface and
codegeneracy
maps induced by those of
A_{\bullet,\bullet}
and let
\Delta_{K}=\{K_{p,p}\}_{p\geq 0}

denote the diagonal of the bicosimplicial spectrum
K_{\bullet,\bullet}
, then the
left homotopy cofinality of the diagonal
inclusion
\Delta_{K}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2}_{K}
implies
that this map
induces a weak equivalence on the homotopy limits
(cf.
[
43
, Thm. 13.7]
,
[
44
, Prop. 7.11]
).
We thus get weak equivalences

(12.8)

\epsilon^{*}_{1}\colon K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{H},{\mathbb{Q}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{p}K^{\prime}(A_{p,p},{\mathbb{Q}})\xleftarrow{\simeq}K^{\prime}(Y^{\bullet}_{G},{\mathbb{Q}})\colon\epsilon^{*}_{2}.

This implies (
12.4
) and concludes the proof of the first part of
the lemma because
\Delta_{K}
is nothing but the cosimplicial spectrum

[p]\mapsto K^{\prime}(X^{\prime}\times G^{\prime p},{\mathbb{Q}})
obtained by applying the
K^{\prime}
-theory functor on
the bar construction associated to the
G^{\prime}
-action on
X^{\prime}
.
The proof of the second part is identical.
∎

12.2.
Main result for other groups

The following result extends Theorem
11.5
to all groups if we use
rational coefficients. We fix a field
k
.

Theorem 12.5
.

Let
G
be a
k
-group and
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
. Then the
map
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\to K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G},{\mathbb{Q}})
induces a weak
equivalence of spectra

(12.9)

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G},{\mathbb{Q}}).

If
k
contains all roots of unity, then the same is also
true for the Bott-periodic
K^{\prime}
-theory
K^{\prime}(-;{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)[\beta^{-1}]
for every
integer
m
invertible in
k
.

Proof.

We choose a closed embedding
G\hookrightarrow Q
where
Q
is a general linear
k
-group and
let
G^{\prime}=Q\times G
. We let
Y
be the Morita space
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}Q
and

X^{\prime}=X\times Q
with the action of
G^{\prime}
as described in
Lemma
12.4
. Following the notations of that lemma, we let

D_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow A_{\bullet,\bullet}
denote the diagonal simplicial algebraic space
and consider the commutative diagram

(12.10)

\textstyle{X{\times}E^{\bullet}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{G}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}{\times}E^{\bullet}_{G^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{1}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G^{\prime}}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{G^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{D_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{2}}
\textstyle{A_{\bullet,\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{2}}
\textstyle{Y{\times}E^{\bullet}_{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{Y^{\bullet}_{Q},}

in which
\theta_{i},\ \theta^{\prime}_{i}
and
\delta_{i}:=\epsilon_{i}\circ\delta
are
morphisms of simplicial spaces and
\epsilon_{i}
is a morphism of bisimplicial
spaces for
i=1,2
. One also checks that
\theta_{i}
is
G^{\prime}
-equivariant for

i=1,2
, where
G^{\prime}
acts on
X{\times}E^{\bullet}_{G}
via
G^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow G
and
on
Y{\times}E^{\bullet}_{Q}
via
G^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow Q
.

Applying the
K^{\prime}
-theory functor, we get a commutative diagram of spectra

(12.11)

\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G}(X{\times}E^{\bullet}_{G},{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{*}_{1}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}\left(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{G},{\mathbb{Q}}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime*}_{1}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G},{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{*}_{1}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}{\times}E^{\bullet}_{G^{\prime}},{\mathbb{Q}})}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}\left(X^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G^{\prime}}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{G^{\prime}},{\mathbb{Q}}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(D_{\bullet},{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(A_{\bullet,\bullet},{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{*}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{Q}(Y{\times}E^{\bullet}_{Q},{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{*}_{2}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}\left(Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q}}{{\times}}E^{\bullet}_{Q},{\mathbb{Q}}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime*}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(Y^{\bullet}_{Q},{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{*}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{2}}

The arrows
\delta^{*}_{i},\epsilon^{*}_{i}
and
\delta^{*}
in (
12.11
)
are weak equivalences by Lemma
12.4
.
In particular,
\theta^{\prime*}_{i}
and
\theta^{*}_{i}
are weak equivalences.
Furthermore, each arrow

\theta^{*}_{i}
(hence
\theta^{\prime*}_{i}
) is
K(BG^{\prime})
-linear as
\theta_{i}
is

G^{\prime}
-equivariant. We thus get a commutative diagram of
K(BG^{\prime})
-linear maps
of
K(BG^{\prime})
-module spectra

(12.12)

\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime*}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G^{\prime}}(X^{\prime},{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{Q}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
