\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime*}_{2}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G},{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime*}_{1}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(X^{\prime\bullet}_{G^{\prime}},{\mathbb{Q}})}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(Y^{\bullet}_{Q},{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime*}_{2}}

in which the horizontal arrows on the bottom row are weak equivalences.
The horizontal arrows on the top row are weak equivalences
because the maps of stacks
[{X^{\prime}}/{G^{\prime}}]\to[X/G]
and
[{X^{\prime}}/{G^{\prime}}]\to[Y/Q]

are isomorphisms (cf. Lemma
6.1
).

As all arrows in
(
12.12
) are
K(BG^{\prime})
-linear and we showed in the
proof of Theorem
11.5
that all terms on the bottom row are

I_{G^{\prime}}
-complete, we obtain a commutative diagram of
I_{G^{\prime}}
-complete
spectra

(12.13)

\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G^{\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime*}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{G^{\prime}}(X^{\prime},{\mathbb{Q}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G^{\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}_{Q}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G^{\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime*}_{2}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(X^{\bullet}_{G},{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime*}_{1}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(X^{\prime\bullet}_{G^{\prime}},{\mathbb{Q}})}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}(Y^{\bullet}_{Q},{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime*}_{2}}

in which all horizontal arrows are weak equivalences.

It follows from
[
29
, Cor. 6.1]
that
I_{G}=\sqrt{I_{G^{\prime}}R(G)}
and

I_{Q}=\sqrt{I_{G^{\prime}}R(Q)}
. On the other hand,
I_{G^{\prime}}
is the radical of a
finitely generated ideal by Lemma
2.5
. It follows from
Proposition
5.3
(5) that the canonical maps

K^{\prime}_{G}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G^{\prime}}}\to K^{\prime}_{G}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}

and
K^{\prime}_{Q}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G^{\prime}}}\to K^{\prime}_{Q}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{Q}}
are weak equivalences.
In particular,
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{Y}
is a weak equivalence by Theorem
11.5
.
A diagram chase now shows that
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{X^{\prime}}
and
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}_{X}
are
also weak equivalences. Equivalently, the map in (
12.9
)
is a weak equivalence. This concludes the proof of the first part of the theorem
and the proof of the second part is identical using Remark
11.6
.
∎

Corollary 12.6
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
7.6
, the following hold.

(1)

If
G
is special, then the map

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon KH_{G}(X)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to KH(X^{\bullet}_{G})

is a weak equivalence.

(2)

If
G
is arbitrary, then the map

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon KH_{G}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to KH(X^{\bullet}_{G},{\mathbb{Q}})

is a weak equivalence.

(3)

If
G
is arbitrary,
k
contains all roots of unity and
m\in k^{\times}
, then
the map

\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\colon K_{G}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)[\beta^{-1}]^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to K(X^{\bullet}_{G},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)[\beta^{-1}]

is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

Using Theorems
11.5
and
12.5
,
we can repeat the arguments of Theorem
7.6
verbatim to deduce this
corollary.
∎

Corollary 12.7
.

Assume that
k
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero.
Let
G
be a
k
-group
and
g\in G_{s}(k)
. Let
\Psi\subset G(k)
be the conjugacy class of
g
. For
any
X\in{\mathbf{Spc}}^{G}_{k}
, we then have a weak equivalence of spectra

(12.14)

\Theta^{g}_{X}\colon K^{\prime}_{G}(X,k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K^{\prime}((X^{g})^{\bullet}_{Z_{g}},k).

Proof.

We use the maps

K^{\prime}_{G}(X,k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\xrightarrow{\Psi^{g}_{X}}K^{\prime}_{Z_{g}}(X^{g},k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{g}}\xrightarrow{t_{g^{-1}}}K^{\prime}_{Z_{g}}(X^{g},k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{Z_{g}}}\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\pi}^{*}}K^{\prime}((X^{g})^{\bullet}_{Z_{g}},k),

where
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}
is as in (
9.4
) with
X
(resp.
G
)
substituted by
X^{g}
(resp.
Z_{g}
).
The first two maps were shown to be weak equivalences in Theorem
8.12

and the last map is a weak equivalence by applying Theorem
12.5

to the
Z_{g}
-action on
X^{g}
and noting that the theorem is valid if we replace

{\mathbb{Q}}
by any field of characteristic zero.
∎

Remark 12.8
.

One easily checks that Corollary
12.7
also holds for
KH
-theory
of singular
k
-schemes under the additional hypotheses of Theorem
7.6
.

Proof of Corollary
1.7
:

Under our assumptions, the canonical maps
K_{G}(X;{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n)\to KH_{G}(X;{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n)

and
K(X_{G};{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n)\to KH(X_{G};{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n)
are weak equivalences by

[
50
, Cor. 3.17, Prop. 7.15]
.
The result therefore follows from Theorem
7.6
and Corollary
12.6
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.3
.

If
f\colon X\to Y
is an
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-weak equivalence, then
({\operatorname{id}}\times f)\colon G^{n}\times X\to G^{n}\times Y
are also
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-weak equivalences for all
n
by

[
72
, Lem. 3.2.13]
. It follows that the induced maps

K(G^{n}\times Y)\to K(G^{n}\times X)
are weak equivalences for all
n
. As they are
clearly compatible with the face and degeneracy maps of
X^{\bullet}_{G}
and
Y^{\bullet}_{G}
,
we can pass to the limits to conclude that the map

f^{*}\colon K(Y^{\bullet}_{G})\to K(X^{\bullet}_{G})
is a weak equivalence.
We now apply Theorems
11.5
and
12.5
to
finish the proof.
∎

13.
Completion of Hochschild homology

Our goal in the next few sections is to prove the analogue of the
completion theorem (cf. Corollary
12.7
)
for the equivariant homology theories. As in the case of
K
-theory, the key
step is Thomason’s theorem for completion of the homology theories
at the augmentation ideal. For Hochschild homology,
this can be achieved by means of the formal HKR theorem of Ben-Zvi and
Nadler
[
7
]
, as we shall show in this section.
However, the corresponding result for cyclic homology is very delicate
because it involves interchanging homotopy limit and colimit.
We shall study this question in the next section.

We fix once and for all an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic zero.
We shall follow the notations of §
3
throughout our discussion of
homology theories. In particular, we shall consider all functors on sheaves to be
derived. We shall use homological notations for chain complexes.

13.1.
I_{G}
-completion of Hochschild homology

For
{\mathcal{X}}\in{\rm DSt}_{k}
, we let
{\mathbb{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
denote the cotangent complex of
{\mathcal{X}}

and let

{\mathbb{T}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}[-1]={\rm Spec\,}({\rm Sym}^{\bullet}_{{\mathcal{X}}}({\mathbb{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}[1]))
denote the odd
tangent bundle of
{\mathcal{X}}
with the projection
t\colon{\mathbb{T}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}[-1]\to{\mathcal{X}}

(cf.
[
7
, § 1.2.1]
). If
{\mathcal{X}}=[X/G]
where

G
is a
k
-group acting on a smooth
k
-scheme
X
, then
{\mathbb{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
is
given by the complex
(\Omega^{1}_{X/k}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
of

G
-equivariant coherent sheaves on
X
with Cartan differential,
where
\mathfrak{g}
is the Lie algebra of
G
(cf.
[
18
, Exm. 2.1.10]
)
and
\Omega^{1}_{X/k}
is placed in degree zero.
The augmentation of
{\rm Sym}^{\bullet}_{{\mathcal{X}}}({\mathbb{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}[1])
defines the
zero section
e\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{T}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}[-1]
of the projection
t
.

For a derived stack
{\mathcal{X}}
and a closed substack
{\mathcal{Z}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}
, let

\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}
denote the formal completion of
{\mathcal{X}}
along
{\mathcal{Z}}
(cf.

[
34
, § 6]
). It is an ind-stack such that for

S\in{\bf{Aff}}_{k}
, the space

\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(S)
consists of maps
S\to{\mathcal{X}}
of presheaves on
{\bf{Aff}}_{k}

such that its restriction to

\pi_{0}(S)_{\rm red}
factors through the inclusion
{\mathcal{Z}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}
.
Let
\widehat{{\mathbb{T}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}[-1]
(resp.
\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}
)
denote the formal completion of
{\mathbb{T}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}[-1]
(resp.
{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}
) along its
zero-section (resp. identity section).
The derived Hochschild-Kostant-Rosenberg (HKR) theorem for stacks then
provides a natural morphism
\exp\colon\widehat{{\mathbb{T}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}[-1]\to\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}
which
restricts to an isomorphism between the zero-section

e({\mathcal{X}})\hookrightarrow\widehat{{\mathbb{T}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}[-1]
and the identity section
s({\mathcal{X}})\hookrightarrow\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}

(cf.
[
7
, § 6]
).

From now on in §
13
, we shall assume that

G
is a reductive
k
-group acting on a smooth
k
-scheme
X
. We let

{\mathcal{X}}=[X/G]

denote the stack quotient and let
\pi\colon X\to{\mathcal{X}}
be the quotient map.
Let
p\colon U\to{\mathcal{X}}
be a smooth surjective morphism
where
U\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
. Since
{\mathcal{X}}
has
affine diagonal,
p
is representable by schemes. We let
U_{\bullet}
denote the
C̆ech nerve of
p
and let
p_{\bullet}\colon U_{\bullet}\to{\mathcal{X}}
be the resulting
map of simplicial stacks. If we take
p
to be the quotient map
X\to{\mathcal{X}}
, then

U_{\bullet}
coincides with
X^{\bullet}_{G}
of (
9.1
).
Since
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}})
is a module over the ring spectrum
HH(BG)\simeq{\mathcal{O}}({{\mathcal{L}}}(BG))
,
it follows from Theorem
3.3
that it is a module over the ring
spectrum
K(BG)
. In particular, it has an action of the ring
R_{k}(G)=\pi_{0}(K(BG,k))
. The first step to prove the completion theorem for the Hochschild
homology of
{\mathcal{X}}
is the following.

Lemma 13.1
.

The canonical pull-back map
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}})\to{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}})
induces a weak
equivalence

{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}).

Proof.

We let
A
be a simplicial commutative
k
-algebra and let

S={\rm Spec\,}(A)
be the unique object of
{\bf{Aff}}_{k}
defined by
A
.
Let
u\colon S\to{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}
be a morphism of derived stacks and consider the
diagram

(13.1)

\textstyle{S\times_{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}}{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{s^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{[{{\rm Spec\,}(k(e))}/G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{{\rm Spec\,}(k(e))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{L}}}BG\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{[G/G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{{\rm Spec\,}(C[G])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{{\rm Spec\,}(R_{k}(G)),}

where
e\in G
is the identity element and
[G/G]
is the stack quotient for the
adjoint action of
G
on itself.

The arrow
v
is the affinization morphism (cf.
[
7
, § 3]
)
and the bottom horizontal arrow on the right is an isomorphism by
Proposition
5.5
(5). All other horizontal arrows are canonical maps.
The vertical arrows are canonical inclusions of closed substacks.
The right square is Cartesian because

v
is the coarse moduli space for the adjoint action, the middle square is
Cartesian by definition of the map
s
and the left square is Cartesian by
construction. It follows that the outer trapezium is Cartesian.

If we let
w\colon S\to{\rm Spec\,}(R_{k}(G))
denote the composite bottom horizontal
arrow in (
13.1
) and
W=S\times_{{\rm Spec\,}(R_{k}(G))}{\rm Spec\,}(k(e))
, it
follows from above that

u^{-1}(|{\mathcal{X}}|)=|W|
as topological spaces. But this implies by definition
of the formal completion (cf.
[
34
, § 6]
) that the
square

(13.2)

\textstyle{\widehat{S}_{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}}

is homotopy Cartesian.

We now compute

(13.3)

\begin{array}[]{lll}q_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}})(S)&\simeq&u^{*}q_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}})(S)\\
&\simeq&q^{\prime}_{*}u^{\prime*}({\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}})(S)\\
&\simeq&{\rm Map}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{S}}({\mathcal{O}}_{S},q^{\prime}_{*}u^{\prime*}({\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}}))\\
&{\simeq}^{\dagger}&{\rm Map}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{S}_{W}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{S}_{W}},u^{\prime*}({\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}}))\\
&\simeq&{\rm Map}_{\widehat{S}_{W}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{S}_{W}},{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{S}_{W}})\\
&\simeq&A^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}={\mathcal{O}}(S)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}},\end{array}

where
{\rm Map}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{S}}(-,-)
is the mapping space between sheaves of
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules
and the weak equivalence
{\simeq}^{\dagger}
follows by the adjointness.

We finally compute

(13.4)

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}})=\Gamma(\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}})&\simeq&\Gamma({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}},q_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}}))\\
&=&{\underset{S\in{\bf{Aff}}_{k},S\to{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}}{\mathop{{\rm holim}}}}q_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}})(S)\\
&{\simeq}^{1}&{\underset{S\in{\bf{Aff}}_{k},S\to{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}}{\mathop{{\rm holim}}}}{\mathcal{O}}(S)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\\
&\simeq&\left({\underset{S\in{\bf{Aff}}_{k},S\to{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}}{\mathop{{\rm holim}}}}{\mathcal{O}}(S)\right)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\\
&\simeq&{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}},\end{array}

where
{\simeq}^{1}
follows from (
13.3
). This finishes the proof.
∎

We now look at the commutative diagram

(13.5)

\textstyle{{{\mathbb{T}}}_{U_{\bullet}}[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm exp}_{1}}
\textstyle{\widehat{{\mathbb{T}}}_{U_{\bullet}}[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm exp}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}U_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{L}}}U_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{{\mathbb{T}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm exp}_{3}}
\textstyle{\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}},}

where the horizontal arrows in the lower square on the right as well as the map

\alpha
are induced by the completions and the
vertical arrows are induced by
p_{\bullet}\colon U_{\bullet}\to{\mathcal{X}}

associated to the smooth surjective map
p\colon U\to{\mathcal{X}}
. The lower left square
is commutative by the naturality of the derived HKR theorem.

We now note that each
{{\mathbb{T}}}_{U_{n}}[-1]
is complete because

{\rm Sym}^{\bullet}_{{\mathcal{X}}}({\mathbb{L}}_{U_{n}}[1])
is
a bounded complex. By passing to the limit, it follows that
\alpha
is an
equivalence.
The map
{\rm exp}_{1}
in (
13.5
)
is an equivalence by the well known HKR theorem for smooth

k
-schemes while
{\rm exp}_{2}
and
{\rm exp}_{3}
are equivalences by the formal HKR
theorem of Ben-Zvi and Nadler (cf.
[
7
, Thm. 6.9]
).
It follows that
q_{\bullet}
is an equivalence.
In other words,
{{\mathcal{L}}}U_{\bullet}
is complete with
respect to its identity section
U_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow{{\mathcal{L}}}U_{\bullet}
.
In particular, the map

(13.6)

{\mathcal{O}}({{\mathcal{L}}}U_{\bullet})\simeq\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\mathcal{O}}({{\mathcal{L}}}U_{n})\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}U_{n})\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}U_{\bullet})

is an equivalence.

On the other hand, a computation similar to
(
13.4
) (substitute
U_{n}
for
{\mathcal{X}}
) shows that
{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}U_{n})

is
I_{G}
-complete as an
R_{k}(G)
-module.
We conclude that the pull-back map
p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon{\mathcal{O}}({{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}})\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\mathcal{O}}({{\mathcal{L}}}U_{n})
factors through
{\mathcal{O}}({{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}
.
Using (
3.12
), it follows that the pull-back map

p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon HH({\mathcal{X}})\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HH(U_{n})
factors through the
I_{G}
-completion
of
HH({\mathcal{X}})
to induce a canonical map

(13.7)

p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon HH({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HH(U_{n}).

This is the analogue for the Hochschild homology of Thomason’s completion map
(
9.4
) in
K
-theory.

We let

(13.8)

F(U_{\bullet})=\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}F(U_{n})\ \ \mbox{for}\ \ F\in\{HH,HC,HC^{-},HP\}.

Lemma 13.2
.

The
S^{1}
-action on
HH({\mathcal{X}})
induces an
S^{1}
-action on
HH({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}

such that the completion map
HH({\mathcal{X}})\to HH({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}
as well as

p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon HH({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to HH(U_{\bullet})
is
S^{1}
-equivariant.

Proof.

To see that the
S^{1}
-action on
HH({\mathcal{X}})
carries over to
HH({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}
,
note that the
S^{1}
-action on
HH({\mathcal{X}})
is equivalent to the structure of
a
\Lambda
-algebra on it (cf.
[
7
, Cor. 3.14]
)
and there are morphisms of

\Lambda
-algebras
R_{k}(G)\simeq HH(BG)\to HH({\mathcal{X}})
. In particular,
multiplication by any element of
R_{k}(G)\cong\pi_{0}(HH(BG))
is

\Lambda
-linear (in fact,
HH({\mathcal{X}})
-linear).
By using this observation repeatedly, we see that
the
S^{1}
-action of
HH({\mathcal{X}})
descends to compatible action of
S^{1}
on quotients

{HH({\mathcal{X}})}/{(x^{i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,x^{i_{r}}_{r})HH({\mathcal{X}})}

as
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
varies over the
r
-tuples of positive integers,
where
I_{G}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
(cf. Proposition
2.3
). Passing to the
limit over these
r
-tuples and using (
5.1
), we get the first
part of the lemma. The
S^{1}
-equivariance of
p^{*}_{\bullet}
now easily follows
using that each
HH(U_{n})
is
I_{G}
-complete.
∎

The following result is the analogue
of Theorem
12.5
for Hochschild homology. One difference though is
that the result for Hochschild homology is more general in that the map

U\to{\mathcal{X}}
is allowed
to be any smooth cover by a scheme (thanks to the smooth descent
property of the odd tangent bundle) and not just the quotient map
X\to{\mathcal{X}}

(in which case
U_{\bullet}=X^{\bullet}_{G}
).

Proposition 13.3
.

The map
p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon HH({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to HH(U_{\bullet})
is a weak
equivalence.

Proof.

Using (
13.4
) and (
13.6
), the problem is
equivalent to showing that the map
p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}})\to{\mathcal{O}}({{\mathcal{L}}}U_{\bullet})
induces a weak equivalence

p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}})\to{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}U_{\bullet})
.
To that end, we use the left square of (
13.5
)
to get a commutative
diagram

(13.9)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathcal{X}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm exp}^{*}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{T}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}[-1])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}U_{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm exp}^{*}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{T}}}_{U_{\bullet}}[-1]).}

The horizontal arrows are weak equivalences by the formal HKR theorem
(cf.
[
7
, Thm. 6.9]
) while the right vertical arrow is a weak
equivalence
by the smooth descent for the completed odd tangent bundle of smooth stacks
(cf. [op. cit., Prop. 6.4]). This forces the left vertical arrow to be a weak
equivalence.
∎

13.2.
I_{G}
-completion of negative cyclic homology

We shall now use Proposition
13.3
to prove a similar result for
the negative cyclic homology of
{\mathcal{X}}
.

Corollary 13.4
.

The pull-back map
p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon HC^{-}({\mathcal{X}})\to HC^{-}(U_{\bullet})
induces
a weak equivalence

p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon HC^{-}({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}HC^{-}(U_{\bullet}).

Proof.

Recall that a
\Lambda
-module structure on a complex
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{k}
is
the same thing as an
S^{1}
-action on the Eilenberg-MacLane spectrum of

{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
, which in turn is the same thing as a functor

{\mathcal{F}}\colon BS^{1}\to{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}
, where
BS^{1}
is the classifying category of the
topological group
S^{1}

(cf.
[
73
, § I.1]
). In this case,
|{\mathcal{F}}|^{hS^{1}}
coincides with
the homotopy limit
\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{S^{1}}{\mathcal{F}}
of the functor
{\mathcal{F}}
.
We now let
{\mathcal{C}}
be the category of
r
-tuples of positive integers

(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
with the unique map

(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})\to(j_{1},\ldots,j_{r})
if
i_{m}\geq j_{m}
for all
m
.
Then we have a natural transformation

(13.10)

p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon\frac{HH({\mathcal{X}})}{(x^{i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,x^{i_{r}}_{r})HH({\mathcal{X}})}\to\frac{HH(U_{n})}{(x^{i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,x^{i_{r}}_{r})HH(U_{n})}

between functors from
BS^{1}\times{\mathcal{C}}\times\Delta
to
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{H}}
where the
left-hand side factors through the projection

BS^{1}\times{\mathcal{C}}\times\Delta\to BS^{1}\times{\mathcal{C}}
.
In particular, for every
\alpha\in({i_{1}},\ldots,{i_{r}})\in{\mathcal{C}}
and

[n]\in\Delta
, this map is
S^{1}
-equivariant.

If we pass to the limit over
{\mathcal{C}}\times\Delta
, (
13.10
)
recovers the
S^{1}
-equivariant map

(13.11)

p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon HH({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to HH(U_{\bullet})

of Proposition
13.3
. On the other hand, passing to the limit over
BS^{1}
, it
produces

(13.12)

p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon\frac{HC^{-}({\mathcal{X}})}{(x^{i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,x^{i_{r}}_{r})HC^{-}({\mathcal{X}})}\to\frac{HC^{-}(U_{n})}{(x^{i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,x^{i_{r}}_{r})HC^{-}(U_{n})}.

Hence, the limit over
BS^{1}
of (
13.11
) is a weak equivalence if
and only if the limit over
{\mathcal{C}}\times\Delta
of (
13.12
)
is a weak equivalence (cf.
[
17
, § 31.5]
).
Using Proposition
13.3
, we therefore get a weak equivalence

p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon HC^{-}({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{\Delta}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}\frac{HC^{-}(U_{n})}{(x^{i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,x^{i_{r}}_{r})HC^{-}(U_{n})}.

Since each
HH(U_{n})
is
I_{G}
-complete as we showed above, it follows that

HC^{-}(U_{n})=\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{S^{1}}HH(U_{n})
is also
I_{G}
-complete
(cf. Proposition
5.3
(7)). In particular,
the right-hand side of the last equivalence coincides with

\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HC^{-}(U_{n})
. This finishes the proof.
∎

13.3.
Some results on cyclic homology

We end this section with the following results related to cyclic homology
which will be used in the next section.

Lemma 13.5
.

HH({\mathcal{X}})
and
HC({\mathcal{X}})
are homologically bounded below complexes in
{\mathcal{D}}_{k}
.
In particular,
\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HH(U_{n})
is homologically bounded below.

Proof.

Using (
3.12
), claiming that
HH({\mathcal{X}})
is homologically bounded below
is the same thing as claiming that
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}})
is homologically bounded
below. Since the latter is the derived push-forward of the structure sheaf of

{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}
under the structure map
p\colon{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}}\to{\rm pt}
,
it suffices to show more generally that
p
is of finite cohomological dimension
(cf.
[
40
, Lem. A.1.6]
). By
[
56
, Rem. 1.22]
,
this is equivalent to showing that
the structure map of
\pi_{0}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{X}})={\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}=[{I_{X}}/G]
is
of finite cohomological dimension. But this follows from
[
40
, Prop. A.1.8]

under our assumptions.

Since the cyclic homology complex is obtained by taking the sum totalization of
the cyclic bicomplex arising from the mixed complex structure on
HH({\mathcal{X}})
, it
follows that
HC({\mathcal{X}})
is also homologically bounded below.
The second part of the lemma now follows from Propositions
13.3
,

5.3
(1) and
2.3
.
∎

We now consider a general situation in which
H
is a
k
-group which acts on a

k
-scheme

Y
and let
{\mathcal{Y}}=[Y/H]
. Assume that
I_{H}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})\subset R_{k}(H)
and

I_{s}=(y^{s}_{1},\ldots,y^{s}_{r})
for
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We then have an
S^{1}
-equivariant
natural transformation of functors
HH({\mathcal{Y}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{H}}\to\{{HH({\mathcal{Y}})}/{I_{s}}\}_{s\in{\mathbb{N}}}
on
{\mathbb{N}}
, where we let
{HH({\mathcal{Y}})}/{I_{s}}:={HH({\mathcal{Y}})}/{I_{s}HH({\mathcal{Y}})}
. In particular, we get a
natural transformation of functors
({HH({\mathcal{Y}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{H}}})_{hS^{1}}\to{\{({HH({\mathcal{Y}})}/{I_{s}})_{hS^{1}}\}}_{s\in{\mathbb{N}}}\simeq{\{{HH({\mathcal{Y}})_{hS^{1}}}/{I_{s}}\}}_{s\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.
Passing to the homotopy limits, we get a natural map of
R_{k}(H)
-modules

(13.13)

\alpha_{\mathcal{X}}\colon(HH({\mathcal{Y}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{H}})_{hS^{1}}\to HC({\mathcal{Y}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{H}}.

Lemma 13.6
.

Assume that
H
is reductive and
Y
is a smooth

k
-scheme. Then the canonical map

\alpha_{{\mathcal{X}}}\colon(HH({\mathcal{Y}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{H}})_{hS^{1}}\to HC({\mathcal{Y}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{H}}

is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

Letting
M_{s}={HH({\mathcal{Y}})}/{I_{s}}
, it easily follows by using Lemma
13.5

and by iterating the long exact homotopy groups sequences associated to
fiber sequences
M\xrightarrow{x}M\to M/{xM}

(where
M
is any complex of
R_{k}(H)
-modules)
that the inverse system of complexes {
M_{s}\}_{s\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is uniformly homologically
bounded below. That is, for some
N\ll 0
, the canonical map

\tau_{\geq N}M_{s}\to M_{s}
is a weak equivalence for every
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
By replacing each
M_{s}
by
\tau_{\geq N}M_{s}
, we can therefore assume that
each
M_{s}
is bounded below by
N
.

We now look at the commutative diagram

(13.14)

\textstyle{(\varprojlim_{s}M_{s})_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\prod_{s}M_{s})_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{(\prod_{s}M_{s})_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\varprojlim_{s}(M_{s})_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{s}(M_{s})_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{\prod_{s}(M_{s})_{hS^{1}}}

in
{\mathcal{D}}_{k}
, where
u
is induced by the map on the products which is
given levelwise by
k_{n}\mapsto k_{n}-f_{n+1}(k_{n+1})
, where
f_{n}\colon M_{n}\to M_{n-1}
is the bonding map.
Since the transition maps of
\{M_{s}\}
are
\Lambda
-linear, it follows that

u
is
\Lambda
-linear. In particular, all maps in (
13.14
) are
defined and the rows are homotopy fiber sequences.
It suffices therefore to show that the canonical map

\alpha\colon(\prod_{s}M_{s})_{hS^{1}}\to\prod_{s}(M_{s})_{hS^{1}}
is a weak equivalence.

Now, we now recall from
[
18
, Def. 2.2.13]

that
((M_{s})_{hS^{1}})_{n}=\left((M_{s})_{n}\oplus(M_{s})_{n-2}\oplus\cdots\right)

for any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
In particular, we have

(13.15)

\left(\prod_{s}(M_{s})_{hS^{1}}\right)_{n}=\prod_{s}\left((M_{s})_{hS^{1}}\right)_{n}=\prod_{s}\left((M_{s})_{n}\oplus(M_{s})_{n-2}\oplus\cdots\right).

On the other hand, we have

(13.16)

\left(\left(\prod_{s}M_{s}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)=\left(\prod_{s}(M_{s})_{n}\oplus\prod_{s}(M_{s})_{n-2}\oplus\cdots\right).

Since each
M_{s}
is zero below
N
, one checks that
the right hand sides of (
13.15
) and (
13.16
)
coincide. This implies that
\alpha
is a weak equivalence and concludes the proof
of the lemma.
∎

Remark 13.7
.

The reader can check easily that our assumptions about the reductivity of

G
and
H
and the smoothness of
[X/G]
and
[Y/H]
are not used in the proofs of
Lemmas
13.5
and
13.6
even though these
suffice for us.

14.
Thomason’s problem for cyclic homology

In this section, we shall extend Proposition
13.3
to cyclic and periodic
cyclic homology and use these results to prove
Thomason’s completion theorem (cf. Corollary
12.7
) for all
homology theories.
We fix an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic zero.

14.1.
Cyclic homology of a C̆ech nerve

We let
G
be a reductive
k
-group and
X
a smooth

k
-scheme with
G
-action.
We let
{\mathcal{X}}=[X/G]
and let
p\colon U\to{\mathcal{X}}
be a smooth
surjective map with
U\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
. In this subsection, we shall prove the key
lemma which says that

HC(U_{\bullet})
is the homotopy orbit space for the
S^{1}
-action on
HH(U_{\bullet})
.
As the reader may note, this is a delicate statement because it involves
interchanging homotopy limit and colimit.

We let
p_{n}\colon U_{n}\to{\rm pt}
denote the structure map and let

{\mathbb{L}}_{n}={\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{\bullet}_{U_{n}}({\mathbb{L}}_{U_{n}}[1])\in D(U_{n})
so that

HH(U_{n})=(p_{n})_{*}({\mathbb{L}}_{n})
. We let
d_{n}=\text{\rm dim}(U_{n})
.
Since
U_{n}\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
, there is a natural weak equivalence

{\mathbb{L}}_{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{\bullet}(\Omega^{1}_{{U_{n}}/k}[1])
. Each

{\mathbb{L}}_{n}
comes equipped with
a Connes operator
B
which is identified with the de Rham differential

d\colon\Omega^{i}_{{U_{n}}/k}\to\Omega^{i+1}_{{U_{n}}/k}
.
It follows that
{\mathbb{L}}_{n}
is endowed with the canonical
HKR filtration
{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}{\mathbb{L}}_{n}=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{n}}}{{\underset{i=m}{\bigoplus}}}\Omega^{i}_{{U_{n}}/k}[i]
. Moreover,

\{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}{\mathbb{L}}_{n}\}_{m\geq 0}
is an

S^{1}
-equivariant
filtration in which the
S^{1}
-action is given by the Connes operator
B
.
Letting
{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})=(p_{n})_{*}({\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}{\mathbb{L}}_{n})\simeq

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{n}}}{{\underset{i=m}{\bigoplus}}}(p_{n})_{*}(\Omega^{i}_{{U_{n}}/k})[i]
and using
that the derived push-forward commutes with homotopy limits, we get
the following.

Lemma 14.1
.

There is an
S^{1}
-equivariant descending filtration

\{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})\}_{m\geq 0}
of
HH(U_{n})

whose graded quotients are given by

{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})=(p_{n})_{*}(\Omega^{m}_{{U_{n}}/k})[m]
. Furthermore, the
canonical map

q_{n}\colon HH(U_{n})\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}

is a weak equivalence.

Lemma 14.2
.

For every
m\geq 0
, the canonical map

\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\left(\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)

is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

By induction on
m
and the commutative diagram of the homotopy fiber
sequences

(14.1)

\textstyle{\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m+1}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\left(\left({\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\left(\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m+1}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\left(\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right),}

it suffices to show that the canonical map

(14.2)

\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})\right)_{hS^{1}}\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\left(\left({\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)

is a weak equivalence for every
m\geq 0
.

We now note that each
{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})
has trivial
S^{1}
-action
since
{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}{\mathbb{L}_{n}}
is a single term complex
(cf. proof of part (1) of
[
9
, Cor. 3.4]
). Since

\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})
is the product totalization of

{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})
over
n
, it follows that the former also has trivial

S^{1}
-action. Equivalently, it is a mixed complex with trivial Connes operator.
This implies that there is a canonical weak equivalence
(cf.
[
67
, § 5.1.7]
)

(14.3)

\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})\right)_{hS^{1}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\underset{i\geq 0}{\bigoplus}}\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})\right)[2i].

On the other hand, the commutativity of homotopy limits with
finite direct sums implies using the definition of the HKR-filtration
that we have a direct sum decomposition

(14.4)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HH(U_{n})&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\rm fil}^{m+1}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})\right)\bigoplus\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)\\
&&\bigoplus\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})\right).\end{array}

In conjunction with Lemma
13.5
, this implies that

\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})
is homologically bounded below.

We now let
M_{\bullet}=\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})
and
consider the commutative diagram of complexes

(14.5)

\textstyle{\tau_{\geq t}M_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\underset{i\geq 0}{\bigoplus}}(\tau_{\geq t}M_{\bullet})[2i]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\underset{i\geq 0}{\prod}}(\tau_{\geq t}M_{\bullet})[2i]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\underset{i\geq 0}{\bigoplus}}M_{\bullet}[2i]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\underset{i\geq 0}{\prod}}M_{\bullet}[2i],}

where the vertical arrows are the canonical inclusions and the left horizontal
arrows are the obvious inclusions.

We just showed above that the left vertical arrow in (
14.5
) is
a weak equivalence for some
t\ll 0
. In particular, the other two vertical
arrows
are also weak equivalences (as one easily checks) for
t\ll 0
.
On the other hand, the map

{\underset{i\geq 0}{\bigoplus}}(\tau_{\geq t}M_{\bullet})[2i]\to{\underset{i\geq 0}{\prod}}(\tau_{\geq t}M_{\bullet})[2i]
is a weak equivalence since

\tau_{\geq t}M_{\bullet}
is strictly bounded below.
We conclude from this and (
14.3
) that
there is a canonical weak equivalence

(14.6)

\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})\right)_{hS^{1}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\underset{i\geq 0}{\prod}}\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})\right)[2i].

Composing this with the natural weak equivalences

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\underset{i\geq 0}{\prod}}\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})\right)[2i]&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\left({\underset{i\geq 0}{\prod}}{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})[2i]\right)\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\left({\underset{i\geq 0}{\bigoplus}}{\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})[2i]\right)\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\left(\left({\rm gr}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})\right)_{hS^{1}}\right),\\
\end{array}

we conclude that (
14.2
) is a weak equivalence. This
finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 14.3
.

For every
n\geq 0
, the canonical map

(14.7)

\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\left(\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)

is a weak equivalence. The same is also true for the canonical map

(14.8)

\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\left(\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right).

Proof.

If we let
F_{m}=\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}
, then
Lemma
13.5
and (
14.4
) imply that

\{F_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
is a uniformly (in
m
) homologically bounded below sequential
diagram of mixed complexes in
{\mathcal{D}}_{k}
. We showed in the proof of
Lemma
13.6
that in this case, (
14.8
) is a weak equivalence.
The same argument also applies to (
14.7
).
∎

Lemma 14.4
.

The natural map
\alpha_{U_{\bullet}}\colon{HH(U_{\bullet})}_{hS^{1}}\to HC(U_{\bullet})

is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

We let
q=\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}q_{n}
(cf. Lemma
14.1
) and look at the diagram

(14.9)

\textstyle{HH(U_{\bullet})_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HH(U_{n})\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{U_{\bullet}}}
\textstyle{\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HH(U_{n})_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\left(\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\left(\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\left(\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\left(\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right).}

In this diagram, the arrow
q^{\prime}
is induced from
q
by applying the functor

(-)_{hS^{1}}
and
q^{\prime\prime}
is induced by first applying the functor
(-)_{hS^{1}}
to each

q_{n}
and then taking limit over
n
. The arrows
\alpha
and
\delta
are the
canonical maps which commute the two homotopy limits,
\beta
is the canonical map
to the homotopy limit over
m
,
\tau
is the canonical map and

\gamma
is obtained by first taking the canonical
map to the homotopy limit over
n
(for each
m
) and then
passing to the limit over
m
.

To see that (
14.9
) is commutative, we let

{\rm pr}_{n}\colon\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HH(U_{n})\right)_{hS^{1}}\to HH(U_{n})_{hS^{1}}

and
{\rm pr}^{\prime}_{n}\colon\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\left(\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\left(\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)

denote the projection maps for
n\geq 0
. We let
\vartheta=\delta\circ\gamma\circ\beta\circ\alpha\circ q^{\prime}
and
\vartheta^{\prime}=\tau\circ q^{\prime\prime}\circ\alpha_{U_{\bullet}}
. It suffices then to show that
{\rm pr}^{\prime}_{n}\circ\vartheta={\rm pr}^{\prime}_{n}\circ\vartheta^{\prime}
up to homotopy for each
n\geq 0
. To that end, one first
easily checks that

{\rm pr}^{\prime}_{n}\circ\vartheta^{\prime}={\rm pr}^{\prime}_{n}\circ\tau\circ q^{\prime\prime}\circ\alpha_{U_{\bullet}}=\tau_{n}\circ q^{\prime}_{n}\circ{\rm pr}_{n}
, where

q^{\prime}_{n}
is obtained from
q_{n}
by applying the functor
(-)_{hS^{1}}
and

\tau_{n}\colon\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\to\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}
is the canonical
map to the homotopy limit. It remains therefore to show that

{\rm pr}^{\prime}_{n}\circ\vartheta=\tau_{n}\circ q^{\prime}_{n}\circ{\rm pr}_{n}
for every
n\geq 0
.

To show the last claim, we look at the diagram

(14.10)

\textstyle{\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HH(U_{n})\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}_{n}}
\textstyle{HH(U_{n})_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}_{n}}
\textstyle{\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{n}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\left(\left(\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\left(\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\left(\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}^{\prime}_{n}}
\textstyle{\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{m}\left(\frac{HH(U_{n})}{{\rm fil}^{m}_{{\rm HKR}}HH(U_{n})}\right)_{hS^{1}},}

where all horizontal arrows in the middle are the canonical projections and
the composition of all vertical arrows on the left is
\vartheta
.
It is clear that all squares in this diagram commute and
\gamma_{n}=\tau_{n}
. This proves the claim, and hence the commutativity of
(
14.9
).

We now note that the arrows
q^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime\prime}
in (
14.9
) are weak
equivalences. The arrows
\alpha
and
\delta
are weak equivalences by the
commutativity property of two homotopy limits. The arrows
\tau
and

\beta
are weak equivalences by Lemma
14.3
. Finally,
\gamma
is a
weak equivalence by Lemma
14.2
. It follows that
\alpha_{U_{\bullet}}
is a
weak equivalence. This concludes the proof.
∎

14.2.
I_{G}
-completion of cyclic homology

We now let
G
be a reductive
k
-group and
{\mathcal{X}}=[X/G]
, where

X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}^{G}_{k}
. We do not assume that
X
is quasi-projective. Let

p\colon U\to{\mathcal{X}}
be a smooth
surjective map with
U\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
. We shall now prove the analogue of
Proposition
13.3
for cyclic and periodic cyclic homologies.
We begin with the following.

Lemma 14.5
.

For each
n\geq 0
, the spectrum
HC(U_{n})
is
I_{G}
-complete.

Proof.

Using Proposition
2.3
, we can write

I_{G}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\subset R_{k}(G)
.
For
s\geq 1
, we let
I_{s}=(x^{s}_{1},\ldots,x^{s}_{r})
.
We now note that
HH(U_{n})
is homologically
bounded since
U\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
. We showed in the proof
of Lemma
13.5
that this implies that the sequential diagram

\{{HH(U_{n})}/{I_{s}HH(U_{n})}\}_{s\geq 1}
is uniformly homologically bounded in
s
.
It follows therefore from the proof of Lemma
13.6
that the canonical map

(14.11)

\left(HH(U_{n})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\right)_{hS^{1}}\to HC(U_{n})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}

is a weak equivalence. The lemma now follows because we showed earlier that

HH(U_{n})
is
I_{G}
-complete (cf. (
13.6
)).
∎

By Lemma
14.5
, we get a diagram

(14.12)

\textstyle{HC({\mathcal{X}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{HC(U_{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{HC({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{n}}
\textstyle{HC(U_{n})}

which is compatible with the face and degeneracy maps of

\{HC(U_{n})\}_{[n]\in\Delta}
and whose outer square is commutative.
Passing to the homotopy limit of
p^{*}_{n}
, we get a unique map

p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon HC({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to HC(U_{\bullet})
such that the lower triangle
also commutes for each
n
. But this forces the upper triangle to commute too.

We can now prove our main results about the
I_{G}
-completions of cyclic and
periodic cyclic homologies.

Theorem 14.6
.

The map

p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon HC({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to HC(U_{\bullet})

is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

We look at the diagram

(14.13)

\textstyle{{(HH({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}})}_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{\mathcal{X}}}
\textstyle{{HH(U_{\bullet})}_{hS^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{U_{\bullet}}}
\textstyle{HC({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{HC(U_{\bullet}),}

which is homotopy commutative by the naturality of the vertical arrows.
The left vertical arrow is a weak equivalence by Lemma
13.6
and the
top horizontal arrow is a weak equivalence by Proposition
13.3
. It suffices
therefore to show that the right vertical arrow is a weak equivalence. But this
follows from Lemma
14.4
.
∎

Corollary 14.7
.

The pull-back map

HP({\mathcal{X}})\to HP(U_{\bullet})
factors through a weak equivalence

p^{*}_{\bullet}\colon HP({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to HP(U_{\bullet}).

Proof.

Using the commutative diagram of the homotopy fiber sequences
(cf. Theorem
3.3
)

(14.14)

\textstyle{HC(U_{n})[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{HC^{-}(U_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{HP(U_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{HC(U_{n})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{HC^{-}(U_{n})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{HP(U_{n})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}}

and the
I_{G}
-completeness of
HC(U_{n})
and
HC^{-}(U_{n})
, we get that
each
HP(U_{n})
is

I_{G}
-complete. By passing to the limit and repeating the previous argument for the
cyclic homology, we get the factorization of the pull-back

HP({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{p^{*}_{\bullet}}HP(U_{\bullet})
through the
I_{G}
-completion.

We now look at the commutative diagram of homotopy fiber sequences

(14.15)

\textstyle{HC({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{HC^{-}({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{HP({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{HC(U_{\bullet})[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{HC^{-}(U_{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{HP(U_{\bullet}).}

The left and the middle vertical arrows are weak equivalences by
Corollary
13.4
and Theorem
14.6
. It follows that the
right vertical arrow is also a weak equivalence.
∎

The following result extends Corollary
12.7
to equivariant homology
theories.

Theorem 14.8
.

Let
G
be a reductive
k
-group acting on a smooth
k
-scheme
X
and let
{\mathcal{X}}=[X/G]
. Let
F
belong to the set
\{HH,HC^{-},HC,HP\}
of functors
on the category of stacks over
k
. Let
g\in G_{s}(k)
and let

\Psi\subset G(k)
be the conjugacy class of
g
.
Then for any smooth surjective map
p\colon U\to[{X^{g}}/{Z_{g}}]
with

U\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
, one has a natural weak equivalence of spectra

(14.16)

\Theta^{g}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\colon F({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}F(U_{n}).

Proof.

We consider the maps

(14.17)

F({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\xrightarrow{\iota^{*}_{g}}F([{X^{g}}/{Z_{g}}])^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{g}}\xrightarrow{t_{g^{-1}}}F([{X^{g}}/{Z_{g}}])^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{Z_{g}}}\xrightarrow{p^{*}_{\bullet}}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}F(U_{n}),

where
\iota^{*}_{g}
is the pull-back map induced by the closed immersion of
smooth schemes
\iota_{g}\colon X^{g}\hookrightarrow X
.
Since the group of connected components of
Z_{g}
is reductive as
{\rm char}(k)=0
,
it follows from
[
12
, Thm. 13.19]
that
Z_{g}
is a reductive
k
-group.
We deduce by combining
[
18
, Thm. 3.2.3, 3.2.10]
with Proposition
13.3
,
Corollary
13.4
, Theorem
14.6
, Corollary
14.7
and
Proposition
5.7
that all arrows in (
14.17
)
are weak equivalences. This proves the theorem.
∎

15.
Results for Deligne-Mumford stacks

We let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero and let

G
be a
k
-group acting on a
k
-scheme
X
with finite stabilizers
(cf. §
5.3
). Equivalently,
[X/G]
is a Deligne-Mumford
stack. In this section,
we shall show that the equivariant
KH
-theory (without passing to any completion)
of
X
coincides with the Borel equivariant
KH
-theory of the inertia scheme
I_{X}
.
We shall prove the same statement for the homology theories if
X
is smooth.
The corresponding result for
K^{\prime}
-theory was proven in
[
64
]

when
k={\mathbb{C}}
.
These results provide simple descriptions of the equivariant
KH
-theory and
homology theories of quotient Deligne-Mumford stacks.

15.1.
KH
-theory of DM stacks

We recall for the reader that
KH((I_{X})_{G},k)
is the Borel equivariant
homotopy
K
-theory of
I_{X}
while

KH((I_{X})^{\bullet}_{G},k)=\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}KH(G^{n}\times I_{X})

is the homotopy
K
-theory of the bar construction for
G
-action on
I_{X}
.
Our main results for
KH
-theory are the following.

Theorem 15.1
.

There is a weak equivalence of spectra

(15.1)

\Upsilon^{G}_{X}\colon KH_{G}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\simeq}KH((I_{X})_{G},k)

which is natural in
X
.

Proof.

We shall write
KH_{G}(X,k)
as
KH([X/G],k)
throughout the proof.
By Proposition
5.8
, there exists an ideal
J\subset R_{k}(G)
with
finite support
\{{\mathfrak{m}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{m}}_{r}\}
such that

J(KH_{i}(\operatorname{[X/G]},k))=0
for all
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. If let
\Sigma^{G}_{X}=\{\Psi^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\Psi^{\prime}_{n}\}
be as in Proposition
5.8
(1), then
we can further assume that
\{{\mathfrak{m}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{m}}_{r}\}
contains the maximal ideals
corresponding to
\Sigma^{G}_{X}
.
In particular,
KH_{i}(\operatorname{[X/G]},k)
is an
{R_{k}(G)}/J
-module.
We thus get

(15.2)

\begin{array}[]{lll}KH_{i}(\operatorname{[X/G]},k)&\xrightarrow{\cong}&KH_{i}(\operatorname{[X/G]},k)\otimes_{R_{k}(G)}{R_{k}(G)}/J\\
&\xrightarrow{\cong}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigoplus}}}KH_{i}(\operatorname{[X/G]},k)\otimes_{R_{k}(G)}({R_{k}(G)}/J)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}}\\
&\xrightarrow{\cong}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigoplus}}}KH_{i}(\operatorname{[X/G]},k)\otimes_{R_{k}(G)}{R_{k}(G)}/J\otimes_{R_{k}(G)}{R_{k}(G)}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}}\\
&\xrightarrow{\cong}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigoplus}}}KH_{i}(\operatorname{[X/G]},k)\otimes_{R_{k}(G)}{R_{k}(G)}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}}\\
&\xrightarrow{\cong}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigoplus}}}KH_{i}(\operatorname{[X/G]},k)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}}\\
\end{array}

Since the homotopy groups of derived localizations are the usual localizations
of homotopy groups, we get that the canonical map
of spectra

(15.3)

KH_{G}(X,k)\to\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\coprod}}}KH_{G}(X,k)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}}

is a weak equivalence, where the terms on the right hand side are
derived localizations (cf. §
5.1
).
Combining this with Corollary
5.9
, we get that
the canonical map

(15.4)

KH_{G}(X,k)\to\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\coprod}}}KH_{G}(X,k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}}

is a weak equivalence.

By Corollary
2.11
, every
{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}
is of the form
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi_{i}}
for a
unique semisimple conjugacy class
\Psi_{i}\subset G_{s}(k)
. If we let

g_{i}\in G_{s}(k)
be a representative of
\Psi_{i}
, then (
15.4
) and
Theorem
8.12
together imply that there is a canonical weak equivalence

(15.5)

KH_{G}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\coprod}}}KH_{Z_{g_{i}}}(X^{g_{i}},k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{Z_{g_{i}}}}

The canonical map
KH_{Z_{g_{i}}}(X^{g_{i}},k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\to KH_{Z_{g_{i}}}(X^{g_{i}},k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{Z_{g_{i}}}}

is a weak equivalence
by Corollary
2.12
, Proposition
2.3
and
Proposition
5.3
(5).
If
g\in G_{s}(k)\setminus(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\cup}}}\Psi_{i})
,
then
X^{g}=\emptyset
by
Proposition
5.8
since
\Sigma^{G}_{X}\subset\{{\mathfrak{m}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{m}}_{r}\}
.
We can thus write (
15.5
) as

(15.6)

KH_{G}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\underset{g\in G_{s}(k)}{\coprod}}KH_{Z_{g}}(X^{g},k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}},

where the right hand side is a finite coproduct.

Using the decomposition

[{I_{X}}/G]={\underset{g\in G_{s}(k)}{\coprod}}[{X^{g}}/{Z_{g}}]
of the inertia stack
(cf.
[
64
, Lem. 2.6]
), we get

(15.7)

\begin{array}[]{lll}KH_{G}(X,k)&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\underset{g\in G_{s}(k)}{\coprod}}KH_{Z_{g}}(X^{g},k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&\left[{\underset{g\in G_{s}(k)}{\coprod}}KH_{Z_{g}}(X^{g},k)\right]^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&\left[{\underset{g\in G_{s}(k)}{\coprod}}KH([{X^{g}}/{Z_{g}}],k)\right]^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&KH([{I_{X}}/G],k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&KH_{G}(I_{X},k)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}.\end{array}

Letting
\Upsilon^{G}_{X}
be the composition of this weak equivalence with the map

\vartheta_{{I_{X}}/G}
of (
7.1
) and applying
Theorem
7.6
,
we conclude that
\Upsilon^{G}_{X}
is a weak equivalence. Furthermore,
this is natural in

X
since all maps which are used in its definition have been shown
previously to be natural in
X
.
∎

Theorem 15.2
.

If
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{G}_{k}
and
the hypotheses of Theorem
7.6
are satisfied, then
we have a weak equivalence of spectra

(15.8)

\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{G}_{X}\colon KH_{G}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\simeq}KH((I_{X})^{\bullet}_{G},k)

which is natural in
X
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to that of Theorem
15.1
except that we take

\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{G}_{X}
to be the composition of the weak equivalence in
(
15.7
) with the map
\widetilde{\pi}^{*}
of Corollary
12.6

instead of the map
\vartheta_{[{I_{X}}/G]}
of (
7.1
).
∎

15.2.
Homology theories of DM stacks

We assume now that
G
is reductive and
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}^{G}_{k}
.
We let
{\mathcal{X}}=[X/G]
,
I_{{\mathcal{X}}}=[{I_{X}}/G]
and let
\tau\colon I_{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
be the
projection. Let
\Sigma^{s}_{G}
be the set of all semisimple conjugacy classes in
G(k)

and let
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}\subset R_{k}(G)
denote the maximal ideal corresponding to

\Psi\in\Sigma^{s}_{G}
. In this subsection, we shall write
K({\mathcal{X}},k)
simply as

K({\mathcal{X}})
. The key to the main results of this subsection is the
following.

Proposition 15.3
.

For
F\in\{HH,HC^{-},HC,HP\}
, the canonical map

(15.9)

F({\mathcal{X}})\to{\underset{\Psi\in\Sigma^{s}_{G}}{\coprod}}F({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}

is a weak equivalence. Moreover, the right hand side is a finite coproduct.

Proof.

We have shown in (
15.3
) that the localization map

(15.10)

K({\mathcal{X}})\to{\underset{\Psi\in\Sigma^{s}_{G}}{\coprod}}K({\mathcal{X}})_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}

is a weak equivalence. Moreover, the right hand side is a finite coproduct of ring
spectra. Since the trace maps

(15.11)

K({\mathcal{X}})\to HC^{-}({\mathcal{X}})\to HH({\mathcal{X}})

as well as the maps
T\colon HC^{-}({\mathcal{X}})\to HP({\mathcal{X}})
and
HH({\mathcal{X}})\to HC({\mathcal{X}})

are maps of ring spectra (cf. Theorem
3.3
),
it follows that
F({\mathcal{X}})
is a
K({\mathcal{X}})
-algebra for

F\in\{HH,HC^{-},HC,HP\}
.
By taking the smash product of (
15.10
) with
F({\mathcal{X}})
over

K({\mathcal{X}})
, we get a weak equivalence

F({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\underset{\psi\in\Sigma^{s}_{G}}{\coprod}}F({\mathcal{X}})\wedge_{K({\mathcal{X}})}K({\mathcal{X}})_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}.

Since
K({\mathcal{X}})_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}
is the homotopy colimit of sequences

(K({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{x}K({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{x}\cdots)
with

x\in R_{k}(G)\setminus{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}

and the smash product commutes with homotopy colimits
(cf.
[
68
, § 2.4, p. 38]
), the canonical map

F({\mathcal{X}})\wedge_{K({\mathcal{X}})}K({\mathcal{X}})_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\to F({\mathcal{X}})_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}

is a weak equivalence. On the other hand,
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi})^{n}K_{0}({\mathcal{X}})_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}=0

for some
n\gg 0
, as shown in the proof of Corollary
5.9
.
As
F({\mathcal{X}})
is a
K({\mathcal{X}})
-module, it follows that
every element of
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}
acts nilpotently on
each
\pi_{i}\left(F({\mathcal{X}})_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\right)
.
But this implies that the completion map

(15.12)

F({\mathcal{X}})_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\to F({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}

is a weak equivalence (cf.
[
70
, Cor. 7.3.2.2]
).
This finishes the proof.
∎

For every
\Psi\in\Sigma^{s}_{G}
, we pick an element
g_{\Psi}\in\Psi
and
write
X_{\Psi}=X^{g_{\Psi}}
and
Z_{\Psi}=Z_{g_{\Psi}}
. We let
{\mathcal{X}}_{\Psi}=[{X_{\Psi}}/{Z_{\Psi}}]
.
Let
p\colon U\to I_{\mathcal{X}}
be a smooth surjective map with
U\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
.
We now look at the sequence of maps

(15.13)

\begin{array}[]{lll}F({\mathcal{X}})&\to&{\underset{\Psi\in\Sigma^{s}_{G}}{\coprod}}F({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\\
&\xrightarrow{\amalg\iota_{\Psi}^{*}}&{\underset{\Psi\in\Sigma^{s}_{G}}{\coprod}}F({\mathcal{X}}_{\Psi})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{g_{\Psi}}}\\
&\xrightarrow{\amalg t_{g^{-1}_{\Psi}}}&{\underset{\Psi\in\Sigma^{s}_{G}}{\coprod}}F({\mathcal{X}}_{\Psi})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{Z_{\Psi}}}\\
&{\simeq}&{\underset{\Psi\in\Sigma^{s}_{G}}{\coprod}}F({\mathcal{X}}_{\Psi})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&\left({\underset{\Psi\in\Sigma^{s}_{G}}{\coprod}}F({\mathcal{X}}_{\Psi})\right)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&F(I_{\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\\
&\xrightarrow{p^{*}_{\bullet}}&\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}F(U_{n}).\\
\end{array}

In the above sequence of arrows, the first arrow is the completion map of
Proposition
15.3
, the second arrow is the sum of pull-back maps
via the inclusions
X_{\Psi}\hookrightarrow X
, the third arrow is the twisting map of
Proposition
5.7
, the fourth weak equivalence follows from
Corollary
2.12
, Proposition
2.3
and Proposition
5.3
(5),
the fifth weak equivalence is clear, the sixth weak equivalence follows
from the decomposition
I_{\mathcal{X}}={\underset{\Psi\in\Sigma^{s}_{G}}{\coprod}}{\mathcal{X}}_{\Psi}
, and the last weak equivalence is from Proposition
13.3

and its analogue for other homology theories.
Combining these weak equivalences, we get the following analogue of
Theorem
15.2
for homology theories.

Theorem 15.4
.

Let
G
be a reductive
k
-group acting on a smooth
k
-scheme
X
with finite
stabilizers and let
{\mathcal{X}}=[X/G]
.
Given any smooth surjective map
p\colon U\to I_{\mathcal{X}}
with
U\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
and

F\in\{HH,HC^{-},HC,HP\}
, there is a weak equivalence of spectra

(15.14)

\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mathcal{X}}\colon F({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}F(U_{n})

which is natural in
{\mathcal{X}}
.

Proof.

The weak equivalence is shown above and the proof of
its naturality is the same as that
for
K
-theory in Theorem
15.2
.
∎

15.3.
Individual homology groups of DM stacks

We shall now give a complete description of all homology groups of the quotient stack

{\mathcal{X}}
of Theorem
15.4
.
We begin by recalling some facts about the cotangent complex of
smooth Deligne-Mumford stacks.

Let
{\mathcal{Y}}
be a Deligne-Mumford stack over
k
. Recall that there is a natural
augmentation map
{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{Y}}\to\Omega^{1}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/k}[0]
. If

{\mathcal{Y}}
is smooth over
k
(which will be the case below), then this map is a
weak equivalence (cf.
[
76
, § 3]
).
In particular,
{\mathbb{T}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}[-1]\simeq{\rm Spec\,}({\rm Sym}^{\bullet}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(\Omega^{1}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/k}[1]))
.
Since each
\Omega^{i}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/k}
is locally free which is zero for
i>\text{\rm dim}({\mathcal{Y}})
,
it follows that
{\rm Sym}^{\bullet}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(\Omega^{1}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/k}[1])
is a finite complex.
In particular, its augmentation ideal is nilpotent which implies that the
formal completion
\widehat{{\mathbb{T}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}[-1]\to{\mathbb{T}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}[-1]
along the zero section is
an equivalence. We let
{{\rm{\bf{Et}}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}
denote the étale site of
{\mathcal{Y}}
whose
objects are étale maps
f\colon U\to{\mathcal{Y}}
with
U\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
and morphisms are
étale maps between schemes.

We write

(15.15)

{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}={\rm Sym}^{\bullet}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(\Omega^{1}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/k}[1])=(\Omega^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/k}\xrightarrow{0}\cdots\xrightarrow{0}\Omega^{1}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/k}\xrightarrow{0}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}),

where we write
d_{{\mathcal{Y}}}=\text{\rm dim}({\mathcal{Y}})
and the complex is written homologically
with
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}
placed at degree zero.
We let
\Omega^{\bullet}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}
be the (cohomological) de Rham complex of
{\mathcal{Y}}
over
k

(with de Rham differentials) and let

{}_{s_{\geq m}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}
and
{}_{s_{\leq m}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}
denote its
stupid truncations. We let
H^{n}_{dR}({\mathcal{Y}})={\mathbb{H}}^{n}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathcal{Y}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{{\mathcal{Y}}})
.

We let

(15.16)

{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{C}}^{-}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}=({\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}})^{hS^{1}}={\underset{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\bigoplus}}\ \Omega^{\geq i}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}[2i];\ \ {\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}=({\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}})_{hS^{1}}={\underset{i\geq 0}{\bigoplus}}\ \Omega^{\leq i}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}[2i];

\ \ {\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}=({\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}})^{tS^{1}}={\underset{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\bigoplus}}\ \Omega^{\bullet}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}[2i].

For
F\in\{HH,HC^{-},HC,HP\}
, we let
\widetilde{F}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}
be the fibrant replacement
of
F
on the category of presheaves of complexes of
k
-vector spaces on
{{\rm{\bf{Et}}}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Then the canonical map
{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(U)\to\widetilde{HH}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(U)=HH(U)

is a weak equivalence if

U\in{{\rm{\bf{Et}}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}
is affine as one checks using the hypercohomology spectral
sequence. It follows that the canonical map

{\mathbb{H}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathcal{Y}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}})\to{\mathbb{H}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathcal{Y}},\widetilde{HH}_{{\mathcal{Y}}})
is a weak equivalence of
hypercohomology spectra (cf.
[
84
]
).
The same also holds for other homology functors.
In the following,
\Gamma(-,-)
is the global section functor on the category of
chain complexes of étale sheaves.

We now prove the following.

Theorem 15.5
.

Let
G
be a reductive
k
-group acting on a smooth
k
-scheme
X
with finite
stabilizers and let
{\mathcal{X}}=[X/G]
. Then there are canonical weak
equivalences

HH({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}});\ \ HC^{-}({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{C}}^{-}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}).

If
{\mathcal{X}}
is separated, then there are canonical weak
equivalences

HC({\mathcal{Y}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{C}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}});\ \ HP({\mathcal{Y}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}).

Proof.

We have shown in the proofs of Proposition
15.3
and
Theorem
15.4
that for

F\in\{HH,HC^{-},HC,HP\}
, there are canonical weak equivalences

(15.17)

\theta_{{\mathcal{X}}}\colon F({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}F(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}F(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}.

On the other hand, it follows from Lemma
13.1
and formal HKR
theorem (see also (
3.12
)) that

HH(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{T}}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}[-1])
. Since

\widehat{{\mathbb{T}}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}[-1]\to{\mathbb{T}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}[-1]
is a weak equivalence, we get that there is a
weak equivalence

(15.18)

\theta^{\prime}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}\colon HH(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{T}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}[-1])={\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}).

Letting
\Psi_{{\mathcal{X}}}=\theta^{\prime}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}\circ\theta_{{\mathcal{X}}}
, we get a natural weak
equivalence

(15.19)

\Psi_{{\mathcal{X}}}\colon HH({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}).

Since the functor
(-)^{hS^{1}}
commutes with
{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},(-))
,
we get

(15.20)

\begin{array}[]{lllll}HC^{-}({\mathcal{X}})&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&HC^{-}(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}&\simeq&\left(HH(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{hS^{1}}\right)^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\\
&\simeq&\left(HH(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{I_{G}}\right)^{hS^{1}}&\simeq&({\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}))^{hS^{1}}\\
&\simeq&{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},({\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}})^{hS^{1}})&\simeq&{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{C}}^{-}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}),\\
\end{array}

where the first weak equivalence is from (
15.13
),
the third weak equivalence was shown in the proof of
Corollary
13.4
and the fourth weak equivalence is from
(
15.18
).

To prove the result for
HP
when
{\mathcal{X}}
is separated, we note that

(15.21)

HP({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}HP(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})_{I_{G}}\simeq\left(HH(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{tS^{1}}\right)_{I_{G}},

where the first weak equivalence is from (
15.12
) and
(
15.13
).
On the other hand, we can write
HH(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{tS^{1}}
as the filtered homotopy colimit

HH(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{tS^{1}}\simeq\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{i}\left(HH(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{hS^{1}}\to HH(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{hS^{1}}[2]\to\cdots\to HH(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{hS^{1}}[2i]\to\cdots\right).

\hskip 56.9055pt\simeq\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{i}\left(HC^{-}(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})\to HC^{-}(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})[2]\to\cdots\to HC^{-}(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})[2i]\to\cdots\right).

Since localization at the maximal ideal
I_{G}
is also a homotopy colimit, we
get

\left(HH(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{tS^{1}}\right)_{I_{G}}\simeq\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{i}\left(HC^{-}(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})_{I_{G}}\to HC^{-}(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})_{I_{G}}[2]\to\cdots\to HC^{-}(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})_{I_{G}}[2i]\to\cdots\right)

\hskip 56.9055pt\simeq\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{i}\left(E_{1}\to E_{2}\to\cdots\to E_{2i}\to\cdots\right),

where the second weak equivalence follows from (
15.20
)
if we let
E_{i}={\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{C}}^{-}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}})[2i]
.
As
{\mathcal{X}}
is separated, the projection map
{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is
finite. In particular,
{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
is separated and it follows from

[
39
, Lem. 2.15]
that the functor
{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},(-))

commutes with filtered homotopy colimits. We thus get

\begin{array}[]{lllll}HP({\mathcal{X}})&\simeq&{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},\mathop{{\rm hocolim}}_{i}({\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{C}}^{-}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}})[2i])&\simeq&{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},({\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}})^{tS^{1}})\\
&\simeq&{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}).\end{array}

Finally, we look at the diagram of
H_{k}
-module spectra

(15.22)

\textstyle{HC(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})_{I_{G}}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{HC^{-}(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})_{I_{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{HP(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})_{I_{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{C}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}})[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{C}}^{-}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}}).}

The two rows are homotopy fiber sequences and the right square is
commutative in which the vertical arrows are weak equivalences. It follows that
there is a canonical weak equivalence

HC(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{C}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}})
.
Combining this with (
15.17
), we get our claim for
HC
, and
this concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Remark 15.6
.

When
G
is finite, Theorem
15.5
for Hochschild homology was
shown in
[
4
, Prop. 3.1]
by a different method, and can also be
derived from
[
1
, Thm. 4.9]
.
For smooth and separated Deligne-Mumford stacks, Theorem
15.5

for Hochschild homology was shown in
[
39
, Prop. 2.13]

(and used at several places in the same paper) by a completely
different method. However, the proof therein appears to have a gap in that
the authors claim that the composition of the canonical pull-back maps

HH({\mathcal{X}})\to HH(I_{{\mathcal{X}}})\to{\bf{R}}\Gamma(I_{{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}_{I_{{\mathcal{X}}}})
is a weak
equivalence, which is not the case. The reader can see using
(
15.13
) that the map
\Psi_{{\mathcal{X}}}
in
(
15.19
) is not the usual pull-back map.

The following is a direct consequence of Theorem
15.5
. It provides
a complete description of all homology theories of the quotient stack
{\mathcal{X}}
.
We let
d=\text{\rm dim}({\mathcal{X}})
.

Corollary 15.7
.

For every
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, there are canonical isomorphisms of
k
-vector spaces

HH_{n}({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{0\leq i\leq d}{\bigoplus}}H^{i-n}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(I_{\mathcal{X}},\Omega^{i}_{{I_{\mathcal{X}}}/k});

HC^{-}_{n}({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{0\leq i\leq d-n}{\bigoplus}}H^{n+2i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(I_{\mathcal{X}},~_{s_{\geq i+n}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{I_{\mathcal{X}}}).

If
{\mathcal{X}}
is separated, there are canonical isomorphisms of
k
-vector spaces

HC_{n}({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{-d\leq i\leq n}{\bigoplus}}H^{n-2i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(I_{\mathcal{X}},~_{s_{\leq n-i}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{I_{\mathcal{X}}});

HP_{n}({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\prod}}H^{n+2i}_{dR}(I_{{\mathcal{X}}}).

We end this section with generalizations of Theorems
14.8
and

15.4
for the equivariant periodic cyclic homology for
certain group actions on singular
k
-schemes. The result is the following.

Theorem 15.8
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. Let

G
be a reductive
k
-group acting on a
k
-scheme
X
and let
{\mathcal{X}}=[X/G]
.
Let
g\in G_{s}(k)
and let
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}\subset R_{k}(G)
be the maximal ideal
corresponding to the conjugacy class
\Psi
of
g
. We then have the following.

(1)

If
G
acts on
X
with nice stabilizers, then one has a weak equivalence of spectra

\vartheta^{g}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\colon HP({\mathcal{X}})^{\,\,{\widehat{}}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Psi}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HP((Z_{g})^{n}\times X^{g})

which is natural in
X
.

(2)

If
G
acts on
X
with finite stabilizers, then there is a weak equivalence
of spectra

\Phi_{\mathcal{X}}\colon HP({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{{\rm holim}}_{n}HP(G^{n}\times I_{X})

which is natural in
X
.

Proof.

One knows by
[
32
, Exm. 5.2.7]
that
HP
is nil-invariant and
satisfies cdh descent for stacks. We also note that associated to the
abstract blow-up square (
8.21
) (on the left),
one has an abstract blow-up square

(15.23)

\textstyle{G^{n}\times W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G^{n}\times X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G^{n}\times Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G^{n}\times X}

for each
n\geq 0
which is compatible with the face and degeneracy maps of
simplicial schemes
\{G^{n}\times Y\}_{n\geq 0}
, where
Y\in\{X,Z,X^{\prime},W\}
.
Using these observations, the first part of the theorem is proven by repeating
the arguments for the
KH
-theory in Theorem
8.12

verbatim (and using Corollary
14.7
) while the
second part follows in a similar way. We omit the
details.
∎

for hosting him for a part of the work on this paper.
